`plugins/surveillance/index.js` importing `drugForItem` from `server/engine/drugs.js` while the
export sat uncommitted in the working tree. The local suite passed 8988/8988 against a tree that had
it; CI checks out the commit and could not load the plugin. Surveillance owns `plant`, `retrieve`,
`sweep`, `hijack`, `wanted`, `bribe`, `submit` and `scrub`, so the break surfaced as help-verb and
object-gated-verb failures that named nothing to do with drugs, and every content deploy stopped.
**Staging by file is not enough when one change spans two files** — that is the trap this catches.

⚠ **Do not "simplify" the scanner to a regex over raw source.** It blanks comments, template literals
and regex literals first, and a first cut without that reported eleven findings of which ten were its
own fault: a multi-declarator `export const A = 0.45, B = 0.5;` (only the first name seen), a
**comment inside an `export { … }` block** naming the symbol, a nested object literal in an
interpolation (`?? {}`) unbalancing a brace counter, and — the one that proves the point — a regex
holding a double quote inside an interpolation (`${h.replace(/"/g, '&quot;')}`, which every
HTML-building client panel does), which desynchronised the rest of the file. There is ONE scanner and
an interpolation is scanned as ordinary code; two scanners means the weaker one decides what the
stronger one sees. Only NAMED imports are checked — proving a module has no default export means
being sure it is ESM, and being wrong on a push gate is worse than the miss. Escape hatch is
`// imports-ok: <reason>`, reason required. ~2 s over ~1,300 files, no browser, DB or network.

## VINE Graph Workflow

Creating or updating an NPC behaviour graph, dialogue tree, or enemy behaviour graph is covered by
the `mud-designer` skill (push mechanics, auth, the dialogue flat-params gotcha); schemas live in
[docs/vine.md](docs/vine.md).

⚠ **AND THE CAMERA CAN STAND SOMEWHERE NOW, WHICH IS ONE CAMERA UNDER THREE CONSTRAINTS AND NOT A SECOND ONE.** `freecam.js` grew a `stand` mode: feet on something, an eye height that is HANDED IN, and a leash. The mouse, the rim push, the lens, the speed ladder, the idle fade and the key routing are all the ones that always shipped, and what changes is three things about where the eye may be. **Forward drops the pitch term, and that is the whole of "FPS controls"** — flying, W carries the lens and looking up takes off; standing, you look up at the sky and walk forward. **Up-down is inert by ARITHMETIC rather than by a guard**: every control spends its motion through one `place` chokepoint and the chokepoint overwrites z, so R/F and the two mouse buttons cost a clamp and the next control anybody adds inherits the rule. ⚠ **The orbit is refused at the BUTTON as well as at `orbit()`** — the pointer binder reads `cam.orbiting` to decide whether a drag aims or swings, so a middle button that reached the set would send every delta to an orbit that says no and the mouse would stop LOOKING while it was held. ⚠ **And the leash ANCHOR follows a rebase**, or a re-centre is spent dragging the shot to the edge of a tether that is now somewhere else. The gate is the standing block in `scripts/shapes/freecam.mjs`, whose control is a FLYING camera doing the opposite in the same breath.

⚠ **A VANTAGE IS THAT CAMERA BOLTED DOWN, AND IT LIVES IN `plugins/freelook` BECAUSE THERE IS ONE CAMERA-OVER-A-TILE ON THE WIRE.** Furniture carrying `flags.telescope` is a place you stand: `telescope` sends the same `freelook_open` the staff camera sends, with a `stand` block on it. One viewer set, one 15s sky push, one `freelook close` — a second plugin sending that message would be a second owner of a pane whose close reached only one of them. ⚠ **It is deliberately NOT behind the staff gate**: `freelook` is staff-only because it looks at ground you have not walked to, and a telescope is a fitting in a room you already got into. ⚠ **A vantage names a MOUNT rather than an eye height** whenever it stands on something the server does not know the shape of — the Echelon's sun deck is a height that is a property of her MODEL and a position that is a function of the SWELL — so `mount: 'yacht_scope'` is answered by `yachtScopeMount()`, the same function that decides where the instrument is DRAWN, and it is asked every frame so the shot rides the swell with the deck. ⚠ **The same clock, though**: `paintWindshield` runs on `performance.now()` and so does the rAF timestamp the view passes in; a mount sampled off `Date.now()` leaves the camera standing where the deck was at an unrelated instant. ⚠ **And `self` is cleared on the centre cell** (`yachtHelmWindow`'s own line) — that flag stops a cab being drawn inside its own building, which is exactly wrong for a camera standing ON something. ⚠ **AND A VANTAGE BUYS ITSELF MORE OF THE CITY** (`STAND_TUNE`), because every LOD number in this renderer is set for a camera that is MOVING and this is the one seat that cannot: `lodNear`, `decoFar`, `glowFar` and `shadowFar` go up through the per-seat `tune` seam rather than through `RENDER_TUNE`, which is the player's own sliders and would follow them into the cab they get into next. ⚠ **`texRes` is deliberately NOT in it though it is tunable** — it is the one detail knob that can make the picture WORSE rather than slower, since at 2 every surface wants a 2048x4096 atlas page against a WebGL2 floor guarantee of 2048 square, and `buildAtlas` answers a page it cannot fit by dropping the whole city to flat palette colours. ⚠ **`detailNear` is not in `VIEW_TUNABLE` at all** and so cannot be reached this way, which is the right answer: its own gate records 3 to 6 taking a dense night block from 21.0 ms to 24.3.

⚠ **THE ECHELON WAS FLAT, AND THAT IS ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN AN OPINION.** Her deckhouse ran 0.085 to 0.163 — three tiers totalling 0.078 of local height on a hull 2.08 long, a **27:1** superstructure where a 60-metre yacht with four decks is nearer 5:1, while her interior content carries a bridge, a foyer, a suite, a broadcast studio, a stern deck, a helipad, a landing and a sun deck. `YACHT_TIERS` is the table now (a deck is 0.046 of local z, derived rather than picked) and she tops out at 0.222 for 5.5:1. ⚠ **`YACHT_DECKZ` DOES NOT MOVE** — it is the helipad floor and `yachtPadZ` plus cockpit.js's deck-landing capture key off it, so she grows UPWARD FROM THE PAD and every helicopter still lands where it always did. ⚠ **The OVERHANG is what makes a level readable**: the old shape stepped back in plan and stacked roof straight onto floor, so the flanks ran together into one dark wedge — a deck plate standing proud of the house it carries gives a bright top, a dark fascia and a shadow, at every level, from any angle. ⚠ **A SPECULAR LOBE ON A FLAT FACE IS NOT A HIGHLIGHT**, the GL material pass's own finding one layer down: `n·h` is constant over a plane, so with the sun high and an eye above her **every horizontal surface went to (175,182,192) at once** — invisible at a fly-past, and the whole picture from a camera standing on her. `matte` keeps a tenth of it and the flanks, transom, glass and domes are untouched. ⚠ **Nothing on a deck may overlap anything else in the horizontal**: this renderer sorts far→near and fills, so a SUNKEN pool is painted over by the plate it is sunk into — the first cut had a jacuzzi invisible from every angle — and both the spa and the companionway are RAISED instead, the spa's surround a frame of four quads round a hole rather than a plate with water laid on it. ⚠ **And the near clip was hiding the deck you stand on**: `drawYacht`'s canvas near plane was 0.12 tiles, nearly a fifth of her beam, so the terrace under a vantage was entirely inside it; it is 0.065 now and the wall is `cam.proj`'s own `Math.max(0.06, …)`. ⚠ **That is a CANVAS number only** — the collecting pass returns before it, which is why GLASS 2 draws the deck correctly whatever it is and why `yacht.mjs`'s camera-independence check is unaffected. ⚠ **The sun deck is drawn at EVERY distance and deliberately not as a near-LOD**, because that gate is right to forbid geometry that is a function of the camera; what IS near-only is the stroke work (planking, the grab rail, the close-spaced stanchions), which is a fitting and is re-collected every frame anyway.
⚠ **GLASS 2 IS THE DEFAULT RENDERER (2026-09-09).** `RENDER_TUNE.gl = 0` (the **GLASS 2 (WebGL)**
slider, or `__wsTune.gl = 0`) puts the city back on the CPU. It was flipped on the evidence: the camera
agrees with `cam.proj` to 5.7e-14 px across five device-pixel ratios (`gl:parity`, 3,931 projections),
the mesh agrees with the shape every building COLLIDES as at all four facings (`gl:mesh`, 10,853 faces),
a real Coldwater frame measured **18.3 ms average / 31 ms worst** with the pass live, and the
ground-to-air crossfade, the Curtain and the signage all sit on the depth buffer rather than on a probe.
⚠ **It still fails safe**, which is what makes the default defensible: no WebGL2, a lost context, a
shader that will not compile, a texture page the device cannot hold, a pass that returns nothing and a
pass that throws each hand the world back to 2-D and put the flag to 0. ⚠ **And every number is from ONE
machine with a discrete NVIDIA card** — "falls back correctly" is not "is fast on an integrated GPU".

⚠ **THE FOUR SEE-THROUGH BUGS THE FLIP DEPENDED ON, AND WHY THEY ARE ONE BUG.** A painter's queue hides
things by painting over them. With the mass on the GPU it is composited BEFORE the 2-D pass, so nothing
can paint over a 2-D adornment any more and every surface that relied on that draws through the city.
`decoHidden` cannot stand in: it answers whether a WHOLE surface is behind something, and a sign half
behind a tower is both the common case and the one it deliberately answers "draw" to. The fix is the same
each time — put the surface on the depth buffer, TESTED and writing no depth of its own. The **lights**
went first (`gl/sprites.js`), then the **Curtain** (`gl/curtain.js` — a wall taller than the city, whose
gradient, scan bands, rain and crown are all functions of position along and down it, so the port is
faithful rather than an impression), then the **marquees** (`gl/decals.js` — textured quads batched by
texture, the artwork still painted by the same 2-D code into a baked canvas so there is only ever one
idea of what a sign looks like). ⚠ **A wall-mounted sign must stand PROUD of its wall**: `marqueeBand`
mounts at `half * 0.94`, six per cent INSIDE the facade, which the 2-D renderer survived by sorting it
forward and a depth buffer does not — it compares, the wall wins every pixel, and the sign vanishes.
Same trap `FACE_EPS` exists for. ⚠ **And a surface queued through `emitFace` is collected at FLUSH time,
after the GL composite has already run** — the Curtain filled its sink from inside the queued closure and
reached the GPU never, drawing nothing and reporting nothing.

⚠ **THE MATRIX IS BUILT IN THE CAMERA'S UNITS, NOT THE CANVAS'S.** `makeCam` works in CSS pixels; the GL
canvas is a backing store in DEVICE pixels. `projMatrix`'s x row is `2·FL / cam.W` — both terms off the
camera, right by luck — while the y row is `2·depth / H` and `1 − 2·horizonY / H`, which took the canvas's
height and put the whole vertical axis out by 1/dpr with the horizon displaced. On a 459×467 pane at dpr
1.2 that lifted every building **38 px up a 467 px frame**, base and roof by different amounts, so the city
was raised off the ground AND squashed. ⚠ **It is exactly zero at dpr 1**, which is every synthetic canvas
in every headless test and every Modelshop scene — nine reproduction attempts came back clean while a
player saw it in every frame. `gl:parity` now sweeps five ratios through a device-pixel viewport.

⚠ **AND THE TWO RENDERERS MUST AGREE ABOUT WHICH TILES ARE WHOSE.** GL takes a tile on
`massTile(c) && modelFor(c)`; the 2-D pass suppressed its walls on `GL_CELLS && m`, a SECOND expression
that had to agree and did not. They differ on every tile `massTile` rejects while `modelFor` still answers
one — a statue, a gate, a sign, pylons, a strip, a bay, the yacht, a no-fly tile, a curtain tile — so those
buildings had their mass suppressed here and were never uploaded over there: the walls vanished and the
lights and signs hung in the air. It reads membership of the set the collection FILLS now, which cannot
drift. ⚠ No synthetic scene caught it, because every test city is plain `bt` tiles and not one carries a
mark. ⚠ **`worldBlend` is the same shape of miss**: the 2-D pass fades every world object by it and culls
below 0.02, which is how the Mode-7 city gives way to the flat airport scene on the deck — GL collected the
whole window without asking, and drew the real city over the airport on every landing (17.0% of the frame
at `worldBlend: 0`, 12.2% mid-crossfade; 0.00% and 2.78% now). `RENDER_TUNE.gl = 1` (the **GLASS 2 (WebGL)** slider in the flight-sim settings, or `__wsTune.gl = 1`) draws the city's MASS in WebGL2 on a canvas under the 2-D one and runs the model arms with `MASS_OFF`, so every light, sign, adornment, ground pass and HUD still paints on top exactly as before. **That seam is not new** — `MASS_OFF` is how distance LOD has always drawn a far building's neon without its walls. ⚠ **Off is the absence of a code path**: at 0 no canvas is created and no context is asked for, and `framecost` is unchanged to the call. ⚠ **windshield.js imports none of it** — the cold open loads that file and must never touch a GPU, and the smoke suite loads it against a DOM stub with no WebGL — so the pass is INSTALLED from outside (`installGL()` in cockpit.js, which main.js imports at boot), and a throw inside it sets the flag back to 0 and finishes the frame in 2-D. Measured end to end: **5.7 ms → 2.4 ms** on a truck's 33-tile window and **21.8 → 13.6 ms** on an aircraft's 73-tile one, with ONE buffer rebuild over 48 steady frames. ⚠ **The buffer is the whole argument and three separate mistakes rebuilt it every frame** — an order-sensitive key, a mesh built at the camera-relative position rather than the map-window tile, and a set taken from whatever survived the 2-D culls. Each drew a perfect picture; only `glLastFrame().builds` tells them apart from a buffer uploaded once. ⚠ **A rebuild also has to be CHEAP**, because the window recentres as you drive and the resolution dial resizes the canvas under it: 11.1 ms at an aircraft's window until the vertex data was filled into a `Float32Array` directly and a tile was handed over as its shared face list plus an offset rather than as a copy — 0 ms now. ⚠ **And two ways it drew a city nobody could see**: a canvas UNDER the 2-D one is covered by an opaque sky and ground (and on top it would cover every light) — so the GL canvas never joins the document and `drawWorldObjects` BLITS it where the mass used to be queued, riding the current transform so bank and shudder come free — and `MASS_OFF` set globally deleted every building type with no model of its own (`luxtower`), which has no mesh on the GPU to replace it. It is also a per-seat key (`VIEW_TUNABLE`), so a view can opt in with `tune: { gl: 1 }`. **Stage two moved the adornment SURFACES too** — every panel, band, louvre, sill, coping, jamb and soffit goes through `emitFlat`, so that one function records into `MESH_SINK` and answers to `FLAT_OFF` (⚠ suppressing exactly what the mesh took: a gradient or stroke-only quad is not in the mesh, and a blunt flag would delete it from the world rather than move it). The LIGHT — neon, glow, bloom, painted signage — stays on the 2-D canvas, which is why the arms still run. ⚠ **The mesh captures at `ADORN_NEAR`**, the tier that only exists because the 2-D renderer cannot afford it at range: a mesh drawn through a depth buffer can, so the truck driver's recessed doorways, sills and mullions are now simply always there — 1,191 → 2,133 faces at the same 0.13 ms, and the frame 5.1 → 2.0 ms. ⚠ **And the capture offset has to follow the ENTRANCE**: the arm is run displaced so its front faces the stub camera (sixty arms gate on `frontVis`), and that displacement was a fixed step along y — right for a north-facing building and wrong for the other three, so a shop lost its whole near tier on three facings out of four with nothing to say so, because the shape capture only ever runs at [0,1]. `gl:mesh` now captures all four facings and demands identical face counts.

⚠ **AND THE QUALITY PASS IS SCORED, NOT TASTED.** `npm run models:quality` grades every model out of four on things the renderer already produces — a roof face, a light at night, more than one wall palette, any trim — because a pass over 173 buildings cannot be run on judgement one building at a time. At the start: **8 of 173 had any trim at all**, and 5 scored all four; it is 143 and 107 now. ⚠ **The light metric was wrong first, and wrong in the direction that invents work** — it counted gradients and blurs on the stub ctx, which cannot see a glow at all (`glowPool` blits a bitmap `glowSprite` built ONCE on its own canvas, cached per colour), so a building lit only by glows and beacons measured as dark and the board reported **109 of 173 emitting nothing after dark when the real number is 14**, most of them things that should be dark. `shapeAdornCost` runs with the sprite sink installed now and counts what lands in it. It is a report and not a gate on purpose (`--fail-under N` is there for the day the pass is done): a gate would fail the moment somebody added a model and keep failing until they finished it. **Trim is keyed on the ARM, not the record** — a `detail` list is geometry hung on surfaces, and what decides where those are is the arm, so one list serves every record drawn by it, with `m.detail` still winning. That needed the detail layer OUT of `drawAuthoredModel`: a `detail` list is a property of a model RECORD, and the 172 hand-written arms have records. ⚠ **AND MOST TRIM IS DERIVED, NOT AUTHORED.** 156 models had none and they sit under **139 distinct arms** — four models under the biggest — so hand-authoring is 139 lists each written against one arm's own heights, where a number read wrong is a coping band floating over its building. `derivedTrim` reads `shapeForModel` instead, which is what collision, shadows, occluder hulls and the cold open already read, so a band taken off it cannot float whatever arm drew the building: trim went 17 → 143 models for +0.36% frame cost. ⚠ It resolves to ABSOLUTE numbers because `draw3DBoxAt` clamps a half-width to 0.44 and a clamp needs a resolved number. ⚠ **And it sorts candidates on HEIGHT** — the first cut took `lodOrder().byIndex.slice(0, 2)` believing it was ranked, and `byIndex` is re-sorted into SOURCE ORDER before it is returned (the bulk ranking survives only as each entry's `at`), so it took the first two segments an arm happens to draw — usually the plinth and the ground box. Every building in the city got a coping band round its ANKLES, which reads exactly as it was reported: the buildings look like they are coming up out of the ground. ⚠ **And the guards must come before the list is built** — the derived list asks `shapeForModel`, which CAPTURES by running the same arm again, so building it before the `SHAPE_SINK` test makes a capture re-enter itself: it terminates and draws the right picture, so it showed up as 8,435 mesh faces becoming 101,400 rather than as a hang. ⚠ **Author a wall-mounted part at the FOOTPRINT (`[1,0,0]`)**, never at the box's own half-width — `draw3DBoxAt` clamps a half-width to 0.44, so a part placed at `1.14` floats off the side of the building. ⚠ **And a part stands physically off its face** (`FACE_EPS`): `lift` moves a quad in the painter's ORDER and is nothing at all to a depth buffer, where a panel in the plane of its wall z-fights. ⚠ **The framerate contract decides where trim draws**: it is the cheapest canvas work there is and there is a lot of it (four arms measured +3.3% calls, all of it flat fills, `grad` and `blur` unchanged to the call), so **in 2-D it draws at `ADORN_NEAR` only** — bounded by how many buildings are near you rather than by how many are authored — and **in the mesh it is not gated at all**, because a depth buffer draws it for nothing. Trim is a GLASS 2 feature the 2-D renderer also shows you when you stand beside it. ⚠ **Two GL bugs it turned up**: the pass was lighting the city from a fixed north-west fill (`glLightState` has no sun to ask — it takes `LIGHT_STATE` now, the light the arms are shading against this frame), and it was magnifying wall textures with LINEAR where GLASS smooths only on MINIFY, which turned every near facade into a grey wash and looked like the lighting. ⚠ **What stands on the ground**: a painter's queue hides things by painting over them, so with the mass on the GPU the 2-D queue has nothing to sort scatter, trees, lamps or people against — `groundHidden` probes the occluder field the frame already built. ⚠ **The hole this used to claim does not exist.** It said a wood behind one-storey warehouses still drew through them and blamed `OCC_SHRINK`; both halves were written from a screenshot rather than a measurement, and both are wrong. Zeroing the shrink changes nothing (442 probes, 0 hits either way) because at that range the trees stand on ground projecting ABOVE the sheds' rooflines and were never occluded; held against a building's own silhouette with the clock frozen, a wood behind it accounts for NOTHING — 1,030 badly-differing pixels with the trees, 1,187 without. A rendering difference you can see in a screenshot is a hypothesis, and this repo has harnesses for turning those into numbers.

⚠ **AND THE GROUND IS A SHADER, ON BY DEFAULT (`RENDER_TUNE.glFloor`, 0 puts the raster back).** `drawMode7Floor` was the last
large thing in GLASS 1 with no GL path at all, and it is the one that decides whether GLASS 1 can
be retired: switch the 2-D renderer off and there is simply no ground. It was also the biggest
fixed cost in the frame — a per-pixel software raster that costs the same over an empty desert as
over a city, and the whole reason `PERF_DS` exists. ⚠ **AND IT WAS ALREADY A FRAGMENT SHADER,**
written in the only language available: the texel loop inverts the projection to a world point,
samples a per-tile LUT and layers material on it. `client/game/js/panels/gl/floor.js` is that same
function where it belongs — one screen-filling triangle, the LUT as two RGBA planes cached on the
LUT object’s own identity, and `gl_FragDepth` written from the recovered forward distance, because
a screen-filling triangle has ONE depth and the ground has a thousand. Measured with `__glFloor()`
in the Modelshop over sixteen scenes: **worst 2.30% of pixels differing, 0.10–0.72% mean colour**,
and `__glFloorCost()` on a cab frame **32.9 → 1.3 ms** in a city, **65.4 → 0.6** over open sea.
⚠ **The middle column of that table is the finding**: GLASS 2 with the 2-D floor still under it
costs what GLASS 1 does on three of four scenes. Moving the mass, trim, lights and signage bought
almost nothing until the floor followed, because the raster WAS the frame and everything else was
hiding behind it.

⚠ **THREE THINGS THE FLOOR COST TO GET RIGHT, EACH OF WHICH DREW A PLAUSIBLE PICTURE.** The pass
ran BEFORE `g.view.draw()`, which opens with `gl.clear(COLOR | DEPTH)` — so the floor was drawn and
then wiped, every frame, and what looked like a floor was the 2-D backstop wash through a cleared
canvas; all five shader debug modes returning identical pixels is what exposed it. Then **the
tarmac stayed on the canvas while the paint on it moved**: `stripeA` records lane markings into
`GROUND_MESH` but the tile fill under them is a plain `ctx.fill`, and the GL canvas is blitted over
everything the 2-D pass has drawn so far — transparent over the ground until the floor became
opaque, at which point the road vanished and its markings hung in the grass. So `GROUND_FULL` (the
GPU owns the WHOLE ground, and only when it owns the floor) takes the tile fill at `SURF_EPS` and
the kerbs as quads whose half-width is solved for the tile’s distance, because a 1.5 px stroke is a
SCREEN width and a quad is a WORLD one. ⚠ **Four surfaces share the ground plane and they need an
order rather than a coin toss**: floor 0 < `SURF_EPS` < `ROAD_EPS` < `SHADOW_EPS` — and lifting in z
is what orders them, since `f` is ground distance and carries no height term at all, so a higher
quad simply covers a screen row whose floor is further away. And **a shader that does not compile
looks exactly like a shader that draws badly**: the pass catches the throw, sets the flag back to 0
and finishes in 2-D, which is right for a machine with no WebGL2 and wrong as a way to find out
two uniforms were dropped in an edit. `npm run gl:glsl` ([scripts/shapes/glsl-smoke.mjs](scripts/shapes/glsl-smoke.mjs))

⚠ **AND THE FLOOR DEFAULT RESTS ON ONE TERM IN ONE CONDITION.** `drawMode7Floor` hands its LUT and
its camera over and RETURNS, drawing nothing itself — so it has to test `TUNE.gl` as well as
`TUNE.glFloor` before it does. Every way GLASS 2 can fail to draw the replacement ends at
`RENDER_TUNE.gl = 0`, and this function runs BEFORE the pass each frame; without that term the early
return keeps firing for ever on a machine that cannot draw it. Not a degraded floor — **NO floor,
permanently**, on exactly the hardware with no way to tell you why, and quiet, because the backstop
sky-to-ground gradient underneath is smooth, the right colour, and looks like ground until you put
it beside the real thing. Measured at the flip: **69% of the ground differed from the software
raster without the term, 0% with it**. `npm run gl:floorfallback`
([scripts/shapes/floorfallback.mjs](scripts/shapes/floorfallback.mjs)) pins it headlessly against a
hook that answers nothing, one that throws, and no hook at all. ⚠ Its signal is `putImageData` on
the Mode-7 scratch buffer, because the floor is one `drawImage` onto the frame whichever way it is
drawn — and ⚠ the counter has to go through a PROXY, since the DOM stub’s context is itself a proxy
that synthesises methods on `get` and silently discards a wrapper written onto it, which reads
exactly like the floor never rastering.
is the gate for that class: every `uXxx` a shader uses must be declared in that shader, and every
name the JS asks the linker for by string must exist — a missing uniform location is `null`,
`gl.uniform1f(null, x)` is a legal no-op, and the shader reads zero for ever.

⚠ **AND THE CLIP PLANE IS FITTED TO THE SEAT (`RENDER_TUNE.nearFit`, the **Fit near plane to seat**
slider; 0 is the fixed 0.06 tiles GLASS 2 shipped with).** Reported as grass showing under the road
when you are flat on the ground on it, and the answer everybody reaches for — raise the eye — is the
one the report rules out. A near plane is a distance in WORLD TILES and an eye height is not a
constant, and when the two are the same size the plane eats the bottom of the frame: the nearest
ground a frame shows is `EH · depth / (H − horizonY)`, which is about four tiles for an aeroplane at
eye 0.24 and **0.049** for a camera pressed down onto the road at the 0.05 `makeCam` floors to. That
is INSIDE 0.06, so the last tenth of the frame is ground no geometry can reach — no road quad, no
kerb, no lane marking — and the only thing left that can draw there is the floor, which paints the
TERRAIN. `nearFor` in gl/camera.js solves the plane from the camera instead. ⚠ **IT ONLY EVER COMES
IN**: `NEAR` is the ceiling, so the cockpit (wants 0.228), the cab (0.139) and every chase seat come
back **exactly** 0.06 and keep the depth budget that came with it — `Object.is`, asserted per seat,
because the claim is that their matrices are bit-identical rather than close. Only the street seat
(0.0455) and a free camera on the deck (0.0379) move, which is 1.3–1.6× coarser depth at the one
seat that would otherwise not draw its road at all, and the ground layer's own z-fight defence is
`polygonOffset` in DEPTH-BUFFER units and so is unaffected by it. ⚠ **AND IT IS GLASS 2 ONLY**: on
the 2-D painter the same 0.06 is a CLAMP inside `proj` rather than a clip plane, spelled as a literal
at twenty call sites that each test `p.f <= 0.06` to mean "behind the eye", so moving it there is a
different and much larger change — `RENDER_TUNE.gl = 0` still shows the band. ⚠ **ONE PLANE, SPENT
EVERYWHERE, AND THREE PLACES HELD A SECOND COPY.** The mass, the ground, the floor, the sprites and
the strokes share one depth buffer, and two of them built from two different near planes do not
disagree visibly — they disagree by a hair that reads as z-fighting somebody then goes looking for in
the eps ladder. `gl/floor.js` carried a literal `const near = 0.06, far = 400.0` (the exact second
copy the ⚠ on `NEAR`/`FAR` in camera.js warns about), and `sprites.js` and `strokes.js` computed the
z row they push as `uAB` as MODULE CONSTANTS, which is right while the plane is one and a silent
half-tile depth error the moment a seat fits its own. All three take `zRow(cam.near)` now, and
`cam.near` is stamped once per frame in world.js because every pass is handed the same `cam` — the
floor is the exception and the one place it can go missing, since it is driven entirely by
FLOOR_STATE. ⚠ **AND IT IS SOLVED UNDER PITCH RATHER THAN ASSUMED LEVEL**, because a tilted camera is
exactly the one that looks steeply enough down for this to matter; the solve agrees with the shipping
matrix to five decimals at a 14° tilt. `npm run gl:nearfit`
([scripts/shapes/nearfit.mjs](scripts/shapes/nearfit.mjs), in `shapes:smoke` AND in the
`pretest:regress` chain — two lists, per the warning above) asks the reach question through the
SHIPPING matrix: search for the ground distance that lands on the last row, and compare its clip `w`
with the plane. ⚠ **It cannot be asked through `cam.proj`, which clamps `f` at 0.06** — below that the
row saturates and the search answers 0.06 for every seat, which is the number under test. Its
mutation control is three named seats that must be CLIPPED with the plane put back, because a fitted
plane on its own proves nothing: `NEAR_FIT`'s cushion moves it a little on seats that were already
reaching. ⚠ **AND A WHOLE-FRAME HASH IS NOT THE INSTRUMENT FOR "NOTHING MOVED"** — two renders of the
same cab frame hash differently, because the clouds drift, the birds fly and the lights fade, so an
A/B taken that way reports a change on a seat whose matrix is provably identical. The arithmetic is
the proof; the pixels are the picture.

⚠ **AND `npm run gl:opts` GUARDS THE HOP BEFORE THAT ONE.** `installGL()` hands the world pass an
object literal that is an **allowlist, not a spread**, so an option added at the windshield end and
not added there is dropped one hop short of the shader that reads it — and the result is
indistinguishable from a feature that does nothing: the tune key exists, the slider moves, the
uniform is declared, the bench reports 0.0% at every strength. Contact occlusion shipped that way
for an afternoon; the baked per-vertex AO term shipped the same way **immediately afterwards**,
having been wired at both ends, gated, measured and swept first. Three occurrences is where a
comment stops being enough: [scripts/shapes/glopts.mjs](scripts/shapes/glopts.mjs) brace-matches
that literal and demands every `opts.X` world.js reads be a key it passes. ⚠ An exception is a
REASON, never a name. ⚠ And it blanks comments first with `imports:smoke`'s own scanner (now
[scripts/lib/blank-scanner.mjs](scripts/lib/blank-scanner.mjs), one of them on purpose) — this file
is mostly prose and prose is full of `word:`, so a regex over raw source collected 'afternoon' and
'produces' as option names.

⚠ **AND EVERY PER-FRAME LAYER STREAMS ITS VERTICES THROUGH ONE HELPER NOW ([gl/stream.js](client/game/js/panels/gl/stream.js)).**
Eight layers — the mass solids, the ground, strokes, sprites, decals, billboards, the clouds and the
Curtain — each fill a growing `Float32Array` and hand it over once a frame, and all eight did it the
same way: `gl.bufferData(gl.ARRAY_BUFFER, data.subarray(0, n * STRIDE), gl.DYNAMIC_DRAW)` followed by
their whole `enableVertexAttribArray` + `vertexAttribPointer` block. Three things were wrong with
that and none of them shows up in a picture. ⚠ **`subarray` MINTS A TYPED-ARRAY VIEW**, per layer per
frame, at the one point in the frame where a collection pause is most visible — and WebGL2’s
`bufferSubData` takes a source offset and a length, so the view never needed to exist. ⚠ **A
`bufferData` CARRYING A SIZE IS A REALLOCATION REQUEST**, and the vertex count moves every frame (a
building comes into range, a light fades up), so the size handed over is almost never last frame’s
and the driver was asked for fresh storage each time; capacity is a high-water mark grown by
**doubling** now, so a layer settles within a few frames of reaching its working size and then never
asks again. ⚠ **AND THE ATTRIBUTE POINTERS ARE WHAT A VAO IS FOR** — they are recorded INTO the VAO
against the buffer bound at the time, and `bufferData` replaces what is INSIDE a buffer and never the
buffer’s name, so re-binding after every upload is a habit from before vertex array objects.
Measured in the Modelshop with `bufferData`, `bufferSubData` and `vertexAttribPointer` counted on the
prototype, over a 10-frame and a 60-frame run of the same five seats: **`bufferData` 15 and 15,
`vertexAttribPointer` 165 and 165** — flat, so entirely setup — against `bufferSubData` 734 and 3,134,
which is the 9.6 uploads a frame that SHOULD scale. ⚠ **THERE IS DELIBERATELY NO ORPHANING**: the
idiomatic `bufferData(size)` before every `bufferSubData` dodges a write-after-read stall and is
exactly the per-frame reallocation this removes, so if one is ever MEASURED on real hardware it goes
back in the helper once rather than in eight places. ⚠ **AND NO HARNESS HERE REACHES A GL DRAW CALL**
— every one installs a hook that answers null — so a buffer-management regression is invisible to all
of them at once and looks exactly like code that works. `npm run gl:stream`
([scripts/shapes/glstream.mjs](scripts/shapes/glstream.mjs), in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in
`shapes:smoke`, per the two-lists warning above) drives the helper against a RECORDING context, where
the question is not what was drawn but what the driver was asked for, and it sweeps the DIRECTORY
rather than a list of names — a new layer written the old way is the regression it exists to catch.
Mutation-tested 8 of 8. ⚠ Its `bufferSubData` check is on OBJECT IDENTITY, because “a Float32Array
arrived” is also true of a fresh view. The uniform matrices went the same way: `mat4f` in camera.js
holds one scratch `Float32Array(16)` for the eight layers that were each writing
`new Float32Array(viewProjMatrix(…))` per draw — ⚠ **never retain what it hands back**, it is the same
object every time, and anything that needs to keep a matrix takes the plain Array from
`viewProjMatrix` itself.

⚠ **AND THE GROUND BUFFER IS NOT WORTH CACHING, WHICH IS A MEASUREMENT AND NOT A GUESS.**
`gl/ground.js` rebuilds its entire vertex buffer every frame with no cache key anywhere in the file,
and `GROUND_MESH` is refilled from scratch every frame in map-window tiles — which reads as the mass
buffer’s own argument waiting to be applied a second time. It is not, for two reasons that are each
worth knowing on their own. **The quads are NOT camera-independent**: a road quad’s alpha is
`sq.a * ctx.globalAlpha` and that `globalAlpha` is the far fade, while a kerb’s half-width is solved
for the tile’s DISTANCE because a 1.5 px stroke is a screen width and a quad is a world one — so a
window-keyed cache would need the fade moved into the shader and the kerb expanded in a vertex shader
the way `strokes.js` already expands a wire, both of them real changes with a visible surface. And
**the phase costs nothing**: `__glPhases()` on a 1280×720 aerial seat reads `ground` at **0.00 ms**
with the GL floor on, against 0.86 ms for the 2-D path it replaced. ⚠ **A COLD FIRST RUN OF THAT
HARNESS INVENTS TWO FINDINGS**, and both are the kind somebody would act on: it reported
`world:build` at 12.2 ms (GL *more expensive* than 2-D, which is the opposite of the whole design)
and `world:fauna` at 3.40 ms against 0.44 — and on the second and third runs those are **6.0–6.9 ms
against 9.5** and **0.03 ms**. Take three. What the settled table says is that the CPU cost left in a
GL frame is `world:build` ~6.5 ms and `world:arms` ~3.8 ms — running the model arms and collecting
the adornments, every frame, for surfaces whose world-space geometry does not change — with
`world:gl` (the actual submission) at 2.4 ms and `world:occlude` at 0.7 ms. That is where the next
optimisation is, and it is not the ground.

⚠ **AND `__glFidelity()`’s 1.0% IS STALE — IT READS 4.7% AND HAS NOT REGRESSED.** Measured against a
detached worktree at `6468131a6` serving its own Modelshop on another port (the only honest way to
A/B here — never by swapping live files), HEAD reads **4.7%** and the working tree **4.8%**, well
inside the harness’s own run-to-run spread: `office/13` moved 7.0 → 4.4 and `clinic/13` 8.9 → 8.5
between two runs of the SAME build. The drift is the GL-only shading that has landed since the number
was written — the material BRDF, the recovered relief, SSAO, the HDR chain, contact occlusion, the
baked AO term — every one of which is a deliberate divergence from the painter. ⚠ **So a fidelity
reading is only ever a comparison against another reading taken the same week**, and a number in a
doc header is not a baseline.

⚠ **AND THE FACADE GLOWS IN BANDS RATHER THAN IN GRIDS (`RENDER_TUNE.glBand`, 0 puts the punched
grid and the two-pane shopfront back).** Voltage's own authoring note had prescribed it for months —
*"ONE continuous glazed band, divided by mullions rather than punched into separate windows"* — and
the derived kit built the opposite on every building in the city. ⚠ **It is CHEAPER, which is the
part nobody expects**: a ribbon with five mullions is eleven faces where the five punched bays it
replaces are forty, so window-bay parts across the registry fell **1,827 → 982** and the whole
registry mesh went **39,754 → 36,833 faces** — *below* where the signage pass started — while the lit
area on every shopfront facade went up several times over. ⚠ **The lit roll moves from the pane to
the FLOOR**, which is a change of look and not a loss of one: a grid reads as inhabited because some
panes are dark, a ribbon because some floors are, and `litN` still counts rooms so the facade wash
is unchanged. ⚠ **Rich list only, and it has to be** — `bars` is mesh-only, so on the 2-D painter a
ribbon would be one undivided glowing slab with no mullions in it at all. ⚠ **And the door stands
PROUD of the glass rather than being a hole in it**: a dark bay at the same plane is a coin toss on
the depth buffer, and the band's glazing sits at a higher lift than the door's surround, so the
glass wins and the doorway ends up behind its own shopfront. ⚠ **`glBand` and `richKit` both need a
page reload** — the mesh memo and the GL vertex buffer key on object IDENTITY and `meshParams`
carries neither flag, so an A/B is two page loads and never two panels in one pass; a comparison
shot taken the other way is two renders of the same mesh with two different labels on it.

⚠ **AND WORLD TEXT IS ON THE DEPTH BUFFER TOO (`RENDER_TUNE.glSign`, 0 puts the strips back).** A name
across a parapet, a stencilled bay number, a price board painted on a wall: the last thing GLASS 1
still drew in the world, at **eight affine strips a sign** because a 2-D canvas cannot map a texture
through a perspective divide and has to fake it by subdivision. On the GPU it is one quad through the
same `gl/decals.js` the marquees use, so the artwork is still baked by the same 2-D code and there is
only ever one idea of what a sign looks like. On a 35%-built cab frame: **481 `drawImage` calls → 1**
(the GL blit), 480 `transform` → 0, 488 `save` → 8, and 50,691 canvas calls → 48,771. ⚠ **No caller
grew a world-space twin of its quad** — `cam.unproj` is the full inverse of `proj` and every projected
point already carries its own `f`; it answers **null under pitch** rather than guessing, and the one
site whose probe points differ from its drawn quad was deliberately left on the 2-D path.

⚠ **AND ITS FAILURE MODE IS SILENCE, WHICH IS WHY IT HAS ITS OWN TERM AND ITS OWN MEASUREMENT.** A sign
is PAINT ON A WALL, exactly coplanar with it, so a tie in the depth test loses and the lettering is
simply **not there** — no error, no warning, no gap in the picture, just a building with no name, which
looks exactly like a building that never had one. The 2-D queue could not fail that way: `DECO_LIFT`
sorts an adornment 0.6 tiles in front of its own host, so it drew whatever it was standing behind, and
**that lift had been covering for a real geometry bug** — The Dry Goods letters its false front on a
plane a tenth of a footprint INSIDE the board it is painted on, and nobody could have seen it. `cam.unproj`
takes a `pull` that slides a corner along its own view ray toward the eye (`sx` and `sy` are both ratios
in `f`, so the projection does not move and only the depth does — `FACE_EPS`’s job, done where the ray
is known); the inset itself was fixed at the model. `__glSign()` in the Modelshop is the A/B — the same
renderer with the signage drawn two ways, so nothing else moves and every pixel of difference IS the
lettering. ⚠ **Square on is not enough**, and that is where its first draft stopped: at a GRAZING angle a
building’s own parapet and cornice cross the lettering PLANE, so the inset that matters is the one along
the view ray rather than along the wall normal, and it is a different number at every heading — the sweep
carries all four entrance facings from three tiles off the axis for the same reason `gl:mesh` captures
four rather than the canonical one. **72 cases, 55 of them exactly 0, worst 0.020%** — a few letters
trimmed off the end of a board by the parapet that is genuinely in front of them — against **0.062% with
the geometry bug put back**, which is the only reason to trust the number.

⚠ **AND A SORT BIAS IS NOT A DEPTH OFFSET (`DECO_PULL`, and `BLADE_PROUD` for the one part that really
does stand off its building).** `DECO_LIFT` is 0.6 of a tile and it is a QUEUE POSITION: how far an
adornment jumps forward in the painter's order. `emitDecoQuad` and `emitDecoFill` spent that same number
as a `cam.unproj` PULL, which moves the thing 0.6 of a tile through the world — and 0.6 is more than a
building has to give, since a host's own near wall is ~0.44 from the tile centre and a neighbour's is
~0.56. So a neon blade mounted on the FAR face of its own bar came out in front of the NEAR one and ran
straight down a wall it is physically behind, and a blade on the front face cleared the building in front
of it as well. That is both halves of "signage still shows through buildings, and it shows me its reverse
side". The pull is a TIE-BREAKER — paint on a wall is coplanar with the wall and a tie is a loss — so it
is capped at 0.05, and a caller asking for less keeps its own number. ⚠ **`neonBlade` is the exception and
it needed a measurement, not a feeling**: its arms author the anchor INSIDE the facade (on `type:bar`,
0.16 of a tile forward against a front wall at 0.328), because the queue sorted it clear and nobody ever
had to place it. At a tie-breaker's pull the wall wins and the only part of the sign that draws is
whatever crests the roofline, which is the sign not working. 0.25 clears the deepest facade and stays
under the near face of a building one tile in front. ⚠ **It is a PULL, NOT A MOVE** — `sx`/`sy` are
untouched, so GLASS 1's picture is exactly what it was and only the depth it is compared at moves.

⚠ **AND A SIGN HAS A FRONT (`cull` on a decal).** Lettering is paint on a surface, and paint does not
read from behind the thing it is painted on — but a textured quad drawn two-sided does, mirrored, glowing
through its own board like printing on glass. Voltage's roof sign read **"ƎƆATJOV"** from the back of its
own building. What belongs there is whatever the paint is on, and that is already drawn and already
opaque: the board is in the mesh, the wall is in the mesh, a per-tile board is a flat decal of its own.
So `emitSurfaceText` and `marqueeBand` mark their decals `cull` and the lettering stops at the surface.
⚠ **The FRONT of a sign is `gl_FrontFacing == false`** — the quads arrive as TL,TR,BR,BL, a sign's own
reading order, which walks CLOCKWISE in NDC when the sign faces you, and clockwise is the back face under
the default CCW winding. Inverting it hides every sign in the city and shows only the ones you are behind,
so the polarity is stated in the shader rather than inferred. ⚠ And `cull` is in the BATCH GROUPING key,
not merely carried on the batch: it is a uniform set once per draw call, so two decals sharing a texture
and disagreeing about it would be one batch and one of them would silently get the other's answer.

⚠ **AND A SIGN AND ITS BOARD WERE SHED BY TWO DIFFERENT RULES, ONLY ONE OF WHICH WAS A RULE (`RENDER_TUNE.signFar`, 0 puts the three-tile shed back).** Reported as *"all signage, mandarin and english, are not showing their text at all"*, and it was true of the whole city: a derived-kit board, gantry or blade is captured into the per-model MESH at `ADORN_NEAR` and the GPU draws that at every distance, while the LETTERING on it is a decal built fresh each frame from the tile’s own name — and the only pass that emits one is `detailLayer`, which returned above `RENDER_TUNE.detailNear`. THREE TILES. ⚠ **A `$name` board was not blank, it was ABSENT**, because its slab is a `paint` quad — per TILE, so in no per-model mesh by construction — which is why the fix has to let that one quad through and suppress every other: lettering restored over a board still missing is words in mid-air, a worse picture than the blank plate. Measured on one shop at a FIXED world tile (so its kit roll cannot move under the sweep): 17 decals at two tiles and **ZERO at three through fourteen**, with the board on screen from the mesh the whole way. ⚠ **The obvious fix was already measured and rejected** — `signfloor.mjs`’s own closing note records raising `detailNear` 3→6 taking a dense night block 21.0→24.3 ms and 3→9 taking it to 36.2 — and this is not that: past the ring the pass runs with every part that letters nothing SKIPPED and every quad but a `$name` board suppressed, so `framecost` is unchanged (the 2-D painter never takes this path at all: it needs a live decal sink, where a sign is eight affine strips and shedding it is the renderer working as designed) and a real Coldwater block costs +60–70 decals a frame for 1.5–3× as many signs lettered. ⚠ **The distance is the legibility curve rather than a round number**: 309 px of restored sign at three tiles, 80 at six, 38 at ten, 24 at twelve on a 1200-wide frame — about seven at the 640 the game draws. ⚠ And **`ms` is not the evidence here**, because on a hidden preview pane every frame-time difference measured landed inside `__glFrame`’s own spread; what is deterministic is `calls2d` (unmoved) and the decal count. `npm run` has no name for it: the gate is `node scripts/shapes/signrange.mjs`, the sibling of `signfloor` one ring out, and its mutation control is the size of the bug — **119 of 196 models lose their lettering with the flag at 0**.

⚠ **AND THE OTHER HALF OF THAT REPORT WAS A SECOND OPINION ABOUT OCCLUSION, WHICH IS THE SAME BUG THE LIGHTS HAD.** With the ring fixed, what was left was *"most signs fixed, just not all mandarin character signs"* — and the hanzi blade is not special, it is just the ONLY lettered blade the derived kit hangs (the other carries a mark or nothing). `emitSurfaceText` asked `decoHidden` before every push, which is correct on the 2-D path, where the probe is the only occlusion a sign gets, and is a SECOND ANSWER on the GPU path to a question `gl/decals.js` already settles per pixel. The two disagree in one direction only: the probe is all-or-nothing against a rasterised field with a bias, a shrink and a grow, so every case it gets wrong is a visible sign DELETED. ⚠ **And the sign it gets wrong most is a blade**, which hangs off a building’s corner out over the pavement — beside, and partly behind, the facade it is bolted to, which is exactly the partly-covered case the probe answers worst. Measured on the Solenne block at night: **30 of 62 signs dropped at one heading and 12 of 14 at another**, now 0 at all four, with the sign count going 2→14, 32→66, 43→64 and 12→15. ⚠ **Nothing draws through a building as a result, and that is measured rather than assumed**: the same shop behind a nine-storey wall contributes **0 pixels** with the probe gone (the quads are pushed and the depth test clips every one), against 88,837 in clear air. Same argument, in the same words, as the one that stopped `glowPool` probing. ⚠ **The canvas fallback keeps the probe** — `emitDeco` does its own — so what is left in `SIGN_TALLY.hidden` means "the 2-D fallback dropped one", and in a GLASS 2 frame it should read 0. ⚠ No headless gate can see this class: the probe reads a field a real frame rasterises, so under `canvasResidue` it answers false and the bug is invisible — the instrument is `signTally()` on a painted frame.

⚠ **AND `__glLeak` CANNOT SEE ANY OF THAT, WHICH IS WHY A GREEN 173-MODEL RUN MEANT NOTHING HERE.** Its
subject stands at EIGHT tiles and the 2-D painter runs `detailLayer` only within `RENDER_TUNE.detailNear`
— THREE — so not one board, blade, canopy, pipe, lamp or neon run is ever emitted into a frame it
measures. Same blindness `glresidue` was carrying one layer up. ⚠ **And it is not a parameter**, which was
tried and measured: the wall is three rows deep, so a subject close enough for the kit lands IN the wall
rather than behind it, and pulled back a row the wall no longer covers it (98 of 173 then draw almost
nothing over it and prove nothing) — restoring the bug and re-running finds zero either way. Seeing this
class needs ONE low occluder between the camera and a subject two tiles out, which this arrangement
cannot express. Until somebody builds that, **the picture is the instrument**: reverse `ent` so the sign
is on the far face, and look. ⚠ What DID change is that its noise floor is now measured **with the subject
in the scene** — the wall-only pair is a floor for the wall and cannot see a blink light's phase, a
stack's smoke or a cache eviction, so it read a confident **0** while the same sweep re-run on a fresh
page moved between 0 and 300 px. The floor itself still moves (65 and 79 px on two runs), so a row just
over it is a question, not a verdict; what is unpinned has not been run down, and it is neither the clock
nor `Math.random`, both of which are.

⚠ **AND EVERY SIGN IN THE CITY WAS LETTERED ON THE SAME TYPEWRITER (`signFontOf`).** `SIGN_TRADE` named a
hand for 40 of the 173 models and everything else got bold monospace — and it was worse than that, because
an ARM's own signage passes `bakeSignText` no options at all, so every name cut into a frieze, painted on
a false front or run down a blade was mono whatever the table said about its type. The hand is derived now
on exactly the axis `accentOf` already uses for colour: the trade's where the table names one, the MATERIAL
family otherwise, because what a building is faced in is the best available statement of when it went up.
Six faces (`deco` and `condensed` are new), published through `_signFace`, the `_bladeSign` idiom. ⚠ **A
works keeps the typewriter** — stencil on steel is what a plant letters itself in, and `plain` (51 models,
nearly all Terminus and the Thornwarren) falls through to it for the same reason. ⚠ **And the hand must not
decide how big the sign is**: almost every caller maps the baked canvas onto a quad it sized against the
RESERVED box, a cell per character, so the texture's aspect is a contract — measure the ink instead and a
serif "THE MERIDIAN" is half the width and comes out two-thirds too wide on an unchanged frieze. The ink is
measured and the BOX is kept; it grows only when a face overruns, and `tight` (the road sign, the one
caller that fits its quad to the texture) is the stated exception.

⚠ **AND THE DERIVED KIT MAY BE DECLINED ONE SECTION AT A TIME (`KIT_DECLINE`).** `have` already keeps the
kit out of a section somebody has drawn; this is the other half of that sentence — a section nobody has
drawn and nobody should. One entry so far: `meridian`, a 1930s deco apartment landmark whose crown is a
stone lantern under a verdigris cupola and whose name is already cut into a limestone frieze, was being
given a backlit hoarding on legs over the roof. The rest of its kit stays. The test for adding one is
whether the part would be wrong on that building whoever built it; an arm that merely has taste about its
own facade should draw the facade.

⚠ **AND THE WORLD IS DONE, WHICH IS A DIFFERENT CLAIM FROM "GLASS 1 IS RETIRED".** `windshield.js`
cannot be retired and should not be: it runs the 173 arms that PRODUCE the mesh, the lights and the
decals, it owns the scene, the occluder field, LOD and collision, and it draws the overlay. GLASS 2
is the rasteriser underneath it. By that measure the world has arrived — with the dial pinned, a cab
frame is **2.8-3.6 ms** and an aircraft frame **6.5-8.3 ms** at 640×360, the city is the SMALL half
of its own frame (1.1-3.7 ms of it), and the 2-D adornment queue is **43 faces**. What is left on the
canvas is an overlay — the distant ridge, sky and cloud layers, weather, the dash, the glass, the
badges — and it should stay: no ordering problem to solve, no measurable cost, and the HUD needs
text, so moving it means building a glyph atlas to replace something that already works.

⚠ **AND THE THREE WAYS MEASURING THAT WENT WRONG ARE WORTH MORE THAN THE ANSWER.** `__glFrame()` in
the Modelshop exists because the "what do we port next" question was got wrong three times running,
convincingly each time. **A city built from hand-picked models picks the answer** — eight chosen
names put the 2-D queue at 1,597 faces a frame and named it the last big thing left, and one of the
eight was The Meridian Lobby, whose gargoyles alone are 44% of every face all 173 models emit; swept
across the registry the whole queue is 861 faces, mean 5 a model, 63 models emitting none. **A loose
resolution dial reads as the renderer** — it sheds resolution exactly where the frame is expensive,
so a storm measured cheaper than clear sky and night cheaper than day. And **a call count is not a
millisecond**: `drawSkyline` is 492 of the `1,800 calls left in a frame and is two filled 241-point
polylines whose median over 200 reps is **0 ms**; storm weather is 1,477 calls and does not move the
frame either. ⚠ The harness reports a SPREAD for the same reason — the same seat gave the occluder
pre-pass an 8.4 ms COST and a 6.7 ms SAVING in two consecutive runs, which is how a plan to delete it
nearly got made. A difference inside the spread is not a finding.
⚠ **HARDWARE COVERAGE IS CHECKED AGAINST THE STANDARD, NOT AGAINST THIS GPU.** `glCapabilities()` answers "will this machine run it" from a throwaway context. The pass wants 8 vertex attributes against a WebGL2 guarantee of 16 and 13 varying components against 60 — neither is close. ⚠ **The texture page is the one that can bite**: the guarantee is only `MAX_TEXTURE_SIZE ≥ 2048` and every surface in the city at `texRes: 2` wants **2048×4096**, which on a floor-spec device is an `INVALID_VALUE` and nothing else — no throw, no warning, a city wearing a black texture. `buildAtlas` takes the device limit and refuses a page that will not fit, dropping to flat palette colours with one warning; the packing also balances on the CELL rather than the count, which brings the `texRes: 1` worst case to 2048×2048. A typical frame is a 256×512 page. ⚠ **Other hardware is still untested** — every number here is one machine with a discrete NVIDIA card, and "falls back correctly" is not "is fast on an integrated GPU". If it ever measures SLOWER somewhere, the first knob is `antialias` in `createGLView`: the 2-D canvas has no MSAA, so GL is buying smoother edges nobody asked for at full-frame cost.

⚠ **AND A PASS THAT DID NOT DRAW HANDS THE WORLD BACK.** The mass is suppressed on the strength of the GL pass existing, so "it returned nothing" is not a quiet outcome — it is a city of floating lights standing on no buildings. A machine with **no WebGL2** and a driver that **took the context away** both arrive there WITHOUT THROWING, so the catch was never enough; the pass answers null and the flag goes back to 0, exactly as a throw does. The context-lost listener calls `preventDefault` (the default makes the loss permanent) and `lost()` is asked every frame.

⚠ **AND FAILING SAFE IS NOT THE SAME AS LETTING GO (2026-09-17).** Every way GLASS 2 can stop drawing was handled and none of them RELEASED anything. A scene owns a context and everything hanging off it — programs, the vertex buffer, the atlas page, the HDR chain, the shadow map, the mirror target, up to 448 cached textures — and `disposeWindshield` cleared the 2-D scene alone, so a seat that closed held all of it for the life of the page. Bounded and merely wasteful for the three fixed seats; **unbounded for the helm**, which mints a fresh canvas id per wheelhouse (`helm-chase-<random>`), so every open built a scene nothing would ever release. ⚠ **The browser's ceiling is the mechanism, and it is 16**: measured in a real browser, 24 opens made 24 contexts, and **from the seventeenth the browser force-lost one per open — OLDEST first**, which in a session is the seat the player is flying or driving. So closing a wheelhouse blacks out the cockpit, and at **156 MB a scene** (1920×1080, dpr 1 — 63 MB of it the MSAA×4 float colour buffer, 24 MB its depth, 12 MB the shadow map) the sixteen still resident are ~2.5 GB, which is the whole-browser crash underneath the black screen. ⚠ **`loseContext()` rather than dropping the reference**, because "collected eventually" is not a budget and there is no per-object dispose to write — losing the context frees all of it in one call. The seam is `glDisposeScene` in world.js reached through `installGLDispose`, because windshield.js must not import gl/. ⚠ **AND THE CAP IS THE PART THAT MATTERS LATER** — a dispose path is a thing a caller can forget and this file cannot see who forgot, so `MAX_SCENES = 8` evicts the least-recently-PAINTED (not the Map's own insertion order, which would take the live seat and keep the abandoned ones) and turns forgetting into a rebuild. ⚠ **No headless gate can see any of this**: every harness in the repo installs a GL hook that returns null, so not one of them reaches a context at all. The instrument is the Modelshop with `HTMLCanvasElement.prototype.getContext` patched to count `webgl2` creations, and `texImage2D`/`renderbufferStorage` patched to tally bytes — ⚠ branch on ARITY there, since the 9-argument overload carries width and height where the 6-argument one carries the format and type enums, and reading 6408×5121 as a size reports 2 GB for one frame.

⚠ **AND FIDELITY IS MEASURED, AT 1.0%.** `__glFidelity()` in the Modelshop is the spike's "does it look like the same city" question as a function — the first answer, 6.8-12.7%, was a number in a README that nothing could re-run. ⚠ It compares ONE BUILDING, ALONE, from a fixed seat, because a whole-frame diff of a city measures COVERAGE (GL draws the whole window and the lights reach 22 tiles) and every one of those differences is an improvement that would land in the number as error. ⚠ **And the clock is frozen** — two renders of the same EMPTY scene differ by ninety-eight thousand pixels, because the clouds drift, the birds fly and the water moves. Two things closed it: **the far edge staggers** (each tile dissolves at its own moment, up to three tiles inside the limit, or a row gives up its opacity in unison — one expression, `hazeJitter`, with two readers) and **a barrel roof carries the painter's own shade** (the one mass primitive that lights itself, worst case at 3.0%; ⚠ `flat` is a LIGHTING answer and not a kind, so a barrel stays MASS and `gl:mesh` goes on checking it). What is left is `emitFlat`'s hairline stroke, inside the noise.

⚠ **AN ARCHETYPE IS A BUILDING TOO, and the occluder field had never heard of one.** A `building_type` with no model of its own is drawn by the shared biome set (`drawBuilding`, not `drawTypeModel`), and everything downstream of capture asks `shapeForModel`, which answered null — so the pre-pass `continue`d and an archetype contributed NOTHING: `luxtower` is a thirty-storey tower, and contacts, lights and the own ship went straight through it. The capture path dispatches on the arm now, so an archetype captures through the same primitives, sink and affine solve as the 172 models, and `shapes:smoke` holds all eleven to the same two gates (it captures; it is affine at an unseen scale) off a list DERIVED from `BLDG_TYPE_3D` rather than written beside it. The evidence is the budget going DOWN — framecost 278,336 → 277,545 with eight fewer `shadowBlur` passes, which are signs that had been drawing through buildings. ⚠ **AND THE NIGHT CITY KEEPS ITS LIGHTS PAST NINE TILES.** `lodAdorn` sheds lights past `lodNear` because on canvas they are the expensive half, and a cab's `lodNear` is NINE TILES — so from a truck at night the far end of a lit street went dark. As a depth-tested quad a glow is six vertices, so with the sprite sink installed `glowPool` sheds at the tier a beacon does and the window bloom's 8-tile cull opens to 22. GL-only: with the flag off the ladder is what it always was.

⚠ **AND EVERYTHING ELSE A BUILDING WEARS IS ON IT TOO (`RENDER_TUNE.glDeco`, the **GL adornments** slider; 0 puts it all back).** The lights went first and the Curtain, the marquees and the world text followed, and what was left was the third shape GLASS is made of: a **STROKE** — a mast, a lattice leg, a catwalk rail, a guy wire, a crane jib, a light-runner tracing a tower's corner — plus a handful of screen-space billboards (a blade, a dish, a cross, a perched buzzard, a windsock) and a few flat world polygons (a helideck's markings, a forecourt's paint, a price board). Every one of them was still painting on the canvas that is composited AFTER the city, with `decoHidden` the only thing between it and the warehouse in front — and `decoHidden` answers per SURFACE: hidden only when the WHOLE of it is covered, so a blade standing proud of a roofline clears at its anchor and draws in full, straight through the building. **That is every "showing through a building" report there has ever been here.**

⚠ **A STROKE IS GEOMETRY AFTER ALL, WHICH IS THE PART THE FILE ALREADY SAID WAS IMPOSSIBLE.** Three separate comments read "a stroke has a screen width and no world thickness, so there is nothing to hand a triangle list" — true of a mesh, and not true of a vertex shader, which is exactly where a screen width can be expressed. `client/game/js/panels/gl/strokes.js` expands two world endpoints and a pixel width into a quad the same way the sprite layer expands a light from a point and a radius: six vertices a segment, no texture, no bake, one draw call for every wire in the city. ⚠ **AND IT IS NOT A BAKED BILLBOARD, WHICH WAS TRIED AND MEASURED AND TAKEN BACK OUT** — `markBillboard` re-bakes at the live camera every frame, a canvas repaint plus a `texImage2D` per instance per frame, affordable for a handful of landmarks on their own tiles and a cockpit framerate below GLASS 1 for a part that hangs off ordinary buildings by the dozen. ⚠ **A 0.8-pixel brace is a whole pixel at 80% alpha**, not 80% of a pixel of coverage, so the layer floors the width at one device pixel and carries the shortfall in the alpha — without that a lattice reads as a scatter of dropouts. ⚠ And the `shadowBlur` halo a neon wire wears at night is **one more quad**, additive and sorted to the end, rather than the single most expensive thing canvas2d does.

⚠ **THE REST WENT TO THE LAYER ITS OWN SHAPE BELONGS IN, AND THE KEY IS THE APPEARANCE, NEVER THE CAMERA.** A screen-space billboard becomes a decal through `cam.unproj` (`emitDecoQuad`); a flat world polygon in a plain colour becomes one directly (`emitDecoFill`); a puff of smoke becomes a sprite. ⚠ **`emitFlat` → the MESH is the wrong home for several of these and that is a decision, not an oversight**: a mesh is captured ONCE per model at a fixed hour and shared by every tile drawing it, so a surface whose colour is chosen by whether it is night has no single answer at capture time — a dark doorway that is deliberately warmer after dark is exactly that. ⚠ **A recessed opening IS depth-tested quads after all, and the way in is to SLIDE IT INTO THE WALL** (`slideToPlane`/`emitRecessBay`). It is painted as a dark rectangle with the back wall laid OVER it, which is the painter's own trick and not what the geometry says — the back wall is BEHIND the opening it is seen through, so handed over at its own depth the buffer hides it and the recess comes out flat, and `draw3DBoxAt` draws all four walls and has no way to leave a hole for it. That bought a `markBillboard` bake for months, at one depth, re-baked every frame. ⚠ **But a point slid along its OWN eye ray projects to the same pixel wherever on that ray it sits** — `cam.unproj`'s `pull` already turns that on for a tie-breaker — so each interior corner is slid FORWARD until it lands in the plane of the gable: the picture does not move, the parallax is still solved from the live eye every frame, and every layer becomes a flat world quad lying in the facade, which is the one shape `emitDecoFill` was written for. Per-pixel against the city, at the wall's own depth across the whole opening, with no bake and no upload. ⚠ **The layers must be STAGGERED**, because collapsing them onto one plane collapses the painter's order with them — each sits about `FACE_EPS` in front of what it covers, every step well inside `DECO_PULL`. ⚠ **And the 1.4px frame became three quads rather than a stroke**: the only part of it you could ever see was the half spilling OUTSIDE the rim onto the lit wall (inside it, it was a near-black line on a near-black opening, six values apart), and `emitWire` has to spend a real pull to clear the mass it is authored inside — a rim traced on a gable under a barrel roof is inside that roof's own solid, so four segments came out in front of their own building to be drawn at all, measured at 42 leaked points in `glself`. Three slabs of jamb and lintel lying flat in the wall leak 0. The canvas composite stays as the fallback for the angles where the solve is degenerate. ⚠ **The one surface that may NOT move is the regional hangar's lit departure lounge**, because what it is behind is the pavilion's own glass and the mass pass writes depth whatever its alpha — as a quad it would be hidden by the very glass it is read through. It keeps the canvas and gets `markHidden` (which spans the pavilion) instead of a probe at one point in its middle, and it declares itself through **`keptOnCanvas`** so the gate below can allow that one reason without allowing the eight-thousand-line function it lives in.

⚠ **AND IT IS GATED HEADLESSLY, BECAUSE THE FAILURE IS SILENT IN EVERY DIRECTION.** `npm run gl:residue` ([scripts/shapes/glresidue.mjs](scripts/shapes/glresidue.mjs), in `shapes:smoke` and therefore in the push gate) runs every model arm the way a GL frame runs it — every sink open, `MASS_OFF` and `FLAT_OFF` set — and tallies what still lands in `FACE_SINK` **by the function that queued it**. That is a question the finished frame cannot answer: at flush time the closure's origin is gone, and every guess at it before this was wrong (the mast and the dish were each blamed, patched and found innocent while `latticeTower` sat there queueing thirty-nine of them). It went from **16 painters and 470 faces to one declared exception**. ⚠ The gate is an ALLOW-LIST OF REASONS, never of function names — a budget passes a new leak the moment somebody deletes an old one. ⚠ **And it needs a REAL camera**: every one of these reaches the decal layer through `cam.unproj`, which a stub camera has none of, so under the stub the census is a list of things that are already fixed.

⚠ **AND AN ADORNMENT STILL HAS TO CLEAR ITS OWN BUILDING, WHICH THE LIFT WAS ALWAYS DOING FOR IT.** `DECO_LIFT` is 0.6 tiles and reads like a sorting hack; it is not. A mast stands at its tile's CENTRE and the front wall is 0.44 tiles nearer, a fire stair is bolted to a wall it is physically inside, a dish sits on a roof among the crown boxes — the painter's queue put all of them in front of their own host, and a depth buffer compares instead, the host wins, and the part is **simply not there**. The Dynamo lost its entire external fire stair that way, silently. So every one of these paths applies the caller's own lift through `cam.unproj`, which slides a point along its OWN view ray toward the eye: the projection stays bit-for-bit where it was and only the depth moves. It is still far less than the ~1 tile gap to any building actually in front, so a wire goes behind a real building and now does it per pixel instead of all-or-nothing.

⚠ **AND PAINT ON A HORIZONTAL SURFACE IS NOT VISIBLE FROM UNDERNEATH, WHICH IS THE WHOLE OF WHAT WAS REPORTED.** The Solenne's sky pad is an aiming circle, a touchdown ring and an H lying on a slab at the top of a tower. Lifted 0.6 tiles toward the camera they were painted anyway — from the street, four tiles from the foot of the building and a very long way BELOW the deck, which is "a circle on the Solenne under the sky pad". On the depth buffer the markings sit a hair above their own slab and the slab hides them exactly as a real one would; above the pad the two renderers agree pixel for pixel. ⚠ The corollary is real and deliberate: **there is no seat in GLASS from which a very tall tower's pad markings can be seen**, because the optical axis is horizontal and the roof leaves the bottom of the frame before the eye gets above it. The guidance a pilot actually flies is `drawRoofPadCatch`, which is a separate thing and unaffected.

⚠ **AND A BAKE MUST NOT REPEAT WHAT THE QUAD ALREADY DOES.** The Dynamo's wind wheel is raked (`sq`) and its quad is already `R` by `R · sq` — a texture drawn squashed as well came out at 0.38 of its height, with nine tin vanes reduced to a few thin spokes and the rim band clipped off the edge of its own canvas. The texture says what a thing LOOKS like and the quad does all of the placing; that split is the rule the whole layer runs on, and it is the one a bake is most likely to break.
⚠ **AND IT IS MEASURED IN PIXELS TOO (`__glLeak()` in the Modelshop), WHOSE FIRST DRAFT ASKED THE WRONG QUESTION.** Stood behind a wall of thirty-storey towers every model reports zero leak — a true answer, and not the one that matters: `decoHidden` is all-or-nothing and gets the fully-covered case RIGHT. The bug is the partial one, so the wall is a **nine-storey warehouse** and the subject is taller than it. Twelve models, night and day: **2,700 leaked pixels with the adornments on the canvas against 588 with them on the depth buffer**, over a noise floor of 648 across the same cases — Halcyon Towers 400 → 16, the Solenne 367 → 56, The Green Room 194 → 8. ⚠ Two things had to be pinned before the numbers held still, and each cost a working measurement: **settle the clock rather than merely freezing it** (`fadeLights` ramps over ELAPSED time, so under a frozen clock no light reaches its slot and the same model measured 24, 14 and 37 on three consecutive runs) and **pin `Math.random`** (GLASS throws a meteor across a clear night sky on a random timer, and settling the fade is exactly what advances it). ⚠ The harness **prints its own noise floor** rather than subtracting it — the same map rendered twice still differs by a few pixels along the wall's roofline, nobody has run that down, and a leak inside it is not a finding. ⚠ **AND THE FRAME COST IS INSIDE THE SPREAD, WHICH IS THE CLAIM AND NOT A HEDGE.** One sweep read a cab frame 2.6 → 1.9 ms and 3.2 → 2.2 ms and the next read the same pair the other way about; the honest statement is that it costs nothing measurable, which is what you would expect when a light-runner stops being two dozen queue entries each setting `shadowBlur` — the single most expensive thing canvas2d does — and becomes two dozen quads in one draw call. A dense night cab frame carries 2,086 strokes, 42 decals and 396 lights on the GPU that used to be 2-D faces.
⚠ **AND THE LIGHTS ARE SPRITES NOW.** Every light in GLASS is one shape — a world point, a radius in SCREEN pixels, a colour and an alpha — so `client/game/js/panels/gl/sprites.js` draws `glowPool`, `drawCityBloom` and `blinkLight` as depth-TESTED quads, and **the occlusion probe stops being asked**: hiding a light behind a wall was `decoHidden` against a rasterised field with a bias, a shrink and a grow, each a number somebody chose, and it is the bug the whole plan opened with. ⚠ **The radius is handed over, never re-derived** — the identical `clamp(k / f, lo, hi)` the painter computes, so the two renderers cannot drift apart by one being retuned; the quad is expanded from the point in the vertex shader. ⚠ **A light writes no depth** (it is the appearance of a thing, not a thing, or two glows on one wall cut holes in each other), ⚠ **the additive ones are sorted to the end of the buffer** (a bloom ADDS and a ground glow lays over, and painting one as the other is a lit window versus a grey smear), and ⚠ **a light needs a nudge toward the camera in clip z**, because it sits ON the surface it belongs to and would otherwise z-fight — the same job `DECO_LIFT` does by sorting. Measured on a night cab frame: **9.6 → 7.2 ms**, 2,458 faces and 44 lights on the GPU. That also makes lights cheap to author, which matters less than it looked: only 14 models are actually dark. See tools/modelshop/README.md.

⚠ **AND A PIXEL SIZE HANDED TO ANY OF THOSE LAYERS IS IN DEVICE PIXELS (`npm run gl:dprsize`).** Three layers size themselves in pixels rather than world units — lights, strokes and billboards — and all three convert with `2.0 / uViewport` against the CANVAS viewport, so a producer must scale by `_frameDpr`. `pushLight` and `pushStroke` did; **all four billboard producers did not**, so every landmark, every scatter species, the rooftop marquee neon and every air contact rendered at `1/dpr` of the size the 2-D painter draws them. ⚠ **That is not a hidpi-only bug, which is why it reached prod as a moving one**: the ratio is `baseDpr × st.resStep`, and `resStep` is the adaptive frame-time dial, so the error is not a constant — the South Gate grew and shrank between two screenshots of a parked helicopter. The dial's own harness says how far: `dials` reports a 14→48→14 ms ramp visiting 0.6/0.7/0.8/0.9/1, which is a **1.67× swing** on a landmark that has not moved. ⚠ **It is exactly ZERO at dpr 1** — every headless harness (`dom-stub` sets `devicePixelRatio: 1`) and every Modelshop scene — the same trap as the camera matrix built in the canvas's units, and answered the same way: sweep ratios. ⚠ **Sizing the quad is only half** — leave the bake canvas at CSS resolution and you ship a landmark that is the right size and visibly soft, so `markBillboard` and `bakeContacts` rasterise at `_frameDpr` too (their size GUARDS stay on the CSS box, which is a fact about the view rather than the display). One funnel, `pushBillboard`, because four producers meant four chances to forget. ⚠ **`drawMarquee`'s push is unreachable today** and is a stated exception in the gate rather than a name on a list: `bldgStyle` only answers arch `marquee` for bt bar/club/casino, a bt present always takes the table over the biome, and all three have `TYPE_MODEL` arms — so `modelFor` answers first and `drawBuilding`'s `case 'marquee'` never runs.

⚠ **AND THE ROAD FADING IS THE SAME DIAL, NOT A SECOND BUG.** Road geometry is world-space and dpr-blind (the mesh is identical at every canvas size), so what moves is how many device pixels a thin kerb or lane marking lands on — and with MSAA a sub-pixel quad is partial coverage, which is to say a fainter line. Measured from an aerial seat: across the dial's range the share of road quads under one device pixel goes **23.3% → 33.7%** and their mean coverage **0.609 → 0.504**. This file's own note at `resStep` predicted it in words ("the road blinking between light levels"); `deadbandStep` stopped it DITHERING and never stopped it resting somewhere else when the load changes. The kerb solve is floored at half a device pixel of half-width now, so a kerb is never thinner than a pixel — ⚠ a no-op at dpr ≥ 2/3, so it can only ever widen one that was about to dissolve. Lane markings are authored world widths and still shrink, correctly.

⚠ **AND A BUILDING WITH NO POWER GOES DARK NOW, WHICH IS A FEATURE THE SERVER HAD ALREADY SIMULATED FOR MONTHS.** A severe storm faults individual junction boxes offline — the building-level distribution feed rather than the hardened central plant — so the sim has produced scattered per-building blackouts since it shipped, `pw` reached the client the whole time, the traffic signals read it, the streetlights read their own `sl`, and **the BUILDINGS read nothing**: a tower with its feed down kept its lit window grid, its neon, its blades, its blooms and its rooftop ad over a blacked-out street. ⚠ **THE WIRE IS FOUR ANSWERS AND USED TO BE ONE BIT** — `pw` is 1 powered / 2 browning out / 0 dark, with `em` and `og` over the last of those. `overloaded` collapsed into 0 and that was a LIE the canopy told: `applyPowerLightEffects` keeps the cheap fixtures on in a brownout, sheds the expensive ones, and explicitly holds streetlights up as infrastructure that never competes for the pool — so a browning-out junction went dark out of the windscreen while the room said the lights were flickering. **`pw === 0` still means exactly what it always meant**, which is why the signal test already reading it needed no change. ⚠ **AND `og` IS THE EXEMPTION WITH A WHOLE REGION BEHIND IT**: Deadwater is dark BY CONSTRUCTION — 4,836 orphan `power_zones` rows, one per tile, no generator anywhere — and so is the Under, and every light in those arms is flame, oil or carbide; read as a power cut this feature would have blacked out the Null's powerhouse for a fault their region cannot have. `getZoneOnGrid` tells a feed that FAILED from a connection that never EXISTED, which are identical to the sim and opposites out of a cockpit, and **a dead generator is still a generator** (out of fuel, smashed, storm-faulted — the blackout is real). ⚠ **IT IS TWO RENDERER STATES THAT LOOK LIKE ONE**: `POWER_DUTY` scales what a building EMITS and `POWER_WIN` says what the ROOMS BEHIND THE GLASS are doing — and that second one is **BAKED INTO THE WALL TEXTURE** rather than drawn, because a lit window in this city is a texture and not a light. Set only the first and a dark building wears a full grid of lit windows, which is most of the bug; set only the second and its neon is still on.
