Both are frame state armed per tile by the world pass, the `ADORN_TIER` idiom, because a power ARGUMENT would have to be threaded through `drawTypeModel` → `drawTypeModelArm` → every one of the 170-odd cases and the first arm anybody added would be the one that forgot it. ⚠ **ONLY THE NIGHT BAKE VARIES** — a building with its feed down looks like its neighbours at noon — so the day texture is shared and the GL atlas stays at one entry per palette however dark the city gets; and ⚠ **`flatWallCol` TAKES THE VARIANT TOO**, which is the easiest half to forget, since it is what a wall is painted with once it is too small to blit a texture onto: without it a blacked-out tower is dark up close and **lights up as you fly away**. ⚠ **SCALE ONCE, AT THE POINT THAT OWNS THE ALPHA**: guards in `glowPool`, `blinkLight`, `groundLamp` and `lightBeam` left **526 leaks** on the census's first run, because `pushLight` has a dozen DIRECT callers that reach no primitive at all — a stack's ember, a steam plume lit from below, a rooftop helideck's perimeter ring — so the emitters only ever switch OFF and the dimming is one line in `pushLight`, or `lightBeam` → `glowPool` → `pushLight` applies the same factor three times. ⚠ **AND THE GATE MAY NOT GO IN `pushStroke`**, which carries structure as well as light: a dark building's masts, lattice legs and catwalk rails would vanish with its neon (1,859 structural strokes stand in a blackout, and the gate asserts it). ⚠ **A DEAD SIGN IS A PAINTED BOARD, NEVER A DELETED ONE** — the streetlight's own rule pointed at signage — so `neonBlade`, `marqueeBand` and `bakeSignText` take `powerNight`, which is **idempotent** rather than a factor so nothing is quietly halved once per hop through a chain nobody traced. ⚠ **THE 2-D PAINTER NEEDS ONE THING GLASS 2 DOES NOT**: a dark building's arm runs at `night: 0` (the only seam for the lit-trim decisions 170 arms each make privately), and `draw3DBoxAt` derives its texture blend from that same argument — so without `POWER_NB` the wall comes out as the DAY texture, several shades PALER than the lit buildings beside it, which is exactly backwards. GLASS 2 gets the light half free and needs an atlas VARIANT per dark palette (⚠ never for a roof — no windows in it, and doubling every roof entry on a page with a hard device ceiling drops the whole city to flat colours) plus a group that may **disagree with the frame about `nb`**, since the mesh is memoised per MODEL and shared between tiles, so the variant rides the GROUP and `rectOf` takes it. ⚠ And `windowKey` carries the grid state — the one term in that key that is not a property of the building, so leaving it out means the buffer is not stale and the blackout arrives at the next corner instead of now. `npm run gl:power` ([scripts/shapes/power.mjs](scripts/shapes/power.mjs), in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in `shapes:smoke`, per the two-lists warning above): 61 claims over 267 models, mutation-tested 13 of 13, with the GLASS 2 hop chain read out of the source ⚠ **with comments blanked first** — two mutants survived a raw-source scan because the gate's own prose named the identifiers it was looking for. ⚠ **AND THE PICTURE IS `__glPower()` / `__powerShot()` IN THE MODELSHOP**, since no harness here reaches a GL draw call: on a 640x360 cab seat at 23:00 over 9,312 wall pixels — control 0%, brownout 8.0%, dark 21.6%, emergency 25.3%, **off-grid 0%** (the exemption, exactly), emergency-vs-dark 10.2% at worst 37. At 13:00 every state collapses to the control, which is the design rather than a weak result. ⚠ **THE EMERGENCY CIRCUIT WAS AN OCCUPANCY OF 0.06 FIRST AND MEASURED AS ONE PANE PER FACADE**, so `dark` and `emergency` returned an identical 90.8% / worst 60 — a feature nobody can tell from a full blackout is not a feature, and the fix is not a bigger number (scattering more rooms reads as a building half occupied, which is what a brownout already says) but a COLUMN: the stair lit at every landing, the one thing a battery is there for. ⚠ **AND A BENCH SCENE OF NAMED MODELS MEASURES HALF THE FEATURE** — a named model binds its own arm and PAINTS ITS OWN FACADE, so the shared biome wall texture the window grid lives in is never reached, and that is how the pair read byte-identical for an afternoon; the scene carries plain `bt` buildings as well now, and the named half still earns its place as the only half with neon, blades and lettering on it. ⚠ **AND THE EMERGENCY PAIR IS COMPARED TO EACH OTHER, NEVER TO THE LIT STREET**, or both land on the same figure to a tenth of a per cent and the row says nothing about whether the stairwell is drawn.

⚠ Two traps that cost real time: **`wallTex`'s bake closure binds `w` to the palette RGB** three hundred lines from the top, so a mode read through that name compares an ARRAY to a small integer, every branch takes the ordinary arm, and a blackout bakes a fully lit facade while every counter reports the feature working; and `type:noodle_bar` **emits 0 strokes on its first run and 4 on every one after**, a cold shape/kit cache, so a census comparing a model's first two runs is reporting the cache.

⚠ **AND THE CITY LIGHTS ITS OWN WALLS.** The same list the sprite layer draws is fed to the MASS shader as point lights (`RENDER_TUNE.glLights`, 0 puts it back), so a neon sign washes the facade it is bolted to instead of hanging in front of a wall that has never heard of it. Nothing new is authored, nothing new is collected, and **the term is ADDED after the key shading**, so a frame with no lights in it is unchanged. ⚠ **How far a light reaches comes from how BRIGHT it is, not from how big its bloom is drawn**, and the first cut had that backwards. A sprite carries a radius in SCREEN pixels, so converting it back through the projection looks like the obvious answer and is an answer to the wrong question: that radius is `clamp(k / f, lo, hi)`, a clamped screen size chosen so a halo looks right on a canvas, carrying almost nothing about how much light the source puts out. Measured, it resolved every sign in the city to about half a tile of reach and the whole feature moved **ZERO pixels** on a building lit by two neon signs. ⚠ **The diffuse term is WRAPPED, and that was measured into rather than chosen**: the commonest light in GLASS is mounted FLUSH on the wall behind it, so the direction from that wall to the light is nearly perpendicular to the wall's own normal and a pure cosine is ~0 — straight lambert moved 0.4% of the wall pixels in a dense night frame against 1.7% at wrap 0.5. ⚠ **And it is scaled by the NIGHT rather than left to the sprite alphas**, because GLASS goes on drawing signage by day: without that a shopfront measured a pink cast over 19,000 pixels of its own wall AT NOON. `__glLights()` in the Modelshop is the measurement — **16.9% of wall pixels moved on a dense night cab frame, 12.8% from the air, 0.3% by day**, at no cost this machine can tell from its own noise. ⚠ **That row has now been wrong twice and in two different ways, and neither was a regression.** It read 43/23.5/0 until 2026-09-11, when the gain was cut 1.5 → 0.45 because the wash was drowning the buildings it was built for — and, the one worth remembering, **the harness had been reporting 0.0% at every setting of its own sweep since `fadeLights` shipped**. It went to **0.70** on the sweep's knee and came straight back to **0.45 on the eye in the game**, which is the part worth keeping. The bench was not wrong about where the knee is; it was answering the wrong question. `litPct` and `worst` say how far a setting is from SATURATING a wall, and the live report was never that walls were saturating — it was *"the wash effect is too dramatic"*, meaning it reads as a light show over a city rather than as signs lighting the walls they are bolted to. No column in that table is about that, and there is no obvious one to add, because the thing being judged is whether the effect draws attention to itself. ⚠ **`wrap` came down 0.6 → 0.35 in the same breath, and it is the ANGLE half**: reported as *"a few things seem influenced by the external camera view at certain angles"*, which is exactly what a wrapped diffuse does — it lights faces turned AWAY from the light, so from an external camera you see walls glowing whose source is round the other side of the building and nothing on screen explains them. It is measurably an aerial term: at a fixed gain, dropping wrap to 0 costs the cab 4.2 points of coverage and the air seat 7.4, so it does close to twice as much work at the seat the complaint came from. ⚠ **And a sweep row named as a MULTIPLE and written as an ABSOLUTE goes stale silently**: `gain x1.5` held a literal 2.25, which was ×1.5 of the 1.5 that shipped when it was written and ×5 of what shipped afterwards, printing a label nobody would have chosen the number for. The gain rows are named by their value now. ⚠ **AND IT WENT 0.45 → 0.18 ON 2026-09-12, WHICH IS THE THIRD CUT AND THE FIRST ONE WITH A CAUSE RATHER THAN A PREFERENCE**: the wash is additive per light with no ceiling, and the city has been gaining emissive surfaces the whole time — the neon tone pass, window ribbons, roof armatures, corner blades, lamp posts, eighty-four lettered signboards — so a denser frontage buys more wash out of an untouched gain, and **a number calibrated against last month's city is too big today without anybody editing it**. Four of the twelve slots land within half a tile of each other on one facade, which takes four washes where a walk-up with one sign takes one; the gain reaches both, so it is set for the dense case. Held against the picture, flattening starts between 0.20 and 0.30 and 0.45 is the 1.5 failure again at a third of the number. ⚠ **The same change split `wet` out of `gain`**, because `rgbRaw` — what a wet road reflects — was scaled by the same knob, so cutting the wall wash would have dimmed every neon reflection on a rainy street to 40% of what it was swept at, inside a change that is only about walls. `wet` keeps 0.45. **Every gain row in the sweep was ABOVE what ships**, which is the right table for "how much headroom is left" and useless for another cut; it brackets both sides now. ⚠ **AND THE FOURTH CHANGE WAS TO THE REACH, BECAUSE THE GAIN HAD NEVER BEEN THE KNOB** (2026-09-17, reported as *"can we make signs have a bloom and that be the wash light instead of it affecting an entire building"*). Put the reach in the units the city is built in and the three cuts above stop looking like taste: a storey is `FLOOR_Z × bldgH × bldgStretch` ≈ **0.196 tiles**, a building is **0.76 tiles** wide, and an ordinary neon reached `minR + span·√I` ≈ **3.06 tiles** — fifteen storeys up the facade and four building-widths across. The wash was never too bright; it was **the size of a city block**, and the only way to stop a block-sized light flattening a facade is to turn it down until it does nothing, which is the ratchet 1.5 → 0.18 was walking down. So the wall takes a SHARE of the reach (`wallR` 0.32, about a tile) with a falloff exponent over it (`focus` 3, where 2 is exactly the term that shipped), and the gain goes to **2.5**. ⚠ **THE REACH WAS NOT THE WALL'S TO SHRINK — the same trap `wet` was split out of one sentence earlier.** `r` has THREE readers: this wash, and the wet road in **both `ground.js` and `floor.js`**, where it is the length of a neon streak on tarmac and was swept at those numbers; shrinking `minR`/`span` would have taken every rainy-street reflection down with the wall, silently, inside a change that is only about walls. `minR`/`span` keep their values, the road goes on reading `r`, and the wall reads a new `rw`. ⚠ **AND `washK` EXISTS BECAUSE A LIT FACADE IS AN AREA SOURCE**: `facadeGlow` tags its lights `wash` and a storey of lit windows genuinely does throw a broad dim field, so tightening it would have deleted `glWash` by arithmetic rather than by a decision — it keeps its own share of the reach and `washK × gain` is pinned to the 0.18 it was tuned at, **so move one and you must move the other**. ⚠ **AND 0.18 → 2.5 IS NOT A TYPO, WHICH THE FIRST CUT OF THIS CHANGE PROVED THE HARD WAY**: 0.55 reads as three times brighter and measured as **SIX PER CENT** of the light the wash had been laying on the city, because a gain is only ever meaningful against the area it is spread over — the feature was gone and the numbers looked like it was working. The row that ships was swept over wallR × focus × gain against the old wash and scored on the three things that separate a bloom from a tint: **area 0.31×, peak 1.56×, energy 0.34×, and zero pixels blown to white** — the last being the column that actually bounds it, since a pink sign has to lay PINK on its wall and every row above gain 2.5 at this reach starts whitening the middle of the pool. ⚠ **`glHdr` keeps a hot core from CLIPPING but that is not a licence** — the tonemap still has to bring it back, and a core that arrives desaturated arrives desaturated. ⚠ **And `__glLights()`'s own cab seat reads 0.0% for this and is not the instrument** — measure it as `gain: 0` versus the setting under test, never by toggling `RENDER_TUNE.glLights`, which runs `g.litW.clear()` and restarts every light at weight 0 under a frozen clock: same class as the `settleFade` bug one paragraph up, and it reads exactly like the feature being off.
⚠ **AND A SURFACE NOW ANSWERS THE LIGHT ACCORDING TO WHAT IT IS MADE OF (`RENDER_TUNE.glMat`, 0 puts the city back on one BRDF; `glBump` is the relief half).** Sixteen material families have decided what a wall LOOKS like for as long as `wallTex` has existed — coursed ashlar, riveted plate, board-and-batten with knots and nail bleed — and none of them has ever decided how it BEHAVES: brick, sheet copper, curtain glass and weathered timber were all shaded with one half-lambert key and two overlay tints. That is why metal has never read as metal here. **Metal IS view-dependence** — a reflection carrying the metal's own hue, a value that changes as you drive past — wood is the absence of it, and frost is it with the sharpness taken out; not one of the three is expressible as albedo at any texture resolution, and until now the fragment shader **had no eye position at all**. It has one (`eyePos` in camera.js, solved for the point `viewMatrix` sends to the origin, asserted by `gl:parity` at 972 cameras including under pitch) and a five-column table per family, authored in windshield.js beside the painters. ⚠ **THE FAMILY IS DERIVED AND NOTHING IS AUTHORED** — a palette key already resolved to a family through `wallMaterialOf`, so 284 keys and 8,223 mass faces over 173 models got a response with no content file touched; the index is resolved once per model beside `texKey`, never per vertex. ⚠ **AN INDEX OUT OF RANGE IS UNDEFINED BEHAVIOUR IN GLSL ES, NOT A CLAMP** — on this driver it reads zeros, and a zero row is gloss 0, which is `pow(x, 0.0)` = 1.0: a full mirror over the whole surface, on one machine, with nothing in any log. `npm run gl:mat` is the gate for that class and nothing else in the repo can see it (`gl:mesh` compares geometry, `gl:glsl` compares names; both pass with every building pointing at family 41). ⚠ **THE ENVIRONMENT COMES OFF THE REFLECTED RAY, NEVER OFF THE NORMAL**: every facade in this city is vertical, so a sky/ground blend taken off `n` is one flat colour on every building and reads as a tint somebody added — it is the reflection that swings, handing you the sky at a tower's foot and the ground at its top, and that gradient is what made the Solenne stop being a pale wash. ⚠ **AND A SPECULAR LOBE ON A FLAT BOX IS NOT A HIGHLIGHT.** Every face here is planar, so a lobe evaluated across one has a single normal and therefore a single value: it does not travel, it just paints that whole wall lighter. Tuned by eye at `(0.35 + 0.65·metal)` it took a cab frame to 23.7% of wall pixels moved and The Forge came back uniformly brighter — which is exactly what "the wall got lighter" looks like. At `(0.15 + 0.30·metal)` the same frame is **8.4%**, and what carries the material read is the RELIEF, which varies per texel. ⚠ **THE RELIEF HAS TO HAPPEN BEFORE THE KEY TERM, AND DID NOT.** It recovers a normal map from the albedo's own luminance gradient — no second atlas page, no painter changed — and it was first written inside the material block, perturbing a normal `lit` had already been computed from: it reached the reflection and the specular and **not the diffuse**, which is the only place a mortar joint or a plate lap shows. It measured 25.2% without it against 24.1% with it — correct, tunable, wired at both ends, and inside the noise. ⚠ **AND A TERM MUST BE MEASURED AGAINST THE FRAME IT IS ADDED TO**: that comparison was against the no-material frame, and relief both brightens and darkens, so some of the pixels it touches land back NEAR that baseline and the count goes DOWN while the picture changes. `__glMaterials()` now carries its own second baseline. Measured: **8.4% of wall pixels at a noon cab, 14.5% from the air, 0.3% at night**, relief **0.3% / 2.0%** (few pixels, mean 11.5 and worst 79 of 255 on them — joints, laps and rivet lines, which is where relief belongs), off-building **0.00–0.01%**, and a cost inside the run-to-run spread at every seat. ⚠ **IT IS A DAYLIGHT FEATURE AND THE NIGHT NUMBER IS ARITHMETIC RATHER THAN A BUG**: both ends of the environment gradient are the night sky and the sun's highlight goes with the sun, so after dark there is nothing for it to reflect. The neon could light it, and deliberately does not — the wall wash is diffuse-only by an explicit decision recorded in the shader. **Frosted glass is a material row, not a grab pass**, and that is a finding rather than a shortcut: every glazed surface in this city is an opaque SKIN over a solid box, so there is nothing behind a pane to see and nothing to refract, and the GL buffer a backdrop grab would read holds only what GL drew — no sky, which is still painted by the 2-D pass. What frost looks like from outside is a reflection with its lobe removed and an even glow in its place, which is a wide exponent beside a big sheen. ⚠ Its one home is **`ty_clone_vat`, MOVED OUT OF `PLATE_WALL`**, which is a fix and not a reclassification: the palette's own comment calls it "dark glowing vat glass" and it was being painted as riveted steel with laps and rivet lines.

⚠ **AND THE CITY HAS A MIRROR IN IT NOW (`CHROME_WALL`; `FROST_WALL` went from one key to three).** "Modern chrome" turned out to be a surface nobody could author: `ty_hf_chrome` and its neighbours are in `PLAIN_WALL`, whose own comment is exact about what they are — a seamless clad panel, flat fill, no coursing, *"the difference between chrome and pale stone"* — and that BRDF row is `metal: 0.00` with no environment term at all, so a building faced in it reads as pale render at any RGB. A mirror is not a bright surface, it is a surface showing you something else, so `matChrome` paints the WORLD the panel reflects: sky above, ground below, a hard dark line where they meet. ⚠ **The specular lobe cannot say that** — a lobe needs an eye and a texture has none — so the horizon goes in the albedo and `gl/context.js` puts the sun on top of it. ⚠ **It TILES, and that is the look rather than a compromise**: a mirrored curtain wall is a grid of panels each holding its own copy of the skyline, and the joints are what make the repeat read as panels instead of banding. ⚠ **Its `bump` is near zero for `lattice`'s reason and not because chrome is smooth** — the relief term reads the albedo's own gradient as geometry and this albedo's biggest gradient by far IS that horizon, so embossing it grows a physical ridge across the middle of every panel in the city. ⚠ **A NEW FAMILY RATHER THAN A RETUNE OF `PLAIN_WALL`**, which carries 40 keys — every soffit, painted kerb, kiosk flank, lighthouse shell and crane body in the game — so a horizon ramp and `metal: 0.88` there would turn every painted kerb in Coldwater into polished steel to get two buildings right. ⚠ And `MAT_ORDER` is **APPENDED**, per its own warning: the index rides in the vertex buffer, so a family inserted in the middle renumbers the city (19 → 20, against a shader array of 24). `FROST_WALL` grew for the mirror reason — it held exactly one key for as long as it existed, which made it a coolant tank rather than a material, and etched glass is what a nightclub's dance hall and a couture vitrine are lit through.

⚠ **AND ONE BUILDING PUTS A LIGHT INTO THE SKY (`skyBeam`, the twelfth authored adornment).** Every other adornment is a thing ON a building; this is a shaft leaving one, sweeping — what a destination club has instead of a bigger sign, since you can see it from the far side of the basin and cannot read it. ⚠ **IT IS `lightBeam`, AND ITS FIRST CUT WAS A STROKE — WHICH IS THE WHOLE LESSON.** A stroke has a length in the world and a width in SCREEN PIXELS, so a beam drawn as one is the same thickness from the next street and from ten tiles up, and the only way to make it fade is to cut it into pieces and step each one down. That is the exact reasoning `lightBeam`'s own header sets out for the beacon, and it applied here word for word — the defect was reported in one line, *"make sure voltage spotlights aren't just lines"*. A cone of light is a line of GLOWS whose radius grows along it, the widths are in TILES, and the sprite layer draws them additively and depth-tested, so the club's own drum cuts its beam per pixel instead of an all-or-nothing probe. **Nothing new was written for it**, which is the part worth keeping: the primitive had existed since the beacon was built, and reaching for a stroke was reaching past it. ⚠ **It is the lighthouse's beam at a third of the width** — the beacon leaves its lantern 0.31 of a tile across and opens to 1.36 over a 6.6-tile throw because it is a sea light; a rooftop searchlight is a smaller instrument pointed up. ⚠ **Its duty is 1, the only cycle in the motion layer that never parks** — everything else there is idle most of the time because what makes a crane read as machinery is that it stops, and a searchlight that stopped for twenty seconds reads as broken — and `RENDER_TUNE.motion` 0 still pins it at its home bearing, a beam standing still rather than a beam deleted. ⚠ **It is night-only, and `moving.mjs` had to be TOLD**: that sweep runs at `night: 0` because machinery works in daylight, so a night-only part measured there is one pose and reads exactly like a part that never moved; `AT_NIGHT` is a per-entry exception rather than a change to all of them, and both halves are mutation-tested (a beam that does not sweep, and a beam that ignores the flag). ⚠ **And it starts OUTSIDE its own lamp**, which is the beacon's note and a depth-buffer fact rather than a nicety: a node rooted ON the deck is half inside real geometry, and the buffer takes that half — a beam coming out of a dark notch the shape of its own housing. ⚠ **A SPRITE CAN DECLARE ITSELF A MOVING PART NOW (`pushLight`'s `tag`), AND THAT IS WHAT THE REWRITE COST.** `moving.mjs` hashed `canvasResidue`'s default return, which is COUNTS — and every entry passed by luck, because a slewing crane's own backface culling changes how many quads survive. A `lightBeam` cone's node count is a property of the CONE, the same number at every bearing, so the beams swept perfectly and the gate reported ONE POSE: the exact false negative that file's own header is written about, pointed at the newest thing in the layer. The digest hashes POSITIONS now, and ⚠ **only of parts carrying `MOTION_TAG`** — a building drifts smoke, strikes an arc, breathes a vat and pulses a beacon on clocks that are not this flag, and hashing every position in the frame reports all four of those as never parking.

⚠ **AND A SIGN CAN SAY WHAT THE BUILDING SELLS (`$trade`), WHICH IS `$name`'s SIBLING AND WAS MISSING.** `SIGN_HANZI` and `SIGN_WORD` have held the trade vocabulary since the derived blade shipped — 夜總會 for a nightclub, 時裝 for a couture house — and an authored model had no way to reach it, so both of the rebuilds below put the building's NAME on two fittings at once. That is the density rule `SIGN_WORD`'s own header is written about: *"what makes those streets read is not the number, it is that the signs say DIFFERENT THINGS"*, and three boards repeating CASH & CARRION look worse than one. ⚠ **The HAND picks the language** — a `hanzi` font gets the Chinese word and anything else the English one — so the lettering a part asks for and the vocabulary it draws from cannot be set to disagree. ⚠ **And `$trade` is NOT `$name`'s twin for caching**: `$name` varies per TILE and therefore cannot be captured into a per-model mesh, which is what `perTile` guards at all three call sites; a trade word is a property of the MODEL and captures like any other authored string. ⚠ `atelier` was in neither table (`boutique` was, and they are the same trade), so the one blade that most needed this resolved to an empty string — and a sign with nothing to say is not drawn, which is the correct failure and a silent one.

⚠ **AND THE TWO BUILDINGS THAT ASKED FOR ALL OF IT ARE VOLTAGE AND AURELIA, REBUILT 2026-09-20.** The report was *"weak overall, and the sign is hidden behind stuff"*, and the second half was arithmetic: Aurelia's name band sat at z 0.093 with a canopy at 0.12 reaching 0.105 out over it and a glazed bay spanning 0.018 to 0.108, so the band was behind both. On the painter a marquee sorts forward of its own wall and drew anyway; on a depth buffer it is simply **not there**, which is a shopfront with no name on it — `sign:stand` and `signband.mjs` are the two gates for exactly that. Four traps came out of the rebuild and each one is silent when wrong. ⚠ **A `bladePanel` on a DRUM is mounted on nothing**: `anchored.mjs` only reads box faces, and it is right to — a flat slab hung off a cylinder stands in air at every point but one — so Voltage's vertical name got a real sign pylon to be bolted to, which is what a club sign that size has anyway. ⚠ **A flank part's `cy` is the MASS HALF-WIDTH and not a guess**: both models put their pilaster ranks at a plausible ±0.36 while the boxes are OFFSET in `cx`, so one rank was buried 0.07 inside its wall and the other floated 0.03 off it — and the buried one stopped the derived kit's `paintFree` from noticing that wall was spoken for, which put a gable-end advertisement straight through the fins. ⚠ **A building that already has its name up wants a TRADE WORD on its second fitting, not the name again** — see `$trade` above; both of these shipped saying it twice, and neither reads as wrong in a screenshot. ⚠ **And a `marqueeBand` mounts at `cy + half·0.94`, never at its own `cy`** — it pushes itself out along the entrance normal, so the authored number is the REMAINDER of the wall face, and authoring the wall plane into it double-counts the offset. That is the floating-sign bug `anchored.mjs` was written for, found on this same building.

⚠ **AND IT SNOWS ON THE GROUND NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glSnow`, 0 is the renderer as it shipped; `snowForce` is the bench seam).** Snow has been a first-class weather state on the server since long before this renderer existed — `WEATHER_TYPES` carries `snow` and `blizzard`, `currentPrecip` is three words wide, and it is chosen by TEMPERATURE as well as by the roll, so any rain below 1°C falls as snow — and the client has drawn the FLAKES the whole time, in the weather-FX overlay and in the scene. What had never happened is any of it LANDING: the ground stayed the colour it is in July through a whiteout blizzard. ⚠ **IT COSTS NOTHING TO COLLECT BECAUSE IT IS THE ELSE BRANCH OF A CARVE-OUT THAT WAS ALREADY THERE.** `WET_NOW` deliberately refuses snow ("it settles rather than sheets, and a snowy road that mirrored the neon would read as ice"), so the sample is already taken, already classified, and already has an empty else branch. This is that branch: no new packet, no new authored field, and the wet road untouched. ⚠ **AND THAT CARVE-OUT WAS ASKED TWICE AND COULD ANSWER ITSELF TWO DIFFERENT WAYS (2026-09-19).** The sentence above is the whole design and it shipped as two independent expressions: the wet branch took the headline word OR'd with a cell test that refused snow, the snow branch took the headline word OR'd with a cell test that demanded it, and **both headline terms were blind to the cell actually overhead** — while `drawWeather`, which decides what the player sees FALLING, lets a local cell override the headline. So a snow HEADLINE with a rain CELL over you drew rain streaks, wet the road AND deepened the snow at full rate, all three at once. Reported as *"can puddles not gather on grass so much"*, which is what a field of lying snow looks like when nobody is expecting one — and settling that took a measurement rather than a reading of the screenshot, because **puddles cannot reach grass at all**: `pud` is gated `vRoad > 0.5`, and open ground is the FLOOR, which has no puddle term in it. ⚠ **AND RAIN DID NOT MELT SNOW, AT ALL** — `SNOW_THAW_S` is the clear-sky thaw and there was nothing else, so a downpour left the ground white for the full seven minutes. The classification, the four time constants and the melt are one file now ([client/shared/ground-accum.js](client/shared/ground-accum.js)), which `drawWeather` also reads for its own snow/rain split, because **the SERVER integrates the same arithmetic**: ⚠ **A CLIENT THAT HAS JUST OPENED ITS EYES IS HANDED THE GROUND** (`sky.ground` → `wxGround`). All three quantities integrated forward from ZERO on every page load, so a player logging in ten minutes into a blizzard stood on bare summer grass beside somebody standing in snow, and the two did not converge until it thawed — the puddles take a minute to arrive and the snow the better part of ten. `groundAccum()` in environment.js runs the identical step off the day's own precipitation and ships it with the sky. ⚠ **LAZY, NOT A TICK** — a base plus a timestamp, the idiom bionic heat and NPC relations already use, advanced at `setCurrentPrecip` so every stretch it integrates is one constant rate. ⚠ **AND THE STEPS ARE EXACT RATHER THAN EULER**, which is what lets one 30-minute step and a hundred thousand 16 ms steps land on the same number; written `P += dt·(a − P/τ)` the seed would be a few per cent adrift every time the sky changed. ⚠ **SEED, NEVER RE-SEAT**: the server's figure is the day's headline and the renderer's is refined by the cell you are standing under, so adopting it every push would drag a pilot inside a rain cell back to the global answer four times a second — and once is enough, because a reservoir forgets its initial condition exponentially. ⚠ **RAM only**, its durable residue being the weather itself; a restart takes the ground back to dry and bare. ⚠ And the threading is **eight hand-offs across four view files** plus the server end, a missed one being a seat that silently starts bare for ever — so `gl:snow` pairs every `wxField:` in those files with a `wxGround:` beside it, and all seven mutants of the above are caught. ⚠ **IT IS AN ACCUMULATION AND NOT A FILM, WHICH IS THE WHOLE REASON IT IS NOT ANOTHER READING OF `uWet`** — a road is wet a second into a shower and dry a minute after, whereas snow builds while it falls and lies long after the sky clears, so it is the linear reservoir `POND_NOW` already uses with a much longer thaw (`SNOW_LIE_S` 120, `SNOW_THAW_S` 420; the ratio is the level a sustained fall settles at and the thaw is how long the city keeps it). Driven off the live precip the world would go back to summer the moment a cell drifted past. ⚠ **THREE SHADERS, BECAUSE EACH DRAWS GROUND THE OTHER TWO CANNOT.** The floor takes the open terrain, the ground pass takes the streets, and the mass takes every up-facing surface in the city. That split is not a choice — `GROUND_FULL` paints every road and pavement tile as an opaque quad ON TOP of the floor, so a city snow painted into the floor is covered by exactly the surface it belongs on; it is the same forcing the wet reflections hit, recorded in the same words on `uWet`, and this was built with it already known. ⚠ **THE FLOOR BURIES THE MATERIAL AND KEEPS THE LANDFORM.** `tex` at that point is the material multiplier with the hillshade already folded in, so mixing it toward 1.0 — the obvious way to write "cover it up" — deletes the relief with the texture and hands back a flat white sheet with no shape in it. Mixed toward `shadeW` instead, the cracked clay, the wind ripple and the turf grain go under and the hills stay, which on a real snowfield is the only thing you can see. ⚠ **THE STREET IS THE DRAINAGE MODEL READ THE OTHER WAY UP.** The puddle field derives three terms from one variable — the distance across the carriageway — and asks where water COLLECTS: the gutter, the wheel ruts, the sag between gullies. Snow answers the opposite question over the identical geometry, because the thing that makes a rut hold water is the thing that scours it clear of snow: it is where the wheels go. So the wheel tracks are the BARE part and the kerb line is the BANKED part, and `vLat`/`vKerb` have carried both answers all along — no new attribute, no new authored field. ⚠ **`vKerb` 0 IS ONE ANSWER FOR WATER AND TWO FOR SNOW, AND THAT COST A VISIBLE BUG.** A junction, an apron, a forecourt and a depot hardstand all have no camber, so all four fall back to the isotropic field for the water. For snow they split on `vRoad` instead: a junction is the most heavily trafficked carriageway on the street and a yard is not trafficked at all. Read off the kerb alone, every crossroads in the city held a full cover while the streets feeding it were ploughed black — which is exactly what it looked like on Marrow Street, a white band lying across the road at the two tiles the snapshot calls `nsw` and `nes`. ⚠ **AND A FLAT SCOUR TERM UNDER THE RUTS DELETES THE EFFECT.** It was `clamp(rut * 0.85 + 0.30)`, and `rut` is a pair of Gaussians that only falls to ~0.04 at a lane EDGE and sits at ~0.47 half way between the tracks — so the constant under it left the whole travelled way between 0.00 and 0.30 of a cover, which at a real depth is a road with no snow on it at all. The Gaussians say two dark wheel tracks with white between them on their own; nothing needed adding. ⚠ **THE MASS TAKES `n0`, NEVER `n`.** Snow is placed by gravity against the real surface, and `n` is that surface with the albedo-recovered relief and the shading bevel folded into it — so taken off `n`, a brick wall collects snow in its mortar joints and every chamfered corner in the city grows a white line down it. Same distinction, for the same reason, that the shadow bias one block up is written around. ⚠ **AND THE PITCH A SURFACE WILL HOLD RISES WITH THE DEPTH**, which is one expression and is most of what makes it read as weather: a dusting sits only on what is dead flat, and it takes a real fall before a pitched roof or a canted parapet holds any. Written as a fixed threshold, every roof in Coldwater turns white together at the same moment, which is the one thing snow visibly does not do. ⚠ **THE ALBEDO IS SUBSTITUTED BEFORE THE SHADING, WHICH IS WHY THE MASS CHANGE IS THREE LINES.** Everything below composes a lit surface out of `surf` — two overlays, the sun shadow, the chamfer, both occlusion terms, the screen-space pass, then the point lights — so snow is darker in shadow, darker down an alley and lit by the neon bolted above it with not one of those terms knowing it exists. Mixed in at the end it would be a flat white decal over all of them. ⚠ **AND THE ANTIALIAS BAND NEEDS A CAP AS WELL AS A FLOOR, WHICH THE PUDDLE FIELD DOES NOT.** The floor is the puddle shader own trick, because a threshold on a world-space field crawls as the camera moves. The cap is because this field covers the whole street rather than a few hollows in it: at the far end of a road one pixel spans several tiles, `fwidth` goes past the width of the field itself, and the smoothstep stops being an edge and becomes a ramp across the entire road — which reads as fog lying on the tarmac. Measured in the Modelshop against a pinned clock, the depth ramp is monotonic and smooth — **10.4% of the frame moved at a dusting, 55.6% at half, 85.7% at a full fall**, against a control of 0.1% for the same config rendered twice. ⚠ **AND NO HEADLESS GATE CAN SEE THE PICTURE**, because every harness here installs a GL hook that returns a bare canvas and never reaches a draw call: `npm run gl:snow` ([scripts/shapes/snow.mjs](scripts/shapes/snow.mjs), in the push chain and in `shapes:smoke`) asserts the three things that are silent instead — the accumulation classifies snow and rain the right way round and thaws rather than switching off, both ground layers write `uSnow` on EVERY frame (a uniform holds its last value, so a layer that set it only when it had snow would leave the world white for the rest of the session after one blizzard), and world.js hands the depth to all three consumers. ⚠ Its world.js check is **located, never counted** — a first cut asserted the hand-off string appeared at least twice, there are three of them, and deleting any one left two and stayed green. Mutation-tested 7 of 7. ⚠ **AND WHEELS LEAVE TRACKS IN IT (`RENDER_TUNE.glTracks`, 0 is the snow exactly as it lies).** The cover is computed per fragment from world position alone, which is what makes it free and is exactly why it could not have tracks in it: **nothing in the renderer remembered where anything had been**. The store in windshield.js is that memory and it is the only stateful thing in the whole feature. ⚠ **OTHER PEOPLE'S TRACKS NEEDED NO SERVER STATE, WHICH IS THE FINDING.** The first answer given here was that shared tracks meant a write per move on a hot path, and it was wrong: `airContact` has always sent a stable id, a SUB-TILE FLOAT position and an `onGround` flag to every pilot within `CONTACT_RANGE`, at the mover's own sync cadence, and the payload's own comment says the fields are there "for client-side dead-reckoning between relays". Trucks are on the same wire through the `vehicle.contacts` gather hook. So a track is a DERIVATION of what the cockpit is already being sent — nothing is stored, nothing is written, and no packet grew. ⚠ **VELOCITY IS DERIVED FROM SUCCESSIVE POSITIONS AND NEVER FROM `ias`.** The payload offers a speed for exactly this, and the two producers fill it in DIFFERENT UNITS — `airContact` sends knots off `cont.airspeed`, `truckContactsNear` sends mph off `rig.speed` — with nothing on the wire saying which. A point is dropped once the source has MOVED far enough, which needs no units and cannot be wrong about them. ⚠ **ONE ARRAY OF POINTS, NOT OF SEGMENTS, AND THAT IS WHAT PAYS FOR THE FADE.** A segment list is a vec4 of two endpoints with nowhere left to put the burial, so the fade would need a second array and twice the uniform vectors; a polyline carries n-1 segments in n vec4s and has a spare component per point, which the run-break flag then rides for nothing. ⚠ **ONE DISTANCE, TWO WHEELS** — the perpendicular distance to the centreline is all that is needed, since a pair of marks either side of it is `abs(d - half)` against the width, so nothing stores, uploads or walks a second polyline. ⚠ **AND THE GAUGE IS THE RIG'S OWN LIFTER STATIONS, NEVER A NUMBER — THE METRIC DERIVATION IS THE TRAP.** It was two constants, 0.16 and 0.032 tiles, worked out the obvious way: a lane is `LANE_W` across, so a tile is about seven metres and a two-and-a-half-metre axle is 0.18 of one. Every step of that is correct and the answer is **five times too big**, because a vehicle in GLASS is not drawn at its metric size — a truck is `CONTACT_SIZE.truck × ownExtMul`, which is **0.063 of a tile across the cab**, half a metre by the lane's own scale. So a metrically honest wheel track ran a full truck's width clear of the truck on either side, which is what *"far wide outside of the truck body entirely"* was. A mark has to agree with the DRAWN rig, because the rig is the thing standing on it: `podGlow` publishes where every lifter is and (now) how wide it is, and `SZ` is the same expression `drawHeadlightBeam` scales a lamp station by, so this is the derivation the beam already makes one axis over and a wider truck's tracks move outboard on their own — measured, half 0.0321 tiles against a cab half-width of 0.0315. ⚠ **ONE GAUGE IN THE UNIFORM, AND IT IS THE NEAREST RUN'S**, because the shader takes a single half-track for the whole buffer and `near` is already sorted; between two trucks the difference is a quarter of a lifter's width. ⚠ **AND IT IS THE PERPENDICULAR TO THE LINE, NEVER THE DISTANCE TO THE SEGMENT, WHICH IS THE ONE THAT SHIPPED AND THE ONE THAT WAS REPORTED.** Reported as *"circles in the middle from straight driving"*, and it is a property of the distance function rather than of the data: `abs(d - half)` over a CAPSULE distance is an ANNULUS, so each segment draws its two rails and wraps a semicircular arc of radius `half` round each end — and consecutive segments share a vertex, so the arc off the end of one and the arc off the start of the next close into a **full circle at every point in the path**, tangent to both rails, 0.32 tiles across, one every `TRACK_STEP`, for ever. The rasterised straight-line case is the whole diagnosis in six lines of ASCII and took a minute; the shape was read off the screenshot as a loop the truck had driven, which it never was. Measuring to the segment's own LINE with `t` unclamped and cutting it off square draws the rails and nothing else. ⚠ **THE SQUARE END IS MITRED ONLY WHERE THE RUN CARRIES ON** — a butt cap on every segment leaves a wedge on the OUTSIDE of every bend, `half * tan(half the turn)` long, and at a true run end (the head under the wheels, the tail falling out of the buffer, either side of a relay gap) there is nothing to mitre INTO. ⚠ **AND THE LONGITUDINAL FEATHER IS FOR A RUN END AND NOWHERE ELSE**, because `fp` is most of a segment long at the far end of a street and a screen-width ramp at every joint puts a grey dot at every point in the path rather than at none. ⚠ **THE LAST POINT IS PROVISIONAL AND FOLLOWS THE WHEELS EVERY FRAME.** A point was only kept once the source had moved `TRACK_STEP`, so the drawn path ended up to half a tile BEHIND the truck and then grew by half a tile at a stroke — reported as *"they appear in big chunks so it doesn't feel like the truck is dragging them into the snow"*, which is exactly what a polyline whose head only moves six times a second looks like. The head is dragged and committed only when it is far enough from the point BEHIND it, so the stored cadence is unchanged and only the last segment moves. ⚠ **AND A BEND COMMITS EARLY, WHICH IS THE OTHER HALF OF THE SAME RULE.** A point every `TRACK_STEP` is right for a straight and draws a corner in three straight lines — the mitre note above works out that a rig takes a junction in about 25° a point at that spacing, and a pair of rails kinking through 25° at a time is what *"tracks also have to smoothly turn"* was. The head is also committed once the run has TURNED more than `TRACK_TURN` since the last point, which costs **exactly nothing on a straight** because the angle is zero there, and puts eleven points round a right-angle (measured: a 90° bend went from ~25° kinks to 8.0°). ⚠ `TRACK_CREEP` is the floor under it and is not optional: without a minimum spacing a rig shuffling on the spot swings its heading through a large angle over no distance and fills the whole buffer with points inside one lifter's width. ⚠ **AND THE CUT IS TOTAL.** It stopped at 0.88, and an eighth of the cover left lying in the rut is not a wheel track — it is a smear of the same grey the rest of the street is wearing. What a tyre does is take the snow OFF, so the fragment falls all the way back through the mix and hands back the road that is actually there: its own palette, its wetness, the water standing in it and the light landing on it. ⚠ **A TRACK IS BURIED BY WHAT FALLS AFTER IT, AND BY THE WIND IN ITS OWN TIME** — each point stamps how much had fallen when it was cut and when. ⚠ **AND IT IS THE FALL, NEVER THE DEPTH, WHICH IS A BUG THE OBVIOUS READING WALKS STRAIGHT INTO.** It was `depth - laid`: the cover now against the cover then — and `SNOW_NOW` is a RESERVOIR, so it settles at `SNOW_LIE_S` over `SNOW_THAW_S` within a couple of minutes and then stops moving however long it goes on snowing. The single condition under which a rut has to fill in — it is snowing, hard, now — was the single condition under which nothing ever buried one. `SNOW_FELL` is the inflow alone, integrated and never reduced, so a clear night still buries nothing and a heavy fall fills a rut in about a minute (`TRACK_BURY × SNOW_LIE_S` by construction; the old 0.13 carried over would have been sixteen seconds, which is a rig watching its own tracks go in the mirror). The age term is the rest of it, and it is also what stops a session accumulating invisible scars on every road it has driven since the last thaw. ⚠ A buried point leaves the store oldest-first, which it can do because `trackFill` only ever rises. The store is EMPTY whenever there is no snow, so this costs nothing for most of the year. ⚠ **A JUMP BREAKS THE RUN AND KEEPS THE POINT.** A contact leaving and re-entering range, a respawn, a relay gap — joined up, the two positions become one straight gouge through the snow between them. Skipping the jumped point as well loses the FIRST point of every new run, which over a session is most of what a visitor ever laid. ⚠ **AND THE THREE ABOVE ARE GATED, EACH BY THE ONE THING A HEADLESS RUN CAN SEE.** No harness here reaches a draw call, so `gl:snow` asserts that the gauge MOVES WITH THE RIG (a continental and a scrapper must not report one number, whatever its value, and each one's spacing-to-width ratio must be its own model's), that the same arc length costs MORE points round a bend than in a straight line with the worst kink under 15°, and that fifteen seconds of snow buries a track further than fifteen seconds of clear — with `snowForce` PINNED either side of that last one, or a burial keyed on the depth measures 0 in both runs and the two are indistinguishable. Mutation-tested 3 of 3. ⚠ **AND THE MASS PASS IS DELIBERATELY GIVEN NONE** — nothing drives on a roof, and a path walked per fragment over every wall in the city is a real cost for a picture that cannot exist; the gate asserts its absence. Bounded by a rectangle reject before the loop, because thirty-nine segments a fragment is far more than the six the wet reflections run and almost none of the frame is near a track. ⚠ **WHAT IT CANNOT BE IS A TRACKING MECHANIC.** `CONTACT_RANGE` is 12 tiles, so you only ever record the tracks of somebody already close enough to appear as a contact — a trail can never lead you somewhere you could not already see, and each client builds its own set from what it happened to witness, so two players disagree. It says *this junction gets traffic*, never *he went that way*. ⚠ And the tracks you will mostly see are **your own**: NPC traffic is not in the contact feed, and only `seat === 'pilot'` receives one.

⚠ **AND THE MASS IS BANDED (`RENDER_TUNE.glCourse`, 0 removes both bands and leaves the roof coping).**
`models:quality`'s worst-first board is dominated by ONE of its four scores — **more than one wall
palette**, failed by 22 of 173, with 26 more that are two mass segments or fewer, a box and a lid —
and Voltage's authoring note already names the fix: *"the crown takes a different palette so the
silhouette carries a light value instead of being one tone top to bottom"*. The mass belongs to the
arms; what a detail pass can do instead is BAND it, which is how every real building of any period
breaks its own height. `parapet` already draws exactly that and the kit was only ever using it at the
roof edge, so a **plinth** at the pavement and a **crown course** a storey below the top needed no
new kind, no painter and no schema change. Measured against the same street with the flag off:
**2.82% of drawn pixels move at night and 3.60% by day** for +4.8% faces — a better ratio than the
two rich-kit phases, which moved 0.5–2% each for +35%. ⚠ **The crown asks `wants('cope')` and the
plinth does not**: both are the same KIND so the section table cannot tell them apart, but nobody
hand-draws a base course and plenty hand-draw a cornice, and gating a plinth on whether somebody drew
a roof edge is the `vent`-under-`wall` error again. ⚠ **A band is SQUARE-ONLY** — `parapet` takes one
half-width for all four sides — which is barely a restriction (163 of 172 models have a square base)
and is exactly the thing that could silently take the feature to nothing, so the gate watches it.
⚠ **AND THE FIRST REACH FLOOR COULD NOT FAIL**: a plinth, a crown and the coping are all `parapet`,
so "has two" was already true of most models and deleting the whole section left 118 still passing.
What is unique to a plinth is WHERE it is, so the gate counts a band at z ≈ 0 — 98 with the section,
0 without. ⚠ **And the board that found the gap cannot score the fix**: that row counts MASS palettes
and banding is trim, so it still reads 151 of 173. The metric is a proxy for "does the silhouette
carry more than one value"; widening it to count trim would make it score "has any trim" twice. Every bench here pins `performance.now` so a drifting sky is not mistaken for a finding; the light set then became a function of ELAPSED TIME, so under a clock that never advances no light ever ramps into its slot and `glLastFrame().lit` is 0 for ever. A feature that is correctly wired and switched on looks exactly like one that is off. `settleFade` in glbench.js is the fix and it has to run AFTER the setting under test is applied, not once at the top. ⚠ **And the control that says so is the DAY seat**, which has no lights at all and must therefore be free: an early cut of that harness reported the lights costing 2.5 ms there, purely because it measured off-then-on in a fixed order and took the larger of each pair.

⚠ **AND A SIGN STANDS ON THE WALL RATHER THAN IN IT (`RENDER_TUNE.signStand`, 0 puts every marquee back on its own half-width).** Reported as *"legible signs not buried in the building"*, and it is one expression: `marqueeBand` pushed itself out along the entrance normal by `half * 0.94` — its OWN WIDTH — so the width of a sign decided how far forward it stood, and a band narrower than the facade it is bolted to came to rest INSIDE the building. On the painter that was survivable, because the sign sorted forward of the mass and drew anyway; on a depth buffer the only part of it that reaches the screen is whatever crests the parapet, which reads as a name sheared in half or as a shopfront with no sign on it at all. ⚠ **IT HAD BEEN DIAGNOSED AND FIXED TWICE, ONE ARM AT A TIME**, by nudging that `half` up until the sign happened to clear (`helpings` to `fh * 1.07`, `tine` to `WALL * 1.09`) — and both of those notes say in so many words that the COUPLING is the bug. There are twenty-six callers, and `npm run sign:stand` ([scripts/shapes/signstand.mjs](scripts/shapes/signstand.mjs)) put **27 of the city’s 56 name bands behind their own front wall**, the worst (`type:comic_shop`) by 0.282 of a tile — well over half a building in front of its own name. 0 now, with 53 of the 56 pushed out to the wall. ⚠ **THE MASS IS ASKED, NEVER GUESSED.** A constant (`fh * 1.0`, the tile edge) clears every facade in the city and floats in front of the set-back ones — a podium, a forecourt, a recessed shopfront — which is the same defect in the opposite costume; `wallFaceAt` is the building’s own captured front plane over the band’s own z range, so a sign on a set-back wall stays on it. ⚠ **AND IT IS A FLOOR, NOT A REPLACEMENT** (`max`), so the two arms already fixed by hand do not move. ⚠ **THE STAND-OFF IS SOLVED AT THE FITTED BAND, NOT AT THE CALLER’S `wz`** — `marqueeSpan` may move a band to clear an awning or crown a parapet, and a stand-off solved against the pre-fit height answers for a wall the sign is no longer in front of. ⚠ **THE TWO NUMBERS ARE RECORDED BY THE FUNCTION THAT RESOLVES THEM**, because by the time the band is a quad they have been collapsed into one offset and a gate that re-derived them would be a second opinion about the placement rule — `glresidue`’s own lesson.

⚠ **AND A NAME NEEDS A CLEAR BAND OF WALL, WHICH IS A THIRD QUESTION AND NOT EITHER OF THE FIRST TWO.** `sign:stand` asks how far OUT a board stands and `sign:fit` asks whether a wire is drawn across one; this asks whether the band of facade it is painted on belongs to it. Reported as *"adequate logo floats ontop of windows and disappears"*, which is both halves of one defect: **68% of that board's height sat inside the shopfront glazing band and 15% more was behind the canopy, leaving 4% of the name on clear wall.** ⚠ **BEHIND THE GLASS IS THE HALF NOBODY GUESSES** — a `windowBay`'s glazing is drawn at `faceY(cy) + FACE_EPS` and a `signBoard` at `faceY(cy)`, so an overlapping board is behind a near-opaque pane AND exactly coplanar with the window's surround plate, which is a depth-buffer tie decided per pixel; on the painter the later quad simply won and the sign drew, which is why this is new. ⚠ **AND A CANOPY REACHES `out` BEYOND ITS OWN MOUNTING PLANE** (0.105 of a tile is the ordinary shopfront number), so a name inside the canopy's own height is not visible from the street whatever the depth test does. ⚠ **THE Z SEMANTICS ARE NOT SHARED BETWEEN KINDS, AND READING THEM WRONG INVENTS AND HIDES FINDINGS IN EQUAL MEASURE**: `signBoard` and `windowBay` take `z` as a CENTRE while `canopy` and `parapet` take it as a BOTTOM, a window's surround is `hh + fr` tall rather than `hh`, and `neonRun`'s `drop` hangs a return at each END rather than a curtain across the run — a first cut that treated every part as `z ± hh` reported adequate's canopy at 31% when it is 15% and missed the glazing altogether, then flagged a corrected board as buried under a tube it sits clear of. ⚠ **AND WHICH FIX APPLIES IS DECIDED BY HOW FAR THE OFFENDING PART REACHES**: a few hundredths past the board means it is merely SUNK, and raising its `cy` stands it proud of the glass with the author's placement untouched — **nine boards over eight models took that** — while a canopy's 0.1 of a tile means the name has to move to the clear band above the deck, because standing it proud there hangs it off the front of the building. `node scripts/shapes/signband.mjs` is the gate (in `shapes:smoke` AND in the `pretest:regress` chain — two lists, per the warning above). ⚠ Its `PINNED` map is a PIN, not a budget: it fails if a recorded share grows, and it fails if one stops matching, so fixing a pinned model forces deleting its pin. **Four low shops are pinned** — their podium is glazing, a full-width canopy and a parapet with a 0.013-tile gap in it, and whether the right answer is to shrink the name, stand it off the building or move it upstairs is a question for somebody at the Modelshop rather than one to solve in arithmetic.

⚠ **AND A SIGN'S SHAPE IS A PROPERTY OF THE BUILDING, NOT OF WHERE YOU ARE STANDING (`RENDER_TUNE.signSquare`, 0 puts the per-corner pull back).** Reported as *"a lot of signage has a weird way of moving around when moving camera"*, and it is the one class of rendering bug here that no gate could see: the sign is built, fitted, uploaded and drawn every frame, on the right wall, in the right colours — what is wrong is its SHAPE, by a few per cent, and only from some angles. `cam.unproj(p, pull)` spends a stand-off as `f - pull`, the camera's FORWARD coordinate, which scales a corner toward the eye by `1 − pull/f` — and the four corners of a sign seen from anywhere but dead-on have four different `f`. So a rectangle painted on a flat wall arrives at the GPU as a PARALLELOGRAM leaning by an amount that is a function of the camera, and `fitSignPts` then forces the aspect back to the texture's, which turns that lean into the whole sign GROWING AND SHRINKING as you drive past. Measured over the registry: **84 of 354 signs out of square, worst 7.6°, and 88 of them changing width by more than 1% across a 40° swing, worst 8.2%** — worst screen error 58 px. ⚠ **AND THE TWO LAYERS NEEDED TWO DIFFERENT ANSWERS, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** Lettering (`emitSurfaceText`) unprojects EXACTLY — pull 0 — and the stand-off is applied to the finished quad along that quad's OWN NORMAL (`standOffQuad`), which is a rigid translation: same rectangle, camera-free, planar, and it clears MORE at a grazing angle rather than collapsing toward zero exactly where lettering z-fights worst. That is what `faceY` has always done for a bolted-on part and what `FACE_EPS` is named after. Boards, blades and panels (`emitDecoQuad`/`emitDecoFill`) **cannot take that answer** — `BLADE_PROUD` is 0.25 of a tile and exists to win a depth test WITHOUT moving the artwork, because a blade is authored inside its own facade, and standing it off physically would move every bar, club and casino sign in the city. So `unprojQuad` pulls every corner by the same FRACTION of its own depth instead: a homothety about the eye, which leaves all four corners on exactly the pixels they were on (**measured: 0 of 2,148 board/blade/panel quads move, worst 2e-13 px**) and cannot lean. ⚠ **`emitWire` still pulls per endpoint** and is left alone: a stroke's projection is preserved either way, so only its depth varies. ⚠ **The knock-on was in a GATE rather than in the picture** — `signfit`'s depth test had no tolerance, so a fitting bolted flush to the fascia a sign is painted on (0.005 of a tile, less than `FACE_EPS`) was reported as a wire drawn across it the moment the lettering stopped leaning; `COPLANAR` is 0.02 and the five real crossings are 0.2 of a tile and survive it. `npm run` has no name for the gate: it is `node scripts/shapes/signswim.mjs`, in the push chain and in `shapes:smoke`, and it asks the two questions separately — lettering must be SQUARE, everything must hold its shape STILL as the head turns past it. Mutation-tested at **264 findings with the flag at 0 and 0 with it at 1**.

⚠ **AND THE CITY WRITES ON ITSELF IN FIVE HANDS NOW, NONE OF WHICH IS A TYPEFACE.** `bakeTagText` drew a THROW-UP and nothing else, so every piece of paint in Coldwater was the same piece of paint in a different colour. The question that gets asked here is whether this needs fonts, and the answer is no: there are no font files in this repo and no build step to ship one with, and `TAG_FONT`'s own note already settles it for the throw-up — what makes a letterform read as spray is the heavy round-joined OUTLINE stroked round it, not the face underneath. A hand is a RECIPE OF PASSES. Beside the piece there are now a **handstyle** (one ink, one lean for the whole word, a tail thrown off the last letter and back under it — ⚠ the tail goes on FIRST or it is a line ruled across the word), a **stencil** (flat opaque fill, soft overspray, speckle, and BRIDGES punched with `destination-out` so they take the overspray with them — a bridge drawn in the wall's colour would be a coloured bar across the letter, and nothing in a bake knows what colour the wall is), a **roller** (two flat tones offset, no outline, no drips, nap streaks, one dry end), and a **buff** — the city painting OVER, which is the one that is not graffiti at all: a ragged-edged patch of municipal colour at **alpha 0.87 so the GHOST of the piece under it still reads**, because at 1 the ghost was drawn, covered and never seen by anybody. ⚠ **THE ASPECT IS HALF THE PLACEMENT AND COSTS NOTHING** — `fitSignPts` only ever takes LESS of a quad, so a roller baked three times as wide as it is tall occupies a wide band of its reserved patch with no placement code knowing it exists; `TAG_HAND_BAND` is the other half, and it is what makes the hands more than five skins on one shape: a blockbuster goes UP the wall because a pole is why anybody bothered, a handstyle goes low because that is arm's length. Kit tags went from one band to **z 0.010–1.076**. ⚠ **THE HAND RIDES THE VARIANT THAT WAS ALREADY ROLLED**, never a fifth roll — a new roll shifts the stream and moves every tag in the city to fix nothing. ⚠ **PER-LETTER RUNS FORCE THE THROW-UP**, the same consent rule the silver scheme follows: a player who picked four colours letter by letter has SAID what the letters are, and every other hand is one ink. ⚠ **AND THE ROLLER AND THE BUFF ARE KIT-ONLY** — both are deliberately hard to read, which is right for texture on a wall nobody wrote and wrong for a sentence another player is meant to read; a player's own paint gains a hand and **keeps its patch**, because `tagPatch` solves for legible clear brick and a small low handstyle would shrink somebody's words for a reason they never asked for. ⚠ **`__tagSheet()` lays out ONE ROW PER HAND** now and used to lay out one per colour scheme — both come off the same `variant`, so a sheet by scheme visits whichever hands that arithmetic lands on and can silently show none of one, which is exactly how a hand that bakes nothing gets signed off. ⚠ The known limit is that **the kit sizes a tag to the bare gap it finds**, so most kit rollers come out 0.04 tiles wide: the blockbuster's width is wall-limited rather than hand-limited, and widening it means changing what the kit calls a paintable gap.

⚠ **AND THE GLASSHOUSE HAD NO GLASS IN IT, WHICH IS A PALETTE FINDING AND A GEOMETRY ONE (`RENDER_TUNE.hfTint`, 0 puts the quarter back on one hue byte-for-byte).** Reported from the air as *"what's with these buildings? and our glasshouse style?"*, and the quarter is Halcyon Fields — 69 building tiles over thirteen silhouettes. **Every one of them faces its mass in `PLAIN_WALL` keys** (`ty_hf_chrome` [186,198,208], `ty_hf_ice` [218,230,238], `ty_hf_deck`, `ty_hf_slab`), which is exactly the trap `voltage.json` is written about — flat fill, `metal: 0.00`, no environment term, no window grid, *"pale stone's opposite is not a mirror"* — and the `chrome`/`frost` families that fix it reached five authored models and **no Halcyon tile**. ⚠ **AND 85.4% OF ITS WALL AREA IS A FLAT-SHADED DRUM**, so a palette swap could not have fixed it anyway: 2,594 drum faces against 444 box faces over the 23 arms, **zero** `windowBay` calls and **zero** `neonBlade` calls, because `drawFacetDrum` never reaches `wallTex`, shades against a FIXED key direction rather than the sun, and marks its faces `flat` so the GL material pass skips them — and `derivedTrim` takes its wall planes from unyawed BOXES only, so **a drum-mass building is invisible to the whole derived kit** (five of the ten models in the registry with no trim at all are in this quarter). ⚠ **THE VARIETY WAS ALREADY THERE AND IT WAS ON THE WRONG AXIS, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING**: those ramps are 133 calls over **69 distinct lo→hi pairs**, every one hand-picked, and the quarter still read as one white material because all 69 are the SAME HUE — 17–29% saturation at the dark end and **2–12% at the light end**, with `hfChrome` raising the light dot to a power ≥ 2 so most of every drum sits at the light end, which is exactly where they converge. Sixty-nine different numbers, one colour, and all of the difference spent in LIGHTNESS where nobody can see it. So each TILE takes one of eight casts off its own seed. ⚠ **A CAST IS A HUE AND NEVER A BRIGHTNESS** — authored as an angle in the YIQ chroma plane, so `dY` is zero **by construction at every amplitude and however the table is retuned**, which is what keeps the estate reading as one developer's cladding rather than as buildings painted different colours. ⚠ **THE NUDGE SCALES WITH THE FACET'S OWN LUMINANCE**, because a flat offset does its work on the shadow side where the ramps are already distinguishable; written flat it moves the dark end 19.0 against the lit end 13.7, which is the wrong way round. ⚠ **AND IT IS SEEDED, WHICH IS THE ONLY REASON GLASS 2 CAN HAVE IT**: `meshParams` already includes `it.seed`, so the per-model mesh memo is per-TILE and a seeded colour is captured correctly for every copy — a cast off the hour, the camera or the power state would be baked at capture and frozen, the trap `POWER_NB` has to ride the group to avoid. ⚠ **HASHED, NEVER `seed % N`**: the tile seed is linear in both coordinates, so a modulo walks the table in lockstep along a street and lays the casts in stripes — it passes every other check (eight distinct casts, all luminance-neutral, all reaching the capture) and paints a repeating pattern nobody authored. ⚠ **AND `HF_AMP` WAS SET AGAINST THE PICTURE, NOT THE TABLE**: on a pinned-clock A/B (control 0.00%), 13 moves 7.5% of the frame at a mean of 3.4 levels and 20 moves 14.1% at a mean of 4.2 — 13 is at the bottom of what reads at all and 20 is where two towers of the same TYPE stop looking like the same building. ⚠ **The boxes are deliberately NOT tinted**: a palette key is an atlas entry and a per-tile variant on `texKey` is the thing the power note forbids. ⚠ **AND `__street` IS NOT THE INSTRUMENT FOR THE NUMBER** — it freezes no clock by design, so the same setting rendered twice moves **49% of the frame** against an effect of a few levels; pin `performance.now`, `Date.now`, `Math.random`, `resFloor` and `perfDS` and the control goes to 0.00%. `npm run gl:hftint` ([scripts/shapes/hftint.mjs](scripts/shapes/hftint.mjs), in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in `shapes:smoke`, per the two-lists warning above), mutation-tested 7 of 7. ⚠ Its arm scan is **bounded to `drawTypeModelArm`** — a bare sweep for `\n    case '…':` over the whole file collects cases from every other switch at the same indent, and the first run reported `skullbob`, a CAB TRINKET hanging off a mirror, then failed it for not being tinted. ⚠ And its leak check is a SOURCE check on purpose: all 152 `hfChrome`/`hfGlass` call sites are inside `drawTypeModelArm` and `drawTypeModel` re-arms on the way in, so deleting the `finally` restore is **unobservable today** and survives every behavioural probe; what it guards is a future nested arm.

⚠ **AND THE FACADE IS ARTICULATED (`RENDER_TUNE.glPier`, 0 puts every frontage back to a flat plane with windows in it).** The other half of the same report — *"big yawn"* — and this file’s own note under `windowBay` already says why greebles were never going to answer it: **"a flat wall wearing forty greebles is a flat wall"**. Every detail kind before this is bolted ONTO a wall; `pilaster` is a rank of vertical fins standing PROUD of one, from the plinth to the crown, with the glazing set back between them. ⚠ **IT IS ONE PART DRAWING `n` FINS**, the `bollard` rule, and here it is not a convenience: every fin would otherwise be a claim on `KIT_MAX`, and a rhythm is the one thing that means nothing at a count of one. ⚠ **AND THE TWO PERIODS ARE THE SAME PART** — a stone fin gathered into a stepped capital is art deco and the identical fin with a lit line up it is the skyline this game is set in, so which one a building gets is read off axes that already exist (`period`, `mod`) and a street carries both with nothing authored. `glowFrom` shortens the run to the fin’s crown, which is what a 1930s tower does with its verticals. ⚠ **THE LIGHT IS A STROKE AND THE FIN IS GEOMETRY, WHICH IS THE WHOLE COST STORY**: a lit line through `emitLightRunner` is one quad in the stroke layer, where a glowing FACE would be a mesh quad per fin per building over the whole city — so the geometry is rationed (a front face always, its two returns in the mesh only, a cap when asked) and the part that reads at range is nearly free. Rich list only, so the 2-D fallback is provably the renderer it was and `framecost` did not move. Measured: **+2,219 mesh faces over the registry (+4.4%)** for a rank on **68% of buildings**.

⚠ **AND IT IS PLACED BESIDE THE WINDOW GRID, WHICH IS FOUR SEPARATE THINGS IT HAD TO LEARN.** A fin stands at a column BOUNDARY — this section’s own `cw` comment has read "leave a pier at each end" since it was written and nothing ever stood in it — so deriving the pitch anywhere else would be a second expression of the column rhythm and the two would drift into fins drawn across windows. ⚠ **IT IS CHARGED TO THE WINDOW SHARE AND PUSHED BEFORE THE GRID**: `spent` is one counter shared by every section and the name board is pushed LAST, so a part added to the wall costs a building its own name — `gl:mesh` failed nine models with "the rich kit LOST sign" the first time it was charged to the general budget. ⚠ **A SHADE OF THE WALL IS NOT A CONTRAST**: at the strongest multiplier in the set the rank measured correctly in the mesh and was invisible on the building, because most of this city is faced in something dark and a seventh of near-black is near-black — the same trap `feedback_detail_parts_need_own_palette` records. It takes the trim palette, which already guarantees a luminance step. ⚠ **AND A BUILDING THAT LETTERS ITS OWN FRONT GETS NO LINE OF LIGHT UP IT**: the kit knows THAT a frontage carries lettering (`armSignsItself`) and has no idea WHERE, so a runner pitched anyway lands across it — `sign:fit` measured **thirteen models with a neon line through their own name**, on false fronts and fascias from z 0.58 to 1.06. The fins stay, because they sit where a false front’s lettering goes BETWEEN; the light does not. ⚠ **AND THE END FINS COME OFF WHEN A CORNER IS SPOKEN FOR** — the outermost boundaries are the band’s EDGES, so a fin there overhangs the reserved corner by its own half-width and lands under the blade hanging in it; `cols - 1` fins at the same pitch are still centred on the same band, so nothing shifts. ⚠ **AND A RANK THAT ENDED UP ON A PODIUM IS NOT LIT**: standing the fins on the front-most mass is right for the geometry and puts them in front of whatever that projecting box carries — measured both ways, on `main`’s own plane the rank leaks 22 more stroke points into its own building (`glself`) and forward it crosses three more signs (`sign:fit`), so the fins go forward and the light comes off and neither number moves.

⚠ **AND THE CLOUD DECK IS ON THE DEPTH BUFFER (`RENDER_TUNE.glClouds`, 0 puts it back).** ⚠ **It had never been run by a test at all**, which is how it stayed the largest unmeasured thing in the frame: `drawVolumetricClouds` returns on its first line without `wxField`/`acX`/`acY`, and not one harness in the repo passed them — `viewRenderSmoke`, `framecost` and every Modelshop bench hand over a map, an hour and a heading and no weather field, so every number ever written down about the cost of a frame here was a number for a sky with no clouds in it. `npm run shapes:clouds` measured it at **2,757–9,460 canvas calls a frame, median 5,386**, against a whole city block's 17,400 — a third of the frame. On the GPU each card is a quad: **1,200–1,595 canvas calls saved and 0.5–1.0 ms** on a 640×360 frame, and the deck agrees with the 2-D one to **0.09–0.23% mean colour**. ⚠ **That last number is only readable beside the ABLATION**, which is why `__glClouds()` renders a third frame with no deck at all: "the two agree to 0.15%" is also exactly what comes back when the new one draws nothing and the old one drew very little. The deck is worth 2.9–10.4% of the frame, so 0.15% against that is a faithful port. ⚠ **The pass clears COLOUR AND NOT DEPTH**, which is the whole trick — the cards test against the city the world pass wrote a moment ago, so a thirty-storey block can stand IN a 1,600 ft overcast instead of the painter having to pick a side, and the mass is never uploaded twice. Clear depth as well and every cloud is back in front of every tower, which looks exactly like the port having no effect. ⚠ **And it is a SECOND hook at a second moment**, not a sink the world pass reads: the deck collects after the world blit has already run, which is the trap the Curtain fell into — the sink fills, nothing reads it, nothing draws and nothing says so. ⚠ **The sort survives the depth buffer**: a card is translucent, so the order it is composited in changes the colour, and the depth test only decides what the deck is behind. ⚠ **Every card now gets the treatment only the big ones could afford** — in 2-D a card wider than 26 px gets a directional gradient raked toward the sun plus a value-noise mottle and everything below blits one of twelve sprites lit from straight above; a fragment shader solves the two-circle cone per pixel and reads the curdle tile for nothing, so the tier does not exist there. Same argument as the mesh capturing at `ADORN_NEAR`.

⚠ **AND WHAT FLIES HAD TO WRITE DEPTH BEFORE THAT DECK COULD SORT AGAINST IT (`RENDER_TUNE.glAirDepth`, the **What flies in the depth buffer** slider; 0 puts both back).** Reported as *"the clouds seem to be appearing in front of the geese"*, and it is the other half of the paragraph above: a tower can stand in a cloud because the MASS wrote depth, and a goose is a BILLBOARD, which `gl/billboards.js` deliberately keeps out of the depth buffer so a bush cannot punch its own alpha-shaped hole in it. That rule is right for everything standing on the ground and it has one consequence nobody had needed until now — a billboard leaves nothing behind for a LATER pass to test against, and there is exactly one later pass. At the birds' own pixels the buffer still held the ground a few hundred tiles away, so every card passed and the deck painted over the whole flock however far below the cloud base it was flying. ⚠ **AIR CONTACTS TOOK THE SAME OPT-IN AND HAVE SINCE STOPPED BEING CARDS AT ALL** — `bakeContacts` pushed a bogey through the same funnel with no depth, so the deck could sort in front of another aircraft for the same reason; the flag reaches that quad (asserted in the browser, `depth: true` on and `false` off) and a frame with a contact is pixel-identical either way, but **no scene could be built in which it changed the picture** — swept over three ranges and four altitude bands the deck was never in front of the bogey. The geese are the pair that could be measured. ⚠ That path now only runs with `glShip` at 0: **every contact is real triangles** (see `contactIsSolid`), so the card, its per-frame `texImage2D`, and the 700 px guard above which a close bogey fell back to painting over the composited city are all gone by default. The opt-in stays because the card is still what the renderer does with the solids slider off. ⚠ **GROUND BILLBOARDS ARE NOT IN IT AND MUST NOT BE**: a cloud is never in front of a tree, so trees, bushes, rocks and street actors would take the cost and the hard-edged silhouette for a case that cannot happen — with no billboard asking, the prepass is not a code path at all, which is what makes the flag's 0 provably the renderer that shipped. ⚠ **AND IT IS A DEPTH-ONLY PREPASS, NEVER A RAISED CUTOFF ON THE ONE DRAW.** The obvious version discards below half alpha and writes depth in the same pass, which also deletes every texel between the old 0.004 cutoff and 0.5 from the PICTURE — a goose's antialiased rim, and on a contact the canopy glass, the nav-lamp glow and the exhaust, none of which are fringe. The colour pass is byte-for-byte the one that always shipped and a second draw with `colorMask` off lays the silhouette down ahead of it. ⚠ **THE QUAD'S OWN ALPHA COUNTS IN THAT PREPASS AND NOWHERE ELSE**: a flock dissolving into the far haze is drawn at a fraction of itself, and a shape that is 40% there taking 100% of the cloud behind it is a bird-shaped HOLE in the deck — a worse artefact than the one being fixed, sitting exactly at the fade where every flock spends most of its time. ⚠ **AND `depthFunc` IS NOW LOAD-BEARING IN THAT FILE**: the prepass lays the quad's own depth down first, so under the GL default of `LESS` the colour pass fails its own test at every pixel and the bird is simply not drawn — every pass here already sets `LEQUAL`, what changed is that this one depends on it. ⚠ **NO HEADLESS GATE CAN SEE ANY OF IT**, because every harness in the repo installs a GL hook that returns a bare canvas and never reaches a draw call, let alone a depth test — `npm run gl:fauna` asserts the DATA (what flies asks, what walks does not, no other producer does, and 0 removes it, each mutation-tested) and `__glGooseSky()` in the Modelshop measures the pixels. Measured over a whole flight at 640×360, the share of the flock's own pixels still readable under the deck: **74.7% → 84.3% in rain, worst frame 39% → 74%**, the same within a point for cloudy, storm, snow, ash and dust, and **0 pixels outside the flock moved** in any of them. ⚠ **The control is `clear`**, whose deck sits at 7.5 tiles with nothing near a goose: 90.4% → 92.9%, which is what says the change is the cards rather than a wash. ⚠ **What is LEFT is the wash and is correct** — the deck also lays a screen-space haze band and a whiteout flood over the whole frame, neither of them depth-testable, and a bird in haze is hazy. ⚠ **AND IT IS GLASS 2 ONLY**: in 2-D the deck is painted over the finished world with no depth to sort against, so a bird under a cloud is the picture the canvas has always drawn.

⚠ **AND A BAKED BILLBOARD IS FOR A LANDMARK, NEVER FOR A PART OF AN ORDINARY BUILDING.** `markBillboard` paints a 2-D adornment into a canvas and hands it to `gl/billboards.js` as a quad — and it re-bakes at the LIVE camera every frame (`fresh: true`), because the bake has to land where the 2-D pass would have put it. That is **a canvas repaint plus a `texImage2D` upload per instance per frame**. Affordable for the six marks it was written for (a statue, a gate, a sign, pylons, a bay, a no-fly tile — a handful, each on its own tile); ruinous for a mast, a lattice tower or a neon blade, which hang off ordinary buildings by the dozen, and which were routed through it for one afternoon and measured as **a cockpit framerate below GLASS 1**. ⚠ **AND NEVER PUT `dx`/`dy` IN A CACHE KEY.** They are CAMERA-RELATIVE and change every frame the camera moves, so all three minted a brand-new key per frame: an unbounded canvas leak on the windshield side, and on the GL side a 256-entry texture cache turning over in seconds. `markBillboard` prefixes `MARK_TILE` now, so a caller names only what varies WITHIN a tile and cannot put the camera in a key again. ⚠ **AND THE CACHE EVICTED THE WRONG THING, WHICH IS THE PART THAT LOOKED LIKE CORRUPTED ARTWORK.** It took the oldest-INSERTED entry from inside `textureFor`, and the oldest entries are the STABLE keys — a bush, a cactus, the gate on its own tile — which by then are already sitting in `batches` waiting to be drawn. `gl.deleteTexture` on one of those leaves the batch pointing at a dead texture, and a dead texture samples as whatever the driver hands back: the scatter across a whole region turned **bright pink**, and a gate was drawn as **a hovering bush**. Only from an external camera at angles that put enough masts in view at once, which is why no synthetic scene found it. Eviction runs once at the top of `upload` now, against the keys this frame actually uses, and refuses to delete a live one — worth keeping whatever the callers do, because it is what turns a cache miss into a wrong picture rather than a slow one. `glLastFrame().bbTex` is the cache size, because an unbounded cache is invisible until the moment it starts evicting live entries.

⚠ **AND THE DEPOT SHED IS GEOMETRY (`RENDER_TUNE.glBay`, the **Depot in the depth buffer** slider; 0 puts it back on the canvas).** `gl/ownship.js` is `gl/solids.js` now, because the rig was only the first thing that needed what it does — flat-shaded triangles that WRITE depth and are not culled — and the depot shed is the second.
