The shed is the one building in GLASS drawn at a fixed size by its own function rather than extruded from a storey stack, so it is in `MASS_EXCEPT`, it never reached the GL mass, and it was painted on the canvas after the city was composited. `markHidden` was all that stood between it and a warehouse, and `markHidden` answers whether the WHOLE of a mark is covered: a depot is a tile wide and a third of a storey tall, so behind a row of shops most of it is hidden, none of it is all hidden, and it drew whole. Measured from the cab, a shed four tiles out behind a warehouse two tiles out: **the canvas drew 57% of a building that was entirely covered, and the depth buffer draws 0%**; with the wall a tile further back it is 25% hidden against **85%**, the remainder being the ridge genuinely standing above the wall's roofline. ⚠ **IT COULD NOT JOIN THE MASS, AND THAT IS A PROPERTY OF THE SHED.** The mass buffer is cached per map window; the shed's roller door OPENS as a truck comes up the apron and its palette flips between an interior and an exterior read depending on which side of the walls the eye is — both per-frame, per-tile answers a cached buffer cannot carry, so it would rebuild the whole window every frame, which is the one thing that buffer exists not to do. ⚠ **AND IT IS COLLECTED INLINE, NOT FROM `emitFace`.** Every other mark is queued as a closure that runs at FLUSH, after the pass has gone to the GPU and every sink is null — the Curtain's own trap — so the bay branch calls the painter directly, before the hook. ⚠ **THE SINKS TAKE THE POLYGON WHOLE.** `drawVehicleBay` clips every face against its own near plane because a 2-D projection blows up behind the eye; a depth buffer clips in three dimensions against the real frustum, so handing it a pre-cut polygon makes the surviving SHAPE a function of where you are looking from — 127 faces from the road and 40 from a quarter turn round, for a shed that had not moved. ⚠ **AND A WORLD-SPACE QUAD MUST NOT BE HAND-WOUND.** The name board over the door went to the decal layer as four world corners in reading order, which is obviously right and is wrong for half the buildings in the world: a decal marked `cull` is one-sided, which side depends on the winding in NDC, and reading order in the shed's LOCAL frame survives the rotation only up to a mirror — so the lettering was culled on exactly the two `ent` facings where it faces you, and drew on the other two, which is why one depot says the feature works. `emitSurfaceText` takes SCREEN points and recovers the quad through `cam.unproj`, which cannot be mirrored. What is lost is the hairline: `stroke` outlines a face in 1px and a triangle soup has nowhere to put one, so the GL shed is a shade flatter at the corners — 2.7% of a close cab frame, all of it inside the shed's own silhouette.

⚠ **AND NO HEADLESS GATE CAN SEE A BUG INSIDE A GL DRAW CALL.** `npm run gl:bay` ([scripts/shapes/bay.mjs](scripts/shapes/bay.mjs), in `shapes:smoke` and in the push chain) pins everything above that is expressible without a rasteriser — the geometry arrives, it stands up, orbiting the camera moves none of it, moving the camera INSIDE its own tile moves none of it, `glBay 0` collects none, and a GLASS 1 control still paints one. ⚠ Its `cam.ox/oy` check had to become that second comparison, because the offset is SUB-TILE: a first draft held the shed against the map window's bounding box, which reads as watertight and cannot fail — restoring the bug left every vertex well inside it. But **every harness in this repo installs a hook that returns `null`**, which is the no-WebGL2 path, so not one of them ever reaches `drawSolids` — a stale identifier inside that function passed all 26 shape gates, `client:smoke` and `imports:smoke`, and killed the whole GL pass on the first real frame. There is no headless answer to that class; the Modelshop is the instrument, and the A/B is `RENDER_TUNE.glBay` with everything else held still. ⚠ **AND DO NOT CACHE-BUST THE IMPORT TO PICK UP AN EDIT.** `import('…/windshield.js?v=' + Date.now())` builds a SECOND module graph, and `install.js` imports `installGLWorld` from the unbusted specifier — so the hook lands on the old copy, the frame you are painting has no hook at all, and both sides of the A/B come back as pure 2-D and pixel-identical. Reload the page.

⚠ **AND THE SIGNAL MAST WAS THE LAST PAINTER ON THE CANVAS, WHICH IS WHY IT LOOKED LIKE A LAMP POST BUG.** Reported as lamp posts showing through a building, and the street lamps were innocent: `drawStreetLamp` had already moved to the depth buffer with the rest of the roadside, and `npm run worldresidue` said so in one line. What a driver is actually looking at is a **signal mast**, which is a pole, a cantilever boom, two hanging heads AND the cobra-head street light on the opposite side of the same pole. It IS the lamp post on that corner. Every part of it was still painting on the 2-D canvas, composited after the GL city, and what stood between a head three storeys up and the tower behind it was `groundHidden` at the mast’s BASE — one point, all-or-nothing — and later `beginOcclusionClip`, a ~5px cell grid eroded by one. **A mast whose base is visible draws IN FULL however much of it is behind a building**, and the head is exactly the part that is. It is drawn during the SWEEP now — the steel through `emitWire`, the plates through `emitDecoFill`, the lenses as baked decals, the lamps through `glowPool` — so the world pass leaves **0 painters and 0 faces** on the canvas and `worldresidue`’s allow-list is EMPTY. ⚠ **The `emitFace` half is the whole fix rather than a tidy-up**: a queued closure runs at FLUSH, after the composite has read every sink and nulled it, so a mast queued there cannot reach the stroke, decal or light layers *however hard it reaches for them* — the Curtain’s own trap, one layer out. ⚠ **An octagon is eight corners and a decal carries four**, so a lens is a BAKED texture on its own bounding quad rather than a six-quad fan per lamp (three lenses, two heads, two masts to a junction), keyed with no camera term in it. ⚠ **`emitDecoQuad` takes PROJECTED points and `bp` returns a bare `[sx, sy]`** — hand it the latter and `cam.unproj` has nothing to invert, answers null, and the quad drops to the canvas fallback: not an error, just the lens quietly still painting over the city, which is what the census caught when the steel and the plates had moved and twelve lenses had not. ⚠ And **a lit lens’s `face` local SHADOWED the `face` parameter**, harmless while every corner here was screen-space and a scalar where a direction belongs the moment one is not.

⚠ **AND PARTIAL OCCLUSION IS THE MEASUREMENT, BECAUSE FULL OCCLUSION PROVES NOTHING.** A mast completely behind a building is hidden by the OLD probe too — base hidden, skip — so a test that only blocks the whole thing passes either way and is worth nothing. What a probe cannot do is hide PART of one. Measured in the Modelshop against a pinned clock and pinned dice: an occluder overlapping only the mast’s right sliver hides **2%**, one covering its whole width hides **99%**, and clear air is **125–200 px** against a **9 px** noise floor. ⚠ **Three things had to be pinned before that instrument read anything at all**, and each returned a confident wrong number first: the CLOCK (two renders of one scene differ by ~100k pixels, because the clouds drift), `Math.random`, and the **CSS BOX** — a canvas with no CSS size is resized by `paintWindshield` off its own previous backing store, so it shrank 760→418→255 over three renders and every frame diffed as ~100%. ⚠ **And do not A/B `glDeco` live**: toggling it between renders put the baseline at 183,666 px on a frame whose mast is 125. Reload the page between configs, the same rule `glBand` and `richKit` already carry.

⚠ **AND THE ECHELON'S OWN LIGHTS WERE THE NEXT ONE, WHICH IS THE SAME BUG WITH THE HULL ALREADY FIXED.** Reported as *"echelon lights show thru buildings"*, with a screenshot of two sidelights and a beacon lying across the front of a tenement. Her HULL had already moved to `gl/solids.js`, so the ship itself was correctly hidden — and everything BOLTED to it was not, because the whole vessel was drawn from one `emitFace` closure and a closure runs at FLUSH, after the composite has read every sink and nulled it. That was recorded at the time as a stated remainder ("what can still cross a building in front of her is a handrail"), and the honest version of that sentence is that it was a bug nobody had looked at yet. The pad ring, the rails, the mast and its radar bar, the deck lamps, the helipad perimeter lights, the amber beacon, the sidelights, the WAKE and the auto-land guidance dome are drawn during the SWEEP now — strokes through `emitWire`, lamps as hard sprites, the wake's foam V and bow moustaches as flat world quads through `emitDecoFill`, the blooms through `glowPool` — so `worldresidue` reports **0 painters and 0 faces** on the canvas and its allow-list is empty again. Measured in the Modelshop behind a low warehouse: **0 leaked pixels against 14-33 with `RENDER_TUNE.glShip` at 0**, which is the same arrangement in a flag rather than a second copy of the code. ⚠ **THE FITTINGS FOLLOW THE HULL, NOT THE FLAG** — a collect that threw, or `glShip` at 0, leaves the hull on the canvas, and fittings depth-tested against a city her own hull draws through is a worse picture than either half, so that branch keeps the one closure that always shipped. ⚠ **`emitWire`'s DEFAULT PULL IS MORE THAN THIS SHIP HAS TO GIVE**: 0.6 tiles against a half-beam of 0.083 at `YACHT_SCALE`, so the default would slide a rail on the far flank out in front of her own near side — these sit ON a hull in the depth buffer and take `FACE_EPS`. ⚠ **A CONTINUOUS VALUE MAY NEVER REACH A DECAL'S KEY**: the wake's turbulence fill took its alpha from `spd`, a float off the wire, which mints a fresh texture every time the throttle moves a hair — the unbounded-cache bug that drew a gate as a hovering bush. The throttle rides the instance alpha and the colour is one of two. ⚠ **AND THE SAME PASS FIXED EVERY ROOFTOP HELIDECK**, because `padCatchVolume` is shared: that one had never been measured at all, since no census scene has ever carried a pad. ⚠ **The thing to check afterwards is not that the leak went to zero but that anything still draws** — a ship whose fittings were deleted also leaks nothing — so `scripts/shapes/yacht.mjs` now counts the stroke, sprite and decal sinks with her in the scene and with the same sea empty, and all three have to grow. ⚠ And **the depth buffer removed something the canvas was drawing wrongly**: from dead astern she used to show BOTH sidelights, because the far-side lamp was painted over her own hull. Red 0 / green 9 at one heading and red 9 / green 0 at its mirror is the rule of the road, and it is what "occlusion beats the old picture" looks like when the old picture was confidently wrong.

⚠ **AND THE GROUND PASS CANNOT REACH A SINK AT ALL, WHICH IS THE SAME BUG ONE PASS EARLIER (`emitGroundLate`).** Reported as *"wind socks at airports disappear weirdly"* and *"add runway lights"*, and both halves are one line of ordering: `SPRITE_SINK`, `STROKE_SINK` and `DECAL_SINK` are armed INSIDE `drawWorldObjects`, so for the whole of `drawGroundSurfaces` all three are null — every airfield drawer took its canvas path, and `drawWorldObjects` then blitted the opaque GL canvas over the lot. Measured over Coldwater Regional: **72 of 72 runway edge-light ops and 4 of 4 windsock fabric ops issued BEFORE the blit**, so the airport had no lights at night and the socks were gone. ⚠ **The fix is a DEFERRAL, not a move** — the ground pass pushes a closure and the world pass runs it beside the shadow's own drain, which is the seam `OWN_SHADOWS` and `LATE_BILLBOARDS` already exist for; the drawers know nothing about it. ⚠ **A closure must capture its ALPHA as a value**, because the ground pass sets `ctx.globalAlpha` per tile for the far fade and restores it on the way out. ⚠ **And the flat paint stays inline**: `stripeA`/`dashedA` already record into `GROUND_MESH`, so only what STANDS on the tarmac is deferred.

⚠ **A WINDSOCK WAS A RIBBON ON THE GLASS, AND THE TELL IS THE DEPTH SPREAD.** Two projected points and a width in PIXELS clamped 1.4–8: not 3-D at any range, and the same shape from every side because there was no other side. `sockCone` builds the real article — five bands of `n` facets on a curve that hangs away from the swivel as the wind drops, at near-real proportions (a 5 m mast, a 3.5 m sock, a 0.9 m mouth), through `emitDecoFill`. ⚠ **It MOVES, so it may never be mass**: `emitFlat` records into the per-model mesh, captured once at a frozen clock, which would nail the sock to whatever the wind was doing at `now = 1000` for the life of the process. ⚠ **The cull and `solid` are the same decision twice** — a tube is convex, so culling the far half halves the quads for an identical picture, and `solid` puts the near half in the depth buffer so the cloud deck cannot draw through it. ⚠ **A screen-space quad has all FOUR corners at one camera depth** by construction (`quadBox`), and a quad on a cone has four different ones; that is what the gate asserts, and it is the thing a screenshot is worst at settling.

⚠ **AND A HELIPAD WAS DRAWING AS A ONE-TILE RUNWAY.** `c.pad` has been on the wire since the landing capture needed it — `deriveSurfaceCell` sets it from the airfield's own `vtol_only`, already the whole mechanical statement of "no strip, rotorcraft only" — and **nothing in the renderer had ever read it**, so Threshold, the Gantry and the Ascension each got a dashed centreline, piano keys, TWO PAPI glideslope arrays and two windsocks on a pad no fixed-wing can use. It needed no new art: `helideck` is the pad the Solenne's roof has always carried and every part of it is already depth-buffered. ⚠ **A `dust` field is still dirt** — the lawless strips are authored with no paint, no PAPI and no edge lights, so the Gantry keeps its ruts and its sock, and only the surfaced pads get markings.

⚠ **AND THE THREE HAND-WRITTEN TOWERS NEVER GOT `helixRunner`'s FIX.** A corner light-runner LIES ON the shaft it traces, so it only has to win a tie; at `emitWire`'s default 0.6-tile pull it is dragged further toward the eye than the tower has to give (its own near face is ~0.44 from the tile centre), and the runner on the FAR corner comes out in front of the near wall — the helix painted flat across the building instead of going behind it. `helixRunner` has carried `DECO_PULL` for the authored kit since that was found, and the Solenne, Halcyon and the Ascendant spire were missed. Measured in isolation with `glself`: **`named:solenneresidences` 34 leaked stroke points → 8, `type:asc_spire` 23 → 0**, 663 → 614 over the registry, and `gl:mesh` identical at 59,133 faces.

⚠ **NONE OF THAT IS VISIBLE TO ANY OTHER GATE**, because `glresidue` and `glself` run MODEL ARMS and this is the ground pass. `node scripts/shapes/airfield.mjs` (`npm run gl:airfield` for the report; in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in `shapes:smoke`, per the warning above about two lists) asserts the four claims that are silent when wrong, with a GLASS 1 control that must still paint the lot or the check is vacuous. Mutation-tested 4 of 4: removing the deferral reproduces the original bug exactly (15 sock / 132 edge / 16 green ops wiped), removing the cull puts 20 of 50 facets facing away, disabling the `pad` branch loses the TLOF ring, and taking `groundLamp` off the sprite sink puts every lamp back on the canvas.
⚠ **AND EVERY BENCH IN THIS REPO PINS THE ADAPTIVE DIALS, SO NOTHING THEY MEASURE CAN SEE ONE.** `framecost`, `__glFrame`, `__glLights`, `__glClouds` and `__glFlicker` all set `resFloor: 1` and `perfDS: 0`, for the good reason the Modelshop README gives — a loose dial sheds resolution exactly where the frame is expensive and gets read as the renderer. The cost is that a bug driven BY a dial is invisible to all of them at once, and "it is worse in the cockpit" is precisely a report about a dial. That is a second instance of the trap already recorded for the resolution step: `PERF_DS` is still `Math.round((frameMs − 24) / 8)` with no deadband, bistable in the same way `resStep` was, and inert only because `drawMode7Floor` returns before the raster whenever `glFloor` is on. It will not stay inert if the GPU floor is ever switched off.

⚠ **AND THE GL SPIKE IS MEASURED.** `client/game/js/panels/gl/` holds a WebGL2 world pass — GLASS own camera as a 4x4 (`npm run gl:parity`: 2,811 projections, worst disagreement 4.9e-10 px) and the city as triangles taken from the primitives that already draw it via `MESH_SINK` (`npm run gl:mesh`: 7,977 faces over all 173 models, each agreeing with the shape it collides as). Benchmarked in the Modelshop (`__glBench()`) on a 73-building city: the 2-D building pass costs **3.6 ms**, the same geometry in GL costs **0.02-0.04 ms**, and **100x the triangles (286,600, all on screen) costs 0.67-1.06 ms** — a hundred times the detail for a third of what today renderer costs at one times. ⚠ Those numbers are the SPIKE, measured before any of it was wired in — a mass-only pass with no textures and no adornments, driven from the Modelshop GL toggle. What ships is the paragraph above. See tools/modelshop/README.md for the four criteria and for the three ways the benchmark lied before it told the truth.

⚠ **AND SOME OF THE CITY MOVES (`RENDER_TUNE.motion`, the **Moving parts** slider; 0 is the renderer as it shipped).** A wharf crane slewing and lifting, a gantry trolley running its beam, a grabber taking a bite out of a scrap pile, a chain block pulling an engine, an arc welder stammering in an open bay, a rank of clone vats breathing. ⚠ **A MOVING PART MAY NEVER BE MASS, AND THE TRAP IS SILENT IN EVERY DIRECTION.** `draw3DBoxAt` and `emitFlat` record into the shape capture and the per-model MESH, and both are taken ONCE at a FROZEN CLOCK — `captureRawPass` runs the arm at `now = 0`, `captureModelMesh` at `now = 1000` — then cached on the model's own identity for every tile that draws it. So a box whose position reads the clock is captured at one pose: GLASS 1 animates it, GLASS 2 nails it to whatever `sin(0)` came to, and the shape you COLLIDE with is a third answer again. It passes `shapeLinearityError` too, because at a frozen `now` the term is a constant and a constant is affine. **Nothing anywhere would say a word.** So everything that moves goes to the three layers collected FRESH every frame — `emitWire` (strokes), `emitDecoFill` (decals) and `glowPool`/`blinkLight` (sprites) — which is not a restriction to work around, because those three are exactly a jib, a hook block and a lamp. The consequence is that **you cannot fly into a hook block**, and that is the right way round: what stays mass is the tower, which is the part that is genuinely always in the same place. ⚠ **PARKED IS NOT ABSENT** — `motionPhase` answers −1 when the clock may not run and every cycle is written so that the −1 pose IS the `u = 0` pose, so the off-switch gives you a still crane rather than a tower with its jib deleted, and the capture sees the same structure the live frame does. Measured with one setting per process (⚠ **measuring both in one run reports the CACHE**, not the flag: the first pass fills the shape/kit/texture memos and the second gets them free, which showed up as several hundred phantom entries on models with no moving part at all), the flag is worth **+2 sprites across all 213 models** — the welder's strike — because everything else draws the same parts either way. ⚠ **`movingBox`'s `yaw` IS A WORLD YAW AND EVERY ARM THINKS IN ITS OWN LOCAL FRAME**: `F`/`facePt` turns a local POINT into a world one and says nothing about direction, so anything with a bearing of its own has to add the new `faceYaw(E)` — leave it out and the crane is correct on a north-facing tile and rotated on the other three, which is the one facing every harness renders. ⚠ **The idle half is the feature**: `work` is the fraction of the period a machine runs and the phase is offset by a hash of the tile, so no two cranes on one quay are ever in step and at any moment most of them are parked — which is what stops a skyline looking like a fairground. ⚠ **An arc is not a blink** — two incommensurate sines over a threshold, because `blinkLight`'s own sine keeps perfect time and a welder that keeps time reads as a navigation light; and it modulates a LAMP, never the frame, under the same no-strobe rule the drug FX are held to. ⚠ **And an authored model cannot carry any of this**: `case 'authored'` draws boxes and drums out of a JSON file straight into the mesh, so a building ported to `content/building_models/` **loses its arm's motion** — bind by name and the authored record wins.

⚠ **AND A MOVING PART IS SHADED LIKE A WALL NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.motionShade`, 0 is the three flat fills exactly as they shipped).** Reported as the yellow cranes in the south-west, and true of the wharf jibs, the quay gantries and the scrap grabbers alike, because every one of them is `movingBox`. Every mass wall in this city is a texture with `wallLit`’s two-stop ramp over it — tinted toward `mix(sky, key)` at the head and toward the SHADOW at the foot, that second term being the ambient occlusion which is most of why a facade reads as solid — and a moving part had NONE of it: a top face at the raw palette colour and two sides on one grey multiplier bottoming out at 0.44. So a crane’s machinery house was the brightest thing in the frame beside the darkest with a hard line between, which is what cut-out card looks like. It is the same arithmetic the walls run, composited in JS rather than by canvas alpha because the base is a flat colour and not a texture. ⚠ **A DECAL’S KEY IS ITS COLOUR, SO A GRADIENT HAS TO BE A TEXTURE.** `emitDecoFill` takes a `ramp` of two stops now and bakes them into one 4x32 page through the ordinary `bakeQuadTex` cache — the GL path never touches the 2-D `paint` closure, so `fillStyle = aGradient` would have reached nothing at all, and the fallback lays the same two stops down the polygon’s own screen extent, which is how `wallLit` fakes a per-vertex ramp across a mass wall. Both stops are QUANTISED for `roofHatch`’s reason: a shade that slid continuously with the slew would mint a page a frame and turn a 384-entry cache over. ⚠ **And the corners ARE the UVs** — the head of the ramp lands on corner 0, so a side wound from its foot comes back dark lid, bright skirt: a box lit from underground. ⚠ **THE TOP IS THE CEILING, WHICH IT WAS NOT.** Both the old top and the old lit side reached 1.0, so under a high sun a machinery house’s flank was exactly as bright as its lid and the box lost its corner. The sides run `MOTION_LO..MOTION_HI` and the top sits above both. ⚠ **AND THE DIRECTIONAL TERM HAD TO COME IN AS THE RAMP WENT ON** — the ramp already carries a key tint and a shadow foot, so keeping the old 0.44 floor under it stacks two answers to one question and the dark flank goes to nothing. ⚠ **AND `scripts/shapes/moving.mjs` WENT RED ON A PICTURE THAT WAS CORRECT, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING.** Its third check reads the frame’s fills back and asks whether the container on the crane’s spreader is painted the colour the ship’s stow is about to gain — the whole of *what swings out over the water is what lands* — and it built that string out of `berthBoxColour` BY HAND. The moment a box stopped being drawn at its raw palette value that string named nothing in the picture. It asks the renderer now (`motionTopCss`, exported beside the berth constants and for their stated reason: a gate holding its own copy of somebody else’s arithmetic goes on agreeing with itself while the thing it is watching moves). ⚠ **And that export needed `LAST_WORLD_LIGHT`** — nothing outside a frame has a `LIGHT_STATE`, so its first cut answered with the raw palette colour, the exact string it exists to stop anybody using, and the gate stayed red while reporting the right reason. ⚠ **A TRUSS IS THREE SHADES OF ONE PAINT AND WAS ONE.** `latticeBoom` drew its top chord, bottom chord, diagonals and verticals in a single css, which is a drawing of a lattice rather than a lattice; a real one reads because its top chord is the only member turned up at the sky and its web is half in the shadow of the chords either side of it. It costs nothing — a stroke’s colour is a uniform on a quad the layer was already going to draw. ⚠ **And it takes the NIGHT, which it never did**: `movingBox` has always dimmed its faces by the hour and a stroke’s colour is a literal in the arm, so after dark a crane’s house went down with the rest of the city and the jib bolted to it stayed at full daylight yellow — one machine lit two ways, and the brightest unlit thing on the waterfront. ⚠ `cssShade` **refuses anything that is not `rgb()`/`rgba()`**, because a hex colour is digits too: `#ffcf3e` hands the scanner a `3` and comes back a different colour with no error anywhere. ⚠ **AND A CRANE MAST IS A LATTICE, WHICH THE ARM KNEW AND THE PALETTE DID NOT.** Reported as *"I can’t quite tell what this texture is supposed to be, these are usually metal beams"*, against a tall olive smudge. `ty_junk_crane` sat in `PLATE_WALL`, one line under a `draw3DBoxAt` whose own comment reads `// the lattice mast` — so the scrapyard derrick was painted by `matPlate`: plate patches, a rust bloom weighted to every perimeter, pitting across the joints. That is a fair hull and it is nothing at all on a member a fifth of a tile wide, where the plate grid is `max(5, W/2)` by `max(6, H/4)`: two plates across, each carrying a few dozen overlapping rust ellipses. What comes out is MOTTLE — it reads as camouflage, or as brick, or as nothing, and the one thing it does not read as is steel. `LATTICE_WALL` had been written for exactly this and says so in its own header (*"a gantry leg, A CRANE MAST, a fenced compound"*), and it paints the openings near-black with the chords, diagonals, ties and gusset plates as the only lit thing on the surface. ⚠ **AND THE SAME DEFECT WAS ON THE DOCK CRANE, WHERE IT NEEDED A NEW KEY RATHER THAN A MOVE.** `ty_wharf_steel` is the pedestal AND the tower, and those are two different objects: a pedestal is a squat machine base on the quay and genuinely is plate, while the tower above it is sixteen storeys tall and a sixth of a tile wide. So the tower takes `ty_wharf_lattice`, galvanised and a shade under the jib’s own `rgba(120,128,140)` so the boom still reads as the nearer member. **The general rule is that the family is a statement about the SURFACE and not about the material**: the same steel is plate on a hull, lattice on a mast, and `PLAIN_WALL` on a modern painted harbour crane — which is why `ty_crane_body`/`ty_crane_cw`/`ty_crane_cab` are deliberately left in the family with no pattern at all and must stay there. ⚠ **AND THE HOLES IN A LATTICE ARE HOLES NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.latticeCut`, 0 is the painted near-black openings exactly as they shipped).** `LATTICE_WALL`’s own header said *"there is no alpha here (a wall texture skins an opaque box), so the openings are painted as near-black"* — and the premise was never re-checked. Every wall texture in GLASS is a canvas that starts TRANSPARENT, `buildAtlas` composites tiles with `drawImage` onto a transparent page, and the mass shader samples that page: so leaving the openings unfilled and alpha-testing the sample is a truss you can see the sky through, in both renderers, with no second texture and no blend pass. ⚠ **AN ALPHA TEST AND NOT A BLEND, WHICH IS WHY IT IS THREE LINES**: a discarded fragment writes no depth, so the sun-shadow and SSAO prepasses — which run the SAME program, the only one in `gl/context.js` — inherit the holes for free and nothing about sorting changes. ⚠ What it costs is the FAR SIDE of the tower: `draw3DBoxAt` backface-culls, so a hole shows the world behind the mast rather than the members on its own far face. At a fifth of a tile that reads correctly; on a wide frame it would be a visible cheat. ⚠ And the box stays solid to everything not looking at it — the mesh, the CFIT sweep, the occluder field and the shadow all read geometry rather than the texture, so a lattice mast is still a thing you can fly into. ⚠ **THREE THINGS WOULD EACH HAVE SILENTLY FILLED THE HOLES BACK IN.** **The atlas skirt** draws each tile oversized behind itself and then the tile on top — opaque, the top copy hides the ghost; transparent, the stretched ghost shows through every opening, and what should be sky is a smeared second lattice (the cell is cleared between the two now, a no-op for every opaque tile in the city). **The night dim** is a whole-tile `rgba(0,0,0,0.40)` fill, which lays 40% black into every opening — the painted opening arriving by the back door at dusk — so a cut tile dims `source-atop`. And **the 2-D quad overlays**: the lit ramp, the face shade and the fog all fill the wall’s POLYGON, which does not know the texture over it has holes, and `sh` alone is up to 30% black. Canvas2d can only mask that with a scratch canvas and a `source-atop` per wall per frame, so a cut wall takes no overlay at all — a fallback-only loss, since GLASS 2 discards per fragment and lights the steel exactly as before. ⚠ The flat LOD branch is exempt because it paints no texture: a wall too small to blit has no holes to protect and must stay an opaque tone-matched fill, or a distant mast vanishes entirely. ⚠ **AND THE CUT CHANGED WHAT THE RIGHT PROPORTIONS ARE, WHICH IS THE PART THAT IS EASY TO MISS.** Painted dark, a fat member over a dark opening reads as a dark truss and the 16x32 floor was fine. Cut, the identical geometry reads as a SOLID COLUMN WITH PINHOLES: two chords, a full-width tie per bay and two 2px diagonals leave barely a third of a 16-texel section open, and a lattice whose openings are the minority is not a lattice. So it is the eighth `hires` family (four keys against METAL’s twenty-eight — measured in with `atlasfit` at texRes 1, 2 and 3, same page sizes and same band count), and the member widths are FRACTIONS OF THE SECTION rather than texel counts, with the web lighter than the chords — which is both what a truss is and what buys back the open area. ⚠ **AND IT IS A SUBSET OF THE FAMILY, BECAUSE ONE KEY IS TWO OBJECTS.** `ty_gantry` is a gantry LEG at `fh * 0.05` in one place and the Spire cooling plant’s DECK at `fh * 0.86` — nearly two tiles of solid slab the fans stand on — in another, and cut, that deck shows you the sky through the middle of a building. `LATTICE_CUT` is the three keys whose every call site is open structure; `ty_trm_gantry` is held out for the same reason and NOT because it has been checked and failed — it has not been checked, and shipping a see-through hole into Terminus on the strength of a family name is the mistake the subset exists to avoid. ⚠ The flag is BAKE-TIME and needs a reload to A/B, like `glBand` and `richKit`: the wall tiles are cached per palette and the atlas is packed from those, and neither key carries it.

⚠ **AND A BUILDING HAS FOUR SIDES (`npm run models:elevation`, the gate; section 1e of `derivedKit`).** Reported as *"boring boxy buildings"*, and `models:quality` could not see it: that board is 94% satisfied and every one of its four scores — a roof face, a light at night, more than one wall palette, any trim — is answered by the FRONT. The defect was never that a building has no detail. It is that all of it is on one of its four walls. Measured as trim area against wall area on each elevation: **front 54% · left flank 21% · right flank 9.5% · BACK 0.5%, with 174 of 227 models under 2% on the back.** `derivedKit`'s own section 1d says why in its opening line — everything above that point is placed on ONE plane, "so a building in Coldwater has a facade and three blank elevations, and a truck turning a corner drives past the back of a set". ⚠ **AND THE BACK IS NOT ONLY SEEN FROM THE AIR**, which is the assumption that let it sit: of the 203 building elevations in Coldwater that face an actual road tile, **58.1% are a front and the rest are a flank (18.7%), a back (8.4%) or a tile with no entrance recorded at all (14.8%)** — getting on for half of what a driver is looking at. ⚠ **A PIER RANK IS NOT "FACADE ON A FLANK", WHICH IS 1d's RULE AND IS RIGHT**: no shopfront, no awning, no name board, no glazing, because every one of those is derived from the ENTRANCE and a building has one of those. A pier is the structure showing — a long masonry wall is divided by piers because that is how it stands up, which is why a warehouse flank, a party wall and a shed all have them and none of them has a shopfront — and it is the only part in this vocabulary that changes a wall's SILHOUETTE rather than decorating its surface. ⚠ **NEVER LIT, AND THAT IS NOT THE COST TALKING**: a line of light up a fin is advertising, and the back of a building does not advertise. ⚠ **THE PITCH COMES OFF THE WALL AND NOT OFF THE WINDOW GRID** — 1b stands its fins at the glazing's own column boundaries precisely so the two cannot drift, and here there is no glazing to agree with, so the wall's own length is divided at the same bay the front grid uses. Fins stand at the INTERIOR bay boundaries, so a rank spans one bay LESS than its wall however the count comes out and can never overhang a corner. Back **0.5% → 25.9%** and bare backs **174 → 30**, for **+5,062 mesh faces (+9.4%)** — about 35 faces per building that gained a whole rear elevation. ⚠ **AND IT TURNED UP A LATENT NORMAL BUG IN THE PAINTER.** `emitFlat` takes the mesh normal off the polygon's own winding (Newell) and never flips it, so a quad wound for a +y wall claims a +y normal wherever it is put — INTO the building on a back wall, where GLASS 2 then shades it as a face turned away from the light. It was unreachable while section 1b was the only caller, because a front rank stands at `fy` and that is positive on a centred mass. Two of the four quads already disagreed with their own `cullN`: **the +x return has claimed a −x normal since the part was written**, on every building in the city. It is solved rather than reasoned through now — each quad is handed the direction it is meant to face and one function fixes the winding — because hand-deriving which of four quads in three planes to reverse is the sign argument this file keeps losing. ⚠ **THE GATE READS THE SAME FIELD THE RENDERER READS**, which is what caught that: the first run reported a rank of piers on the back as FRONT trim, because that is what the mesh said they were. ⚠ **AREA, NOT A FACE COUNT** — a glazing ribbon is twenty quads across one band and a plinth is one quad round a whole building — and a FLOOR rather than a band, far below where the medians sit, so retuning is free and only a change that takes an elevation back to bare trips it.

⚠ **GLASS can tilt now.** `makeCam` takes an optional **`camPitch`** (radians, positive tips the view down) — until it existed the optical axis was horizontal ALWAYS, so raising the eye was the only way to look down and a plan view was not expressible. The unpitched path is a SEPARATE CLOSURE rather than θ=0 through the pitched one, because `f·1 + u·0` is not the same floating-point expression as `f` and every view in the game goes through that function with no pixel coverage; `scripts/shapes/freecam.mjs` asserts the identity with Object.is. ⚠ **IT IS `camPitch` AND NEVER `pitch`, AND THAT COST A BROKEN FLIGHT SIM.** A view object here ALREADY has a `pitch` — the AIRCRAFT'S ATTITUDE, in DEGREES, which four other readers divide by 26 or multiply by π/180 — and all four cockpit callers pass it. `makeCam` read that field as radians for one afternoon, so every degree of climb tipped the camera fifty-seven degrees: the city hung upside down and the runway swung round like a billboard. `renderModelPreview` had already named its own field `camPitch` for exactly this reason and then handed it back as `pitch`, so the rename stopped one layer short of the place that mattered. `freecam.mjs` now asserts that a view carrying `pitch: 12` projects identically to one with no pitch at all — restoring the bug moves `sy` from 442 to 53,006. **Only the Modelshop preview passes a camera pitch today** — before the sim flies with one, the horizon (drawn as a horizontal line), the sky/ground fill (two rectangles split at `horizonY`) and the cloud/fog placement all have to learn that the horizon MOVES and at a large enough tilt leaves the canvas.

⚠ **AND THERE IS A SEA NOW, WITH A SURFACE AND A SHIP RIDING IT (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaLit`, `glSwell`, `glSeaState`; 0 on all three is the flat painted plane that shipped).** The water already had three phase-modulated sine trains, whitecaps, sun glitter, a moon path, a surf band and a rotor crater, and none of it was ever differentiated — `floor.js` said so in its own comment, *"water is flat and carries its own wave shading below"*. So the swell was a brightness multiplier on a plane: no normal, no specular, no silhouette, nothing riding it. `client/shared/sea-swell.js` is the one JS copy of the height field and `gl/sea-glsl.js` the one GLSL copy, read by BOTH `floor.js` and `water.js` so the surface being shaded and the surface being displaced cannot disagree; `scripts/shapes/sea.mjs` holds all three together and is in the `pretest:regress` chain AND `shapes:smoke`, per the two-lists warning above.

⚠ **THE SWELL THAT SHIPPED IS CHOP, AND MEASURING IT IS THE ONLY WAY TO FIND THAT OUT.** The three trains have wavelengths of **1.09, 1.20 and 0.60 tiles** — four to eight metres, which is wind chop. Lighting chop gives you a sparkling sea and not a rolling one, and no screenshot says which you are looking at. A fourth train at **8.6 tiles** carries the rolling read, and it is the only term a hull may ride: the other three are shorter than the Echelon's beam and would add noise to a rigid-body fit rather than motion.

⚠ **PARALLAX ON THE MODE-7 FLOOR IS NOT FEASIBLE, AND THE ARITHMETIC SAYS SO RATHER THAN TASTE.** The floor is one screen-filling triangle with the world point recovered per fragment by inverting the projection ASSUMING z = 0, so displacing it is a fixed-point iteration whose contraction factor is `(d/EH) · |grad H|`. `uEH` is the eye height in TILES and it is **0.12 for a truck cab**, against a `d` that runs past 40 — so the factor is **20 to 35**, and one iteration overshoots while two diverge. That is geometry and not tuning: Mode-7 makes the whole frame past the near rows grazing, where a 2 cm crest moves the intersection by metres. The replacement is a real mesh (`gl/water.js`), and the decision the whole cost rests on is that **the mesh displaces the 8.6-tile ROLL and the fragment shader keeps the ~1-tile chop** — a 0.6-tile wave needs about 13 vertices per tile against 3 for the swell, which is sixty times the grid for the thing a normal map is already for. ⚠ **The grid is STATIC**: it holds tile offsets from the camera's ground point and is built once, with the position, clock and sea state arriving as uniforms, so it is one draw call and no upload per frame.

⚠ **A TIGHT SPECULAR LOBE ON A FLAT SEA PUTS NOTHING ON THE WATER.** A near-level eye can only mirror light arriving within about twice the steepest facet, so at the amplitude that shipped (6.9°) the sea reflected the horizon and nothing else — correct, wired, and invisible. The exponent is DERIVED from the slope the amplitude produces, so roughening the sea widens its glitter by arithmetic rather than by a second number somebody has to remember to move. ⚠ **And the diffuse is measured against the FLAT sea, never against a constant**: a flat surface gets `dot(up, L)`, which is the light's elevation sine, so `(lam − elev)` is the facet's own deviation and is zero-mean at every hour — written against a literal it carries the sun's height as a brightness offset, and the whole sea drops about 9% at a low sun, which reads as "the evening sea is darker" rather than as a bug. ⚠ **Night moves 0% of the frame and that is physics, not a dead flag**: with the eye 0.12 tiles up and the moon 60° high the mirror point is about 0.07 tiles away, under the truck. A high light cannot glitter for a low viewer, and the existing `moon` path term is the game's deliberate non-physical answer for that.

⚠ **THE SEA READS THE WIND IN KNOTS, AND THE OBVIOUS SOURCE SATURATES AT FORCE 5.** `windFromView().fly` is `clamp(kt/18)` — the WINDSOCK's curve, which is right for a windsock and means every wind from a fresh breeze upward produces an identical sea. Sea state is integrated off knots instead, through JONSWAP fetch-limited growth (`Hs = 4·U·sqrt(1.6e-7·F/g)`), so significant wave height rises **0.29 m at Force 2 to 3.23 m at Force 10** with nothing flattening in the middle. It is driven by WIND and not by gloom, because fog is `WX_HAZE 0.75` and a foggy sea is glass. ⚠ **It needs a reservoir** (`SEA_RISE_S` 150, `SEA_FALL_S` 420, the exact-step form `ground-accum.js` uses), or a per-cell severity snaps as you fly across a squall boundary and reads as a rendering glitch rather than as weather — and the sea takes minutes to get up and longer to go down, which is what a sea does. ⚠ **The crests sharpen because the arithmetic does it**: the trains are Stokes 2nd-order (`a·sin θ − (ka²/2)·cos 2θ`), which gives a measured surface skewness of **0.154** at full sea state against observed seas at +0.1 to +0.3, where a plain sinusoid is 0. ⚠ **AND THE WHITECAP THRESHOLD IS INVERTED, NOT FITTED.** The shipped thresholds are ABSOLUTE (`wv > 0.78`), so scaling amplitude without scaling them makes a calm sea foam everywhere and a storm foam nowhere. The threshold is solved by quantiling the chop's OWN distribution to hit the coverage Monahan and O'Muircheartaigh measured (`W = 3.84e-6·U^3.41`), which tracks to **1.49 points across Force 0–10** and cannot drift when the swell is retuned.

⚠ **THE DECK HEIGHT WAS A CONSTANT WRITTEN DOWN TWICE, WHICH IS FINE FOR A SHIP THAT CANNOT MOVE.** `YACHT_DECK_Z` in windshield.js and `DECK_PAD_Z` in cockpit.js, the second of which even restated `YACHT_H` as a literal 1.7 with a comment saying they had to match. The moment the deck heaves, that is **a helicopter landing on a deck that is not there**. `DECK_PAD_Z` is deleted, `yachtPadZ()` is the one answer, and the gate fails if it comes back. ⚠ **A FITTED PLANE, NEVER THE SLOPE AT A POINT** — a 60 m hull spans a good part of an 8.6-tile swell, so she BRIDGES the wave rather than lying tangent to it, and that difference is most of what reads as a ship in a seaway rather than a decal on a curve; three samples under the bow and the two quarters give heave, pitch and roll, applied at `drawYacht`'s single `lift()` chokepoint so hull, superstructure, pad, rails, mast and lights move together. ⚠ **A hull takes up a FRACTION of the geometric wave slope** (the effective wave slope coefficient, naval architecture's own term) — at 1.0 she rolled 29.9°, which is a knockdown; at 0.55 with caps the gate measures **11.1° of roll and 11.0° of pitch at her worst in a gale**, and that figure is the one to quote because `sea.mjs` re-measures it on every push rather than remembering it. ⚠ **And the wake foam follows the surface**: every point was handed a flat `z = 0.003`, which on a displaced sea is foam hanging over a trough and buried under the next crest.

⚠ **THE PHYSICS MUST NOT DEPEND ON THE FLOOR HAVING DRAWN.** The first cut read the amplitude and clock from state `drawMode7Floor` publishes, and that function is never entered under the DOM stub — so the hull measured dead flat with every part of it correctly wired, which is indistinguishable from the feature not existing. The amplitude comes from the tune and the clock from `now`.

⚠ **`Date.now()` STRAIGHT INTO THE CLOCK FREEZES THE SEA ON THE GPU ONLY.** A GLSL uniform is a float32; at the roll's 0.31 rad/s, `Date.now()` is about **5.5e8 radians**, where float32 resolution is **64 radians** — ten whole waves between representable values. It would not drift or stutter, it would stop, while the JS riding it kept moving. `seaClock()` wraps at `2π/0.01 = 628.3185 s`, and the wrap is EXACT rather than tolerable because **every temporal frequency in the sea and the floor's water block is a multiple of 0.01 rad/s** — which is simply what people pick when picking by hand — so every train returns to precisely its starting phase and there is no discontinuity to blend. Measured: the surface at `t` and `t + 628.3 s` differs by **2.0e-14 tiles**. The one term that cannot join is the rotor downwash, whose frequency is a live control input; its phase steps once every ten and a half minutes under a hovering helicopter, over water already being churned into a crater. ⚠ **This is what makes the sea verifiable server-side for nothing** — it is a pure function of position and wall-clock time, so a server that wants to check a boat's pose calls the same module with no table, no tick and no egress. ⚠ **AND `seaScroll` HAD TO GO FIRST**: it offset the sampling position by an along-heading distance so the water appeared to stream past a ship pinned at screen centre, it was switched off at its only call site because *"it read as fake"*, and it stayed plumbed through four files to a permanently-zero uniform — the LAST per-client term in the sea, and reviving it now fails the build.

⚠ **TWO SHADER TRAPS, BOTH SILENT.** A **backtick inside a GLSL template literal** ends the string mid-shader — the house style quotes identifiers in backticks and the shaders are template literals, so this is the `cab-view.js` boot bug one layer down, and it cost four occurrences across three files. And **GLSL has no default arguments**: `seaSlope` gained a sixth parameter and `floor.js` went on passing five, so the floor shader silently would not compile, the pass failed safe to 0, and the software raster drew the flat sea underneath the GL water — which looks exactly like the new layer being subtly wrong rather than the old one being on. `gl:glsl` checks that every uniform a shader uses is declared and every name the linker is asked for exists; it checks NAMES and not ARITY, so the arity check is now part of `sea.mjs`.

⚠ **AND THE FRAME COST IS UNMEASURED, WHICH IS A STATEMENT AND NOT AN OMISSION.** The same config measured 2.8 ms and 0.9 ms on two consecutive runs, and this file's own rule is that a difference inside the run-to-run spread is not a finding. What IS measured is the picture: the lit term alone moves **22–38% of a cab frame** in daylight, strongest at a low sun, and the displaced mesh moves **13.4%** with the horizon no longer a ruled line. The instrument is `__glSea()` in the Modelshop with a pinned clock, pinned `Math.random` and an **ablation frame at flag 0** — "the two agree to 0.15%" is also exactly what comes back when the new path draws nothing — and it must be warmed once, because the first case's control measures 68% while the renderer builds every lazy cache it has.

⚠ **AND A CREST LIGHTS UP WITH THE SUN BEHIND IT (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaSss`, 0 is the sea as it shipped).** The green glow through the top of a wave is most of what separates water from a shiny surface, and it is the one thing a reflection and a specular lobe between them cannot say: both describe light coming OFF the surface and this is light that went THROUGH it. ⚠ **It is not real subsurface scattering and must not pretend to be** — real SSS needs a thickness to integrate through and this sea is an opaque skin over a height field, the same finding `matFrost` records one surface over. What IS expressible is the three things that decide when a crest lights: the sun in FRONT of you (`-dot(V, uSunDir3)`, and an `abs()` or a dropped minus would light crests with the sun over your shoulder and pass every other check here), the sun LOW (a steep sun goes down into the water rather than across through a crest toward a near-level eye), and a crest to light at all — the lift is `vH`, the mesh's own displacement, so a glass day contributes zero BY ARITHMETIC rather than by a guard. ⚠ **Green over blue over red, because water absorbs red first and blue second**: lifting green and blue together makes CYAN, and cyan along a crest reads as a neon tube laid on it rather than as light through water — measured on screen at gain 1.4 before it was fixed. ⚠ **AND THE DEFAULT WAS SET ON SCREEN, NOT IN THE SWEEP, BY MORE THAN A FACTOR OF TWO.** The pixel bench reads this term at 0.15–0.37% of the frame because it only ever lights sun-side crests inside the displaced patch, so "% of frame moved" is nearly blind to it: the arithmetic said 0.55, which cannot be seen at all, and 4 is a stain across the water. It ships at **1.2**.

⚠ **AND FOAM STAYS WHERE THE WATER WAS DISTURBED (`RENDER_TUNE.glFoam`, 0 is the sea exactly as it lies).** `seaWake` draws a bow crest and Kelvin arms from a hull's CURRENT position, so the whole wake is bolted to the boat and swings round with her; what a real wake leaves is foam on the water she has already crossed, which keeps going straight after she turns away. ⚠ **It is the SNOW TRACK STORE with a different decay law**, and the commit rules moved to [client/shared/trail-store.js](client/shared/trail-store.js) rather than being copied: a rut is buried by later snowfall and a wake disperses on its own, and everything else about laying a polyline behind a moving thing — the provisional head, the early bend, the creep floor, the second point opening on first movement, the spent point leaving — is identical and was a visible defect in the snow first. ⚠ **A WAKE IS ONE BAND, NEVER TWO RAILS**: the snow shader measures `abs(d - half)` because a vehicle has two contact patches with clean snow between them, and carrying that form across draws every boat's wake as a catamaran. ⚠ **And foam is the one part of this sea that nothing reads back** — no physics samples it, no server verifies it, no gate re-derives it — which is exactly why it is ALLOWED to be per-client, stateful and approximate while everything else here is a pure function of position and time.

⚠ **AND THE EXTRACTION IMMEDIATELY FOUND THAT TWO OF THOSE FIVE RULES HAD NEVER BEEN GATED.** Mutation-testing it through `gl:snow` caught the bend and left the provisional head and the eviction green — correctly, because that suite's claims are a gauge, a bend and a burial, which is the right list for snow and blind to the geometry underneath it. `npm run gl:trail` ([scripts/shapes/trail.mjs](scripts/shapes/trail.mjs), in the `pretest:regress` chain AND `shapes:smoke`) drives the pure module directly, **mutation-tested 7 of 7**. ⚠ Its bend case needed **a junction's radius rather than a motorway's** — a first cut swept 90° over six tiles, which is 8.25° of turn per committed step against a threshold of 8, so it sat on the knife edge and failed a rule that works. ⚠ And its creep case has to **drive in first**: the turn term is the angle behind the anchor, so with fewer than three stored points it is 0 and a shuffle from a standing start is held by that instead — deleting the creep floor leaves it green.

⚠ **THREE BUGS IN THAT ONE FEATURE, EACH OF WHICH LOOKED LIKE NOTHING.** ⚠ **`half` IS A RESERVED WORD IN GLSL ES** — the water shader never compiled, so the ENTIRE GL world pass fell back to 2-D, not just the water layer, with every headless gate green; `gl:glsl` compares uniform NAMES and never compiles anything. The tell was counting draw calls: **2 in a forty-frame sail**. It is a build failure now. ⚠ **AND IT WENT INTO THE WRONG PAYLOAD.** It first landed beside `tracks` in the GROUND state — a different object built in a different function — so `water.js` read `s.foam` as undefined and set a count of 0 every frame while the store filled correctly behind it. Two payloads in one file that both look like "the state the shaders read" is the whole trap, and everything measurable was right: the store filled, the upload returned 32 points with sane fades, the picture was empty. ⚠ `gl:opts` cannot see that class — it guards the install.js allowlist, one hop earlier. ⚠ **AND THE BENCH AGED THE TRAIL OUT WHILE PAINTING IT**: foam lives 40 s and the sail ran on the real clock at **29 seconds for sixty frames** on a 900×500 canvas, so the shot showed a correct trail at a tenth of its brightness. Measured once all three were fixed: **0.27% of the frame moves, peak 149 levels, against a 0.02% control** — a wake is a thin thing in a frame that is mostly sky.

⚠ **AND THE SEA IS A SPECTRUM NOW, NOT TWO FREQUENCIES (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaSpread`, 0 is the single-train sea, bit for bit).** A single train is a perfect corrugation: it repeats exactly every wavelength for ever, and the phase modulation that hides that at one-tile scales cannot at sixty-metre ones, because there the repeat is bigger than anything else in the frame. Each peak gains **two sidebands** and keeps its own numbers — `SEA_ROLL` and `SEA_WIND` are untouched, because the sea audio reads them for its LFO rates and that gate pins them. ⚠ **AN FFT WAS CONSIDERED AND REJECTED ON A CONSTRAINT RATHER THAN A COST**: a Tessendorf ocean produces a TEXTURE, and four things read this height field — the floor shader, the mesh's vertex shader, the ship's physics and the gate — two of them on the CPU. That means a 256² FFT in JS every frame, a GPU readback no server can do, or a second coarse copy that disagrees with the first. What is actually wanted from Tessendorf is the SPECTRUM, and a sum of trains drawn from one keeps it closed-form, differentiable, CPU-samplable and deterministic. Measured: **11.4% of a gale frame moves against a 0.02% control**.

⚠ **FOUR THINGS THAT BAND HAD TO GET RIGHT, AND THE LAST ONE DELETED A FEATURE.** ⚠ **The frequencies are SNAPPED to the 0.01 rad/s grid** — `seaClock` wraps at 2π/0.01 and is phase-exact only because every frequency in the sea is a multiple of it, so a physically-derived 0.2542 would put a discontinuity into the surface every ten and a half minutes, on every client at once. ⚠ **The wavenumbers come from the DISPERSION RELATION**, ω² = gk, or the sidebands drift through their peak at a rate no sea does. ⚠ **The spread is an ANGLE as well as a frequency**: two trains at different frequencies on one heading still make a corrugation, just a beating one, and it is only the crossing angle that stops crests staying parallel. ⚠ **AND THE BAND IS NORMALISED IN QUADRATURE, NOT LINEARLY**, which is the difference between spreading a spectrum and quietly turning the sea down: independent trains add as the root of the sum of squares, so a linear share hands back a sea **31% calmer at the same sea state**. ⚠ **That one bug grew a whole mechanism before it was found.** Under the linear share the surface skewness fell from 0.154 to 0.087 — below the +0.1 an observed wind sea carries, the crests quietly back to sinusoids — and a `SEA_SHARP` factor that scaled each component's Stokes correction back up fixed the number and was a fudge dressed as physics. With the energy preserved the peak keeps 96% of its height rather than 62%, and the skewness comes back to **0.146 on its own**, with nothing compensating for anything. The mechanism is gone. **A number that has to be corrected is usually a number that is wrong somewhere else.**

⚠ **AND THE SIDEBANDS ARE GENERATED FROM THE JS TABLE RATHER THAN RETYPED.** The peak trains are still a hand-written GLSL twin held to the original by a gate, and every one of those claims exists because two copies of a number drift; a band of six is six more chances, so `gl/sea-glsl.js` imports `client/shared/sea-swell.js` and interpolates. ⚠ **The emission check must look INSIDE each function, not over the string** — the slope generator emits the same sidebands as the height generator, so a band dropped from `seaRoll` is still found in `seaSlope` and a file-wide check passes. A surface displaced by two trains and lit as though it had three would slip past the analytic-slope check too, because both halves are self-consistent about a surface neither of them draws. ⚠ **And two of the band's own invariants are invisible to every aggregate**: letting the peak keep its full amplitude moves the RMS by 3.6%, inside any non-flaky tolerance, and giving every sideband the peak's own wavenumber changes **no statistic the suite measures at all**. Both are exact in the algebra and are checked there. Mutation-tested 6 of 6.

⚠ **AND TWO BENCHES WERE LYING BEFORE ANY OF THIS COULD BE MEASURED.** `__glSea` set `RENDER_TUNE.glSeaRoll` — **a key that no longer exists**, replaced by `glSeaState` when the sea went weather-driven, so it assigned fine and did nothing and half of what it claimed to measure was silently absent. And every sea bench was **measuring the tide**: `settleFade` pins `performance.now` while `seaClock` runs on `Date.now()` — which is the price of two players seeing one ocean — so the waves kept moving between the three paints and the control differed on **58.7% of pixels under a 17.6% signal**. Pinning both clocks took the noise floor to ~0.01%. ⚠ **`__glSeaShot` is the instrument that was missing all along**: every other one in that file returns a percentage, and "does this read as water" is not one. It paints one frame and LEAVES THE CANVAS UP, and its `sail` mode drives a boat over successive frames because foam is stateful and a single frame shows none of it. ⚠ **The camera IS the boat**, so a forward shot shows no trail at all however well it is working; and **the map is centred on her every frame**, so a half-and-half scene puts her permanently on the shoreline rather than sailing away from it. ⚠ **AND THE SEA'S FRAME COST IS BELOW THIS MACHINE'S NOISE FLOOR** (`__glSeaCost`): a GPU-floor sea frame is 1.5–1.9 ms at 640×360 and the three sea layers land at 0.15, **−0.29** and 0.15 ms against a run-to-run spread of 1.5 — a negative reading is the tell — and pushing to 2560×1440 with the whole frame water barely moved it. ⚠ **The control says the instrument works**, which is the only reason that reading is worth anything: `__glFloorCost` on the same page resolves the sea scene at 208 ms on GLASS 1 against 1.86 ms on the GPU floor, a 112× difference. So the height field could carry several times the trains it has — ⚠ but per-pixel trigonometry is exactly what an integrated GPU is worst at, and every number here is one discrete NVIDIA card.

⚠ **AND A HARBOUR IS FLAT NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaShelter`, 0 is the open sea everywhere).** Waves need distance to build, so water with land close upwind is flat however hard it is blowing — an offshore gale leaves the lee shore like glass and the windward side of the same water unlandable, and one number does both. ⚠ **NOTHING NEW IS MODELLED: `seaAmpsFor` ALREADY TAKES FETCH**, because JONSWAP *is* a fetch model, so a sheltered basin is that same physics asked a LOCAL question rather than a global one. Every water tile searches **40 tiles upwind** for land and the answer rides in `uLut1.a` — ⚠ **which was a constant 255, and is the only reason this is free**: the grid is already built, already uploaded and already sampled by both water shaders, so it costs one write per tile and one swizzle. ⚠ **The wind is in the cache key, QUANTISED to sixteen sectors** — shelter is a function of the wind as well as of the ground, and the wind turns continuously while this grid is rebuilt only when the window recentres, so keying on the raw bearing rebuilds it every frame. Measured in a channel at full gale: **4.7% of the frame moves, peak 212, against a 0.02% control**.

⚠ **AND THE FETCH REFERENCE IS DELIBERATELY NOT `SEA_FETCH_M`, WHICH THE FIRST CUT LEARNED THE HARD WAY.** The authored ocean fetch is 50 km and the whole map window is about 350 m — three orders of magnitude apart — so measured honestly against it **every tile the renderer can see comes out under 7% of the sea**, and the moment the search runs off the end it jumps to 100%. That is a hard line drawn across the water at exactly the search limit, which is worse than no shelter at all. The SHAPE is still JONSWAP's √fetch; the REFERENCE is the search itself, so shelter is 0 against a beach, about a fifth two tiles out and exactly 1 where the search finds nothing — continuous by construction, with no seam to hide. ⚠ **Upwind, never the nearest land**: a distance transform is cheaper, cacheable and wind-independent, and it makes a bay calm whichever way the wind blows, which is the one thing this exists to get right. ⚠ **Running off the grid is OPEN WATER, not land**, or every tile within a march of the window's own edge is sheltered by nothing — a calm band round the whole frame that moves with the camera. ⚠ **And it scales the SWELL and never the chop**: a harbour is flat, not glass, because short wind waves are generated locally over metres and are there even in a dock.

⚠ **AND SHE ARRIVES LATE (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaInertia`, 0 is the kinematic fit that shipped).** A hull that matched the fitted plane exactly is a hull with no mass — it snaps to every crest and is level again the instant the crest has gone. ⚠ **THE FIRST VERSION WAS A STATEFUL SPRING AND IT WAS WRONG, WHICH THREE GATE CLAIMS SAID AT ONCE.** A second-order filter driven toward the kinematic answer is the obvious way to write "she has mass", and state stepped by the WALL clock makes the answer a function of when you asked rather than of where and when you asked ABOUT — and `yachtRide` is also a POSITIONAL QUERY: the drawn deck, the helipad and the telescope mount all ask it about different points and headings. A single integrated attitude collapses all of them onto one number, so the deck stopped heaving over time, stopped differing across eight tiles of sea, and stopped caring which way she was pointing, while looking entirely plausible. ⚠ **SO THE LAG IS TAKEN IN TIME INSTEAD**: the fit is evaluated at three instants and averaged, because the response to a delayed signal is the delayed signal — a weighted sum of past fits is a genuine low-pass with a genuine phase lag and stays a pure function of (position, heading, time). Two clients agree EXACTLY rather than converging, the sea stays server-verifiable, and `yachtPadZ` stays the pure function the helicopter landing reads. ⚠ **The taps sum to one**, which is what keeps a glass day exactly flat rather than nearly. Measured: **she trails the water by 0.70 s and takes up 91% of its movement** — trailing AND smoothing, not a pure delay. ⚠ **What it deliberately has no room for is RESONANCE**: a real hull rolls FURTHER than the wave slope when a beam sea matches her natural period, which is why a beam sea is the dangerous one, and a short symmetric filter cannot have a resonant peak. That needs either state, which costs the determinism above, or a per-train **RAO** (naval architecture's Response Amplitude Operator — a gain and a phase per frequency), which needs the fit decomposed by train rather than sampled as three heights. That is the honest way to add it and it is a different shape of change, not a bigger number here.

⚠ **AND SURF TURNS TO FACE THE BEACH (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaShoal`, 0 is the sea as it shipped) — BUILT, GATED, AND MEASURED INVISIBLE.** Waves slow as the water shallows, so the end of a crest that reaches the shallows first falls behind and the whole crest swings round until it is parallel with the shore. ⚠ **IT IS A DOMAIN WARP AND NOT A ROTATED WAVEVECTOR, WHICH IS THE ONLY REASON IT IS EXPRESSIBLE HERE.** The obvious way to write refraction is to turn each train's `k` toward the shore normal — and the phase of a plane wave is `k·x`, so the moment `k` varies with position the honest phase is `∫k·dx` and `k·x` is no longer a wave: crests stop joining up, and the analytic slope that makes lighting this sea affordable stops being analytic. Warping the SAMPLING POSITION keeps a genuine scalar phase field, and the turn falls out of it: with `p' = p + t·g(d)` the effective wavevector is `k·u + k·(u·t)·∇g`, so a train running obliquely picks up exactly the shoreward component refraction predicts and one already square-on is left alone. Measured: **an oblique crest turns from 44.2° to 38.9° off the shore normal**. ⚠ **A warp of the plane onto itself must be INJECTIVE or it is a fold** — `coastWarp` shipped exactly that bug, a Jacobian determinant of −2.13 that drew a row of cusps along every shoreline and rendered a pond as a starburst — and this one stays at 0.975, with the gate finding the fold threshold between strength 3 and 4. ⚠ **AND THE SLOPE IS TAKEN IN THE WARPED FRAME, which is an approximation and is stated rather than hidden**: the true gradient is the warped one carried back through that Jacobian, so the lighting is off by order `|∇g|` — about a fifth, tangentially, inside the shoal band only. Carrying it properly needs second derivatives of the tile grid, which is a noisy thing to ask a texture for.

⚠ **AND ITS HONEST MEASUREMENT IS THAT THE PIXELS DO NOT MOVE.** At every injective strength, on every seat tried, it lands **at or below the noise floor** — 0.02% moved against a 0.02% control. The maths is right and mutation-tested and the warp only ever touches a two-to-four-tile band at the waterline seen edge-on, which is a small part of any frame. It ships at 0.55 because it is physically right and costs nothing, and this paragraph exists so nobody has to rediscover that they cannot see it.

⚠ **AND TWO OF ITS GATE CLAIMS WERE TESTING THEMSELVES, WHICH IS THE MORE USEFUL LESSON.** The injectivity check ran against a STRAIGHT shore, where the displacement is along a fixed tangent whose magnitude varies only across it — so the Jacobian is a pure shear with determinant exactly 1, **for any strength, by construction**. It reported a confident 1.000 and could not have failed; a fold needs the tangent itself to rotate, which is what a curve does, so it runs round a round island now. And the sign check **reimplemented the warp in JS and then asserted things about the reimplementation**, so flipping the tangent in the GLSL — the exact bug it exists for — changed nothing and it stayed green. It reads the sign out of the shader now. ⚠ **A gate that rebuilds its subject is testing itself**, and both of these looked like ordinary passing claims.

⚠ **AND A STALE FOAM TRAIL POISONS EVERY MEASUREMENT TAKEN AFTER IT (`foamReset`).** The noise floor on a scene with no boat in it went from 0.02% to **4.53%**, and the cause was `glFoam`: foam is the one thing in this sea with MEMORY, the store is per-session, and it ages on the wall clock — so a shot painted minutes after a sail still carried that boat's wake, decaying by a different amount between each pair of paints. Every reading taken in that window was measured against it, including the ones that first suggested refraction was invisible. Harmless in the game, where the world is continuous and the trail is the trail; poisonous to a harness that paints unrelated scenes back to back, which is why `__glSeaShot` now clears it at the top of every shot.

⚠ **AND THE CHOP HAS AN EDGE NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaChopR`, 0 is the sea as it shipped — chop lit but flat at every range).** The chop had always been a NORMAL and never geometry, which is the honest split at distance and **the single biggest thing between this sea and a spectral one up close**: a normal map lights a surface that is still flat, so a crest a metre from the hull has no edge, occludes nothing and does not break the horizon. Within a few tiles that is the difference between water and a photograph of water. Measured: **4.6% of a cab frame and 4.0% of a helm frame move against a 0.01% control** — two hundred times the noise floor, and the first thing in this sea whose visibility was never in question. ⚠ **IT IS TAPERED HARD AND THAT IS THE WHOLE COST STORY**: carrying a 0.6-tile wave across the whole patch is the sixty-times-the-vertices arithmetic this file opens with, so it is displaced over six tiles at the eye and faded out, and past that the normal carries it exactly as it always did. ⚠ **The taper is not optional** — the floor paints the same sea past the patch and displaces nothing, so chop carried at full height to the rim is a step in the surface at a fixed distance from the eye: a ring of broken water that follows the camera, which is worse than the flat chop it replaced. ⚠ **AND THE GRID HAS TO RESOLVE WHAT IT DISPLACES**, which is arithmetic rather than taste: a sine needs about five samples a wavelength before it stops being a wave and becomes a zigzag, the shortest chop train is 0.60 tiles, and `PATCH_PER_TILE` went 5 → 9 to carry it at 5.4. Displacing what the grid cannot carry does not read as rougher water, it reads as torn geometry — and it is silent, because the LIGHTING goes on describing a smooth wave over the top of it. ⚠ **The lighting needed no change at all**, which is what makes this cheap rather than a second seam: the fragment normal comes from the same height field either way, so it describes the chop whether or not the mesh has also moved to it. ⚠ **And 3.2× the vertices measured free** — 64k against 20k, and the sea's own cost stayed at 0.14, 0.38 and −0.13 ms across three seats against a 1.5 ms run-to-run spread, which is this bench saying it cannot see it.

⚠ **AND THE GATE CLAIM THAT FORBADE IT WAS NARROWED RATHER THAN DELETED.** `sea.mjs` had asserted since the mesh shipped that the vertex shader must NEVER call `seaChop` — and that is still true of the WHOLE patch, for exactly the reason it was written. What is permitted now is the chop displaced near the eye and FADED OUT, so the claim tests for the taper rather than for the call, and an untapered `seaChop` in the vertex shader is still the original bug and still fails. ⚠ **A rule that a new feature contradicts is a decision to re-make in the open, not a line to delete** — the gate caught this the moment the feature landed, which is the whole reason it was worth writing down the first time.

⚠ **AND FOAM KEYED ON STEEPNESS WAS CONSIDERED, MEASURED AND NOT BUILT.** Whitecaps threshold on the chop's HEIGHT, and the obvious criticism is that real breaking is about steepness rather than height — so the obvious fix is to key them on `|grad h|`, which the lighting already computes. Measured over 20,000 samples, the correlation between chop height and chop steepness here is **−0.06**: they are independent, and the sign is the clue. At a crest the slope is ZERO, so foam keyed on slope lands on the FLANKS and nowhere near the breaking crest — a change that is easy to justify in a sentence and visibly worse. What actually separates a breaking crest from a gentle one is CURVATURE, a second derivative, which is closed-form here but is a real piece of work for a subtle gain. Recorded so the same sentence does not get acted on next time.

⚠ **AND THE HYDRO HAD QUIETLY STOPPED FLYING, WHICH NOBODY EDITED.** The race boat gets air off a crest — past a threshold the water lets go, it flies, and it lands on whatever is there now, with a bad landing scrubbing speed and a beam-on one breaking the hull. Measured over two minutes at full throttle head into a **45-knot gale, the top of the entire sea scale: ZERO launches**, at every sea state the weather can produce. `launchVs` and the `kick > 0.25` bar were fitted when the swell was a FIXED roll 0.32 / wind 0.20 topping out near 28° of face; JONSWAP then made the amplitudes a function of the wind, and a wind-derived sea is about half as big — 16.1° at `SEA_FULL_KT`, and it would take about **95 knots** to reach roll 0.32. ⚠ **A number fitted against another system's OUTPUT goes stale when that system is retuned, without anybody touching it** — the wall wash's gain one system over, in the same words — and this one goes stale SILENTLY, because a boat that never leaves the water looks exactly like a boat being driven carefully. ⚠ **AND THE THRESHOLD IS NOT THE KNOB, WHICH IS THE PART WORTH KEEPING**: dropping the bar is the obvious repair and the launch speed IS the kick (`s.vs = kick`), so a lower bar buys more launches at the same negligible height — swept to 0.03 the hull left the water **357 times a minute and never cleared 0.011 tiles**, which is a skitter rather than a jump. `launchVs` 0.55 → **0.9** reproduces the authored feel against the sea that actually ships: 13.5 launches a minute against the 13.0 the old number gave on its own sea, peak 0.033 tiles against 0.036, airborne 7.9% against 7.8% — re-derived rather than picked. ⚠ **Section 21 of `sea.mjs` now holds the BOAT against the SEA**, reading `seaAmpsFor`, `glSeaGain` and `SEA_FULL_KT` out of the sources rather than restating them, and asserting HEIGHT as well as occurrence plus a flat-calm control — mutation-tested 3 of 3, where restoring the stale 0.55 reproduces the original bug exactly.

⚠ **AND THE CITY LIES ON THE WATER NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaNeon`, 0 is the sea as it shipped).** A harbour at night was **black under a full neon skyline**, with the planar mirror working perfectly — measured at **0.70% of the frame at a peak of 15/255**, against the same mirror pass moving **20.1% of a wet road**. The mirror is the city's IMAGE, which broken water mostly destroys; what actually reads as a harbour is its LIGHT, smeared into a streak down the water toward you, and nothing in this renderer drew one: the per-light smear in floor.js is gated `uWet > 0.001 && pavedW > 0.02`, which is to say on RAIN, on TARMAC, and over a clear-night bay both are zero. Measured with it: **23.8% of a night cab frame**. ⚠ **IT IS NOT THE TARMAC SMEAR WITH THE GATE SWAPPED.** That one is two hand-fitted Gaussians in the eye frame with a length somebody chose, which is a fair model of a rough opaque surface. Water has a measured answer: the size and shape of a glitter path IS the slope distribution of the surface, so the streak is the PROBABILITY that a patch happens to be tilted far enough to bounce that light into your eye — **Cox & Munk 1954** (JOSA 44:838), who flew over the Pacific, photographed the sun's glitter and inverted its shape to get the statistics, which is this calculation run backwards. Near-Gaussian and ANISOTROPIC (σ²ᵤ = 3.16e-3·U along the wind, σ²_c = 3.0e-3 + 1.92e-3·U across it), which is why a real glitter path is an ellipse stretched downwind. ⚠ **THE WIND IS ALREADY INTEGRATED**, so a glass harbour gives a tight bright reflection and a gale a long dim smear with nothing authored and no second knob — measured, glass moves 2.7% of the frame at a HIGHER mean than the gale's 26.6%, which is small-and-bright against broad-and-dim, the physics reading correctly. ⚠ **AND USING THE REAL OCEAN'S VARIANCE IS CORRECT RATHER THAN OPTIMISTIC**: Cox & Munk measured everything down to the capillary ripples, and the capillary ripples are exactly what this renderer does not carry — the mesh resolves the roll, the fragment normal carries the chop, and below half a tile there is nothing — so a statistical glitter model is how the unresolved half of the spectrum is meant to be represented. ⚠ **BOTH AXES TAKE THE CLEAN-SURFACE INTERCEPT, a departure from the published fit and deliberate**: the along-wind regression comes out with an intercept near zero, which says a windless sea is a PERFECT MIRROR along one axis and rough across it — at 0 kt that gives an anisotropy of 0.18, a path stretched ACROSS the wind, and a peak twenty-two times the reference. Above about Force 3 the two forms agree to a per cent.

⚠ **FOUR WAYS IT MEASURED ZERO WHILE BEING CORRECTLY WIRED, AND EACH ONE LOOKS LIKE A FEATURE THAT DOES NOTHING.** ⚠ **`rgbRaw`, NEVER `rgb`** — the third reader to need that sentence after the wet road and the wet specular: `rgb` is the colour AS A WALL WASH, scaled by that term's own gain and by the night, and on the bench every one of the sixteen chosen lights carried a real `rgbRaw` and a flat **[0,0,0] `rgb`**. ⚠ **THE WALL WASH'S REACH IS A HARD CUT AND A GLITTER STREAK'S WHOLE NATURE IS TO RUN FAR FROM ITS SOURCE** — copied across, it zeroed every light more than 2.2 reaches from the water it was lighting, which in a harbour is all of them; it is `1/(1 + (d/r)²)` now, an inverse square softened at the origin, so a distant sign is dimmer and never absent. ⚠ **THE MESH IS NOT DRAWN AT ALL WHEN THE SWELL IS ZERO** (`water.js`'s own "no swell, no mesh"), so the glitter had to go in `floor.js` too — a glassy sea is the one case where a reflection matters most and the one case the mesh cannot reach; the floor also carries every tile past the 14-tile patch. ⚠ **AND THE BENCH HAD NOTHING TO REFLECT**: `runSeaShot`'s coast is PARKLAND, so the mirror buffer is empty and `glMirror: 0` and `glSeaRefl: 32` come back **identical** — true, and silent about the feature. It takes `city: true` now, and ⚠ the city stands ON THE QUAY rather than set back, because a reflection needs the light and the water to be the same place: six tiles of beach between them put every streak in the last few rows before the horizon and the measurement read it as an effect that does not reach the viewer.

⚠ **AND ONE HYPOTHESIS THAT WAS WRONG, RECORDED BECAUSE THE COMMENT INVITES IT.** `mirror.js` clears texture unit 1 before its prepass with a long ⚠ about `ground.js` leaving `uRefl` bound there and every draw failing `GL_INVALID_OPERATION` — and `water.js` binds the same buffer on **unit 3** and leaves it bound, which reads as the identical bug one unit over. It is not: a feedback loop is only formed when the texture is bound to a unit an ACTIVE SAMPLER of the current program references, and the mirror pass's own shaders sample nothing on 3. Measured over five consecutive frames with every draw call checked: **0 failed draws, mirror buffer stable at 12.84% lit**. One run settled it.

⚠ **AND THE WATER CLOSES OVER THE CAMERA (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaDunk`, 0 is the renderer as it shipped; `glSeaUnderExt` is the extinction strength).** Every seat is floored at **0.05 tiles above MEAN sea level** (`Math.max(0.05, …)` in windshield.js) and a crest reaches 0.099 tiles at 10 knots, 0.199 at 22 and **0.404 at `SEA_FULL_KT`** — so from about Force 4 a low camera is ducked by up to eight times its own height above the mean, and measured over a long sweep a camera sitting at that floor in a 45-knot sea is **under the surface 36.3% of the time**. Above 22 knots a crest tops even a FIXED bridge eye at 0.115; the Echelon's rides, so she clears, and a chase seat or free camera near the water does not. ⚠ **NOTHING ANYWHERE TESTED FOR IT**, and the failure is worse than a missing effect: the mesh is drawn two-sided and depth-writing (`disable(CULL_FACE)`, so a trough seen from a crest shows the far side of the wave), so the surface overhead was ALREADY being drawn — wearing the sky reflection, the sun glitter and the whitecaps it wears from above, under an air sky with air fog. Not a gap, a surface confidently shaded for the wrong side. Measured with the flag: **33.4% of a submerged frame moves at a mean of 119**, against **0.03% above water on a 0.06% control** — provably inert when you are dry.

⚠ **SNELL'S WINDOW IS THE ONE THING THAT SAYS UNDERWATER ON ITS OWN, AND IT IS ONE DOT PRODUCT.** Looking up from inside water the whole sky is refracted into a cone of half-angle 48.6° (`asin(1/1.33)`); outside that cone nothing can get in at all and the surface turns into a MIRROR by total internal reflection. So there is a bright disc of daylight overhead with a dark mirrored ring round it and a sharp edge between them — `cos(48.6°) = 0.661` against a dot the fragment already has, written as the cosine because an `acos` would buy nothing. ⚠ **AND EXTINCTION IS WAVELENGTH-DEPENDENT, WHICH IS MOST OF THE REST OF THE LOOK**: water absorbs red first, green next and blue last, so anything past a metre or two goes blue-green and then simply goes.
