Written as a grey fog it reads as smoke; written per channel it reads as water — one `exp()` a channel. ⚠ **The structural limit is solved by the physics rather than worked around**: `drawMode7Floor` inverts the projection assuming z = 0 with the eye ABOVE, so it cannot draw a surface overhead at all, and past the 14-tile mesh patch there is no surface geometry over your head. Real underwater visibility is 10–30 m, which is 1.5–4 tiles — **well inside the patch** — so heavy extinction hides exactly the region that has no geometry, and the correct-looking answer and the affordable one are the same answer.

⚠ **THREE WAYS IT MEASURED ZERO OR BROKE WHILE LOOKING CORRECT.** ⚠ **THE GATE WAS ON `v.biomeBelow`, WHICH IS A CALLER'S STRING** — the helm view sets it, the cockpit does not and no bench sets it at all — so the whole feature moved **0.00% of the frame** and read exactly like a shader doing nothing. It reads the LUT's own waterness at the camera tile now (`LUT[y][x][3]`, the per-tile material grid the floor and both water shaders already sample), which is the same source the picture comes from; ⚠ and `LUT` starts `null` and is built conditionally, so it is guarded rather than indexed. ⚠ **THE FIRST PLACEMENT WAS IN A `const`'s TEMPORAL DEAD ZONE** — the submersion was computed beside the fog, sixty lines above `const t = seaClock(…)`, which throws `ReferenceError` on EVERY frame; this pass catches, logs once and falls back to 2-D, so the whole GL renderer would have gone off with one line in the console. Same shape as the `cam.ox` bug already recorded here. ⚠ **AND THE TWO QUESTIONS ARE NOT ONE QUESTION**: "is the CAMERA under" is one scalar and decides how much water you are looking through; "is THIS fragment of surface seen from below" is `gl_FrontFacing` and decides whether this piece of surface is a ceiling. The frame that matters most is the eye AT the waterline with a crest between it and the horizon — half that surface is over you and half is still in front of you, and a single camera-wide switch shades half of it wrong.

⚠ **AND COMING BACK UP LEAVES THE GLASS RUNNING.** A dunk is over in under a second and the thing that says it HAPPENED is what is left on the glass afterwards; without it the sea closes over the camera and then is simply not there any more, which reads as a glitch rather than as having been under. ⚠ **NOTHING NEW DRAWS IT** — this file already has thirty beads with a life, a run, gravity, a slipstream flatten and a wiper that erases them, all of it gated on the WEATHER being rain or storm, so a dunk is the same wet glass arriving by another route and opens that same branch. ⚠ **IT IS STATE, WHICH EVERY OTHER PART OF THIS SEA REFUSES TO BE** — deliberately, and on the foam trail's argument: the surface is a pure function of position and time because two clients and a server have to agree about where a crest is, and water on the LENS is not the sea. Nothing reads it back, nothing verifies it and nothing derives from it, which is exactly what makes it the one part allowed to remember something. ⚠ **AND THE BEADS ARE SEEDED FAT AND ALREADY RUNNING**: a rain bead starts at full life and has to grow heavy before it lets go — `life` falls about 0.01 a frame — so a pane seeded the way rain seeds it showed **NOTHING for the first twenty frames after surfacing and only started running at sixty**. A dunk puts the water on already heavy. Measured as an arc: **1.16% of the frame at the moment of surfacing, 2.93% at 1.3 s, 0.95% and a peak of 22 at 7.3 s** — wets, runs, clears. ⚠ **AND THE SCENE STATE IS CACHED ON THE CANVAS ID**, so an A/B run twice on one canvas has the flag-OFF run inheriting the flag-ON run's wet glass — the stale-foam-trail trap, one system over, and it reported 0.00% at every delay until each run got its own id.

⚠ **AND THE BENCH NEEDED A CLOCK BEFORE ANY OF IT COULD BE MEASURED (`clockMs` on `__glSeaShot`).** The wave phase is a property of the clock and every bench here pins it, which is right for everything that measures a POSE and useless for anything that HAPPENS: at the default instant the water at the shot position is in a trough, and **no offset inside a valid map box puts the eye under**. The dunk is a temporal event, so the instant is a parameter — the same way `snowForce` is a depth. The deepest instant for the helm seat was found by sweeping the clock: **0.296 tiles under at `Date.now = 1750000743600`**.

⚠ **AND A STREET LAMP LIGHTS THE ROAD NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glPool`, 0 is the street as it shipped in clear air — see the fog note below).** Reported as street lights being *"a bit harsh, almost a ball of bright light instead of spreading out a more gradual cone around their area"*, and both halves of that are arithmetic rather than taste. ⚠ **A LAMP LIT NOTHING AT ALL, WHICH IS EASY TO MISS BECAUSE THE PICTURE LOOKS DELIBERATE.** Every other thing a light does to the ground in GLASS is SPECULAR — the wet streak in `ground.js`, the glint, the mirror — and all three are gated on water, so on a dry night there was no term anywhere putting a lamp's light on the tarmac under it; what you were looking at was a disc over a road that had never heard of it. `uPool` is the diffuse half, and ⚠ **it is the SAME six lights the wet road already ranks and uploads**, read a second way: those terms ask what the surface bounces at your eye and this asks what lands on it, so a lit doorway spills onto its pavement and a neon sign lays a wash on the road under it with nothing new collected and nothing authored. ⚠ **IT IS NOT THE WALL WASH WITH THE NORMAL TURNED OVER**, which is the thing this renderer has reverted five times (`LIGHT_TUNE.gain` is parked at 0): that one flattened facades and drowned their own lettering, because a wall is the SUBJECT of the shot and a light across it competes with everything drawn on it — tarmac is the one surface in a night city whose whole job is to carry the pool a lamp throws on it, and there is nothing on a road for it to drown. ⚠ **THE SHAPE IS THE IRRADIANCE AND NOT A GAUSSIAN SOMEBODY FITTED**: a point source at height h over a plane lands `cos θ / r²` on it, which normalised to 1 directly beneath is `h³/(d² + h²)^1.5`, and that is the whole of why it reads as a lamp rather than as a bigger disc — **it has no edge anywhere**, 0.35 of its peak a lamp-height out and 0.014 at four times that, so it terminates on its own and needs no cut-off to hide. ⚠ **AND THE HEIGHT IS THE LIGHT'S OWN**, which is what makes it derived rather than tuned: `uWetP[i].z` is already uploaded and the wet streak already reads it for exactly this reason (*"a sign three storeys up throws further than a kerb lamp"*), so a street lamp lays a pool about its own height across and a floodlight up a tower a broad one, from one expression. ⚠ **The floor under that height is not cosmetic** — a light sitting ON the ground has h ≈ 0 and `h³/r³` at d → 0 is a singularity, which is a white pinhole on the tarmac and a worse ball than the one this removes. ⚠ **AND IT ADDS INTO THE HEADROOM RATHER THAN ONTO THE ROAD**: `c + k` saturates a painted line to white long before it saturates the tarmac beside it, so a pool crossing a road marking blows the line out and leaves a hard white bar across the middle of it — the one place a marking is most needed.

⚠ **THE SECOND HALF IS THE FLOOR UNDER A GLOW'S RADIUS, AND IT IS WHY A ROW OF LAMPS WAS ONE BALL.** `glowPool` draws `clamp(s0 / f, 3, 60)` pixels, so past four tiles a lamp stops shrinking and sits at three pixels however far away it is — while its peak alpha stays exactly what it is at arm's length. At sixteen tiles the disc is drawn **sixteen times the area it should cover, at full strength**. That is survivable for a building glow, which is one light on one facade; a street lamp is the only light in the city that appears in a RECEDING ROW, so a dozen of them land within a few pixels of each other at the end of a street and composite into a single saturated blob with a dark road underneath. The alpha now carries what the radius could not, and ⚠ **it is FLOORED rather than taken to nothing** — strict conservation puts a lamp at sixteen tiles on 6% of its strength, and a street light at the far end of a road is a thing you can see. Its peak also came down (`LAMP_HALO_A`), because `0.42 + night·0.4` was **0.82 at midnight against 0.70 for the brightest building glow in the city**, on the one light that is also drawn twenty to a street. ⚠ **AND THE LAMP'S SECOND DISC IS GONE WITH THE POOL ON**: it sat at z 0.01, on the tarmac, a screen-space CIRCLE standing in for light lying on a plane seen almost edge-on — which is the shape the whole complaint is about — and keeping it as well would be two owners of one pool, the worse because it enters the light list at GROUND height where the irradiance term's own floor is all that stands between it and that pinhole. At `glPool` 0 it comes back, so the 2-D fallback (which has no shader pool) is unchanged.

⚠ **AND THE GROUND SHADER HAD NEVER SEEN A LIGHT ON A DRY NIGHT, WHICH IS A SEPARATE FIX RIDING IN THE SAME COMMIT.** The list was handed over on `opts.wet > 0` alone — the ONLY hand-off it has — so a clear night uploaded `uNWet` 0 and **every loop in that shader broke on its first iteration**. The reflections did not care (they are gated on `refl`, which is `uWet` times a coverage and is exactly 0 on a dry road), but two terms that are NOT about water were dying with them: the pool, and **the volumetric light shaft, which is gated on HAZE** — so a fog that came without rain had no cones in it at all, with a slider, a uniform and a loop all correct. Turning `glPool` off does not put that back, deliberately, which is the one place that flag's 0 is not the renderer as it shipped.

⚠ **AND `__street` CANNOT MEASURE ANY OF IT, WHICH IS ITS OWN RULE AND WORTH RESTATING.** A first attempt to A/B the gain there came back with the control — the same setting rendered twice — moving **5.47% of the frame against an effect of 3.93%**; that file freezes no clock and pins no dial on purpose, so it is the instrument for the PICTURE and never for the number. `__glPool()` in the Modelshop is the number and its own control reads 0.00%: the frame moves 0.09% at gain 0.4, **1.46% at the 1.2 that ships** and 4.17% at 2.4, with the ROAD's own flatness — what share of its pixels sit inside eight levels of each other — holding at 58–62% the whole way, which is what says the pools are still pools and not a uniform lift. ⚠ **`peak` is the other half of that reading and is FLAT at 71 from 0.4 to 1.8**, because the term adds into the headroom: **a gain that raises the peak without raising `moved` is growing the ball back**. The 13:00 rows read 0.00% at every gain, which is the control that matters — the list carries no night term by design, so `uNight` is the only thing between this and the pink-cast-at-midday bug the wall wash had. ⚠ **AND A SYNTHETIC STREET MISLED THE FIRST DIAGNOSIS**: `glbench`'s scene has a white blob at its own vanishing point that is there **with the lamps deleted**, and it was read as the reported ball for most of an hour. ⚠ **Every lamp in the baked flight snapshot is OFF** — `sl` comes from `furniture.light_on`, which the content import deliberately leaves at 0 — so 84 lamp posts render with no lamps in them and `__street`'s `cells` patch is how you light them for a frame. Gated headlessly by `npm run gl:pool` ([scripts/shapes/lamppool.mjs](scripts/shapes/lamppool.mjs), in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in `shapes:smoke`, per the two-lists warning above), mutation-tested **13 of 13**.

⚠ **AND A LIGHTNING STRIKE IS A TREE THAT LIGHTS THE SCENE AROUND IT (`RENDER_TUNE.boltDetail` 0–1, `boltForce` the bench seam).** The server has been the single strike authority since `stormTick` shipped and none of that changed; what changed is everything downstream. **THERE WERE THREE GENERATORS AND THEY WERE THREE DIFFERENT PHENOMENA** — windshield.js grew one for the world channel and a second for the on-glass one, hangar-ambience.js a third for the doorway, each a nine-ish-node zigzag with its own jitter constant, width and colour, so a storm looked like one thing through the canopy, a slightly different thing on the glass in front of it, and a third thing from the hangar. One channel in `client/shared/lightning.js`, one painter in `lightning-draw.js`, three readers that differ only in how they project it. **The channel is RECURSIVE** — four generations, ~20 branches, each thinner and fainter — and three rules stop it reading as a bush: a branch carries a bearing away from its parent and HOLDS it (re-jittering about the vertical gives every fork the trunk's own shape at a shorter length); ⚠ **a child whose bearing points back at the channel is MIRRORED about the axis**, because a pure rotation turns half of them round and the tips hook home, which in a still frame reads as legs; and ⚠ **a branch stops in the air where the channel does not** — left to ground freely it is 4.7 arrivals a bolt and the bottom of every strike is a bush. ⚠ **AND HITTING THAT FLOOR HAS TO END THE BRANCH, not just clamp it**: `f <= 0` was the only stop, so a clamped branch carried on at a CONSTANT HEIGHT for the rest of its length, and a run of nodes at one height projects to a hard white bar lying across the road. **Brightness is a RESTRIKE SCHEDULE rather than a fade** — a return stroke and two or three more up the same channel — and ⚠ **a later stroke barely touches the side branches**, which belong to the stepped leader, so the channel stammers while the tree behind it fades once and stays faded; rolled at birth and deterministic, where the old one multiplied by a per-frame coin flip so a fast machine flickered and a slow one did not. ⚠ **AND IT COMES OUT OF THE CLOUD THE CLOUDS ARE DRAWN AT**: `topZ` was `max(3.5, cam.EH + 1.5)` against `cloudBaseZ('storm')` of 5.66, so every channel began well below the deck it was leaving — **and SHORTER THAN THE BUILDINGS**, since a thirty-storey tower is 5.9 tiles, which is why a strike over the city read as a spark between two roofs.

⚠ **AND IT WAS DRAWN UNDERNEATH EVERY GRADE THAT COULD FLATTEN IT, WHICH IS THE PART NO AMOUNT OF TUNING COULD HAVE REACHED.** Additive white cannot clip past 255 before a wash, and the channel was drawn inside the world block with `applyStormGrade`, the fog wash, the G-grey and the truck's own headlamps-off wash at **`rgba(6,8,14,0.62)` OVER THE WHOLE WORLD** all running after it. Measured out of a real cab frame, the hottest pixel in the channel reached a luminance of **102 against a night sky of 15** — a pale grey wire — and it is **255** with the draw moved after them. ⚠ **THE HEADLAMPS WASH IS RIGHT AND ITS REACH WAS WRONG**: it exists so a driver who never found the light switch cannot see FAR, and lightning is the one thing in this game you can see without headlamps. ⚠ **AND THE FLOOD WAS ALREADY DRAWN AFTER THE GRADE**, so the renderer already said the LIGHT from a strike is not subject to the storm's own murk while the channel that produced it was — one event, two treatments, and the one that lost was the one you look at. Both are one block now, after every grade and still UNDER the glass (the cab trim, the windscreen post, the dash and the water on the outside of the pane are in front of YOU, not in front of the weather), with ⚠ **the flood first and the channel over it**, or the channel gives away half its contrast to the light it is itself the source of. Banked through `bankInto`, a named closure with two callers, because a second hand-written copy of the transform is a second chance to get the shudder or the sign of the bank wrong and nothing would say so except a bolt leaning the wrong way in a turn nobody was photographing. ⚠ `cam` is handed forward in a LOCAL (`boltCam`), the `CAB_CAM` idiom, since it is built inside the world block and the bolt is now drawn after it.

⚠ **AND A STRIKE LIGHTS THE CLOUD IT LEAVES AND THE GROUND IT LANDS ON.** Three additive lights, all under the channel and all riding the same envelope. **The AREA** is a broad pool centred on the channel, and ⚠ **its radius is a WORLD REACH THROUGH THE LENS (`BOLT_LIGHT_TILES`), never a number of pixels** — a strike two streets away lights two streets, where a fixed disc lights the whole frame from any range and reads as a filter; the flat flood came down 0.5 → 0.3 when it arrived, because a full-frame fill has no position and the two together blew a night street to paper white (what the flood is genuinely for is the strikes outside the frame, which is most of them). **The GROUND** is a pool on the ground plane whose ellipse is solved from four projected points rather than assumed, and ⚠ **its flattening is CAPPED**, the one place this light is deliberately not honest: an eye 0.12 tiles off the deck looking at a pool ten tiles away sees 1.1 pixels of height against a hundred of width, which draws as a hard bar across the road, and what is being drawn is light scattering in the air ABOVE the contact. **The CLOUD** is lit from inside, ⚠ as a CLUSTER scattered off the bolt's own seed rather than a disc, because three concentric circles read as a lamp hanging in the overcast. ⚠ **NONE of the three is `glowPool`**, which is the obvious reach and would draw nothing: it gates on `ADORN_TIER` and `POWER_DUTY` — frame state left wherever the last building put it — so a bolt after a blacked-out tower would have had its own light switched off by the city's power grid, and its canvas path goes through a queue that flushed with the world pass two hundred lines earlier. `npm run gl:bolt` ([scripts/shapes/bolt.mjs](scripts/shapes/bolt.mjs)) is the gate, in the `pretest:regress` chain AND in `shapes:smoke` per the two-lists warning above: 22 claims, **mutation-tested 14 of 14**. ⚠ Its ordering claim reads **RAW source** where every other scan blanks comments first, because `blank` wipes a template literal whole and the wash it looks for is one — so every anchor there is a shape only the CODE takes, a call with its arguments and never a function name. ⚠ And two of its claims were testing themselves first: the outward rule was read as "did the branch end further out", which a branch does anyway (1.6% against 6.0% with the rule off, needing a threshold too loose to flake), and the one-envelope rule was a COUNT of `boltBright` readers, which survives the flood growing a sine of its own because there are three. The picture is `__glBolt()` in the Modelshop and the number is `__glBoltLum()`; ⚠ the first CROPS rather than rendering bigger, unlike `__glSeaShot`, because `paintWindshield` sizes its backing store from `clientWidth` so a CSS width of 2× renders at 2× and the shot is the same picture again, and ⚠ the second needs a CONTROL frame with the strike pushed past `BOLT_MAX_DIST`, because the brightest pixel in a storm frame is a lit raindrop or the weather badge about nine times out of ten.


⚠ **AND A MAX STORM COULD NOT REACH THE TOP OF ITS OWN SEA SCALE, BECAUSE THE WIND TERM ABOVE IT IS DEAD.** Reported as the waves being weak in a storm. The sea's target is `Math.max(live wind in knots, stormSeverity × SEA_FULL_KT × K)` and the design is that WIND drives the sea while the storm grade only raises a floor under it — which is right, and is not what happens. The day's wind comes from a climate profile's `monthly_wind_kph`, **every authored profile sits between 10 and 22 kph**, `windFromView` converts at 0.54, and **nothing in environment.js puts a storm term on it anywhere** — a blizzard blows exactly as hard as a clear afternoon. So the live wind tops out near **twelve knots** against a floor of thirty-one, the `Math.max` has taken the floor on every frame in every weather since it was written, and **K was doing the entire job**. At K = 0.7 the worst weather in the game asked for 31.5 of a 45-knot scale: drawn Hs 2.79 m and a tallest crest of **1.96 m**. At the full scale it is **3.99 m and 2.82 m**, and section 21's hydro launch was already tuned against that same top of the scale, so the boat and the sea now agree about what a gale is. ⚠ **It is still a floor and still a `max`** — the moment the wind becomes weather-aware this goes back to being what it was written as, and a genuinely windy clear day can still outrun it. Section 23 of `sea.mjs` reads the fraction out of the source rather than restating it and fails if one comes back, with a **ladder control** (crest 0.11 → 0.20 → 0.29 → 0.39 tiles across quarter-severity steps) because a floor that reached the top by clamping every storm to it would pass the first check and make every grade of weather produce one identical sea. Mutation-tested.

⚠ **AND THE BIGGER FINDING IS LEFT STANDING ON PURPOSE: THE GAME'S WIND IS WEATHER-BLIND.** `windKph` is read by the wind chill, the audio wind bed, NPC banter, the windsocks, the flags, the rain slant, the spindrift and the sea, and it is a **monthly climate average with no weather term on it at all**. Fixing it at the source would improve all eight at once and is the correct repair — and it reaches `apparentTemperature`, so a cold storm becomes measurably more dangerous, which is a gameplay decision rather than a rendering one. Recorded rather than gated, because a gate for it would be red on day one and this file's own rule says that is the one option that teaches nobody anything.

⚠ **AND A WAVE LEANS FORWARD NOW (`SEA_SKEW` 1.4, `SEA_ASYM` 2.0 in sea-swell.js).** Reported as the waves not breaking over, and the answer is in two halves that are worth keeping apart. **SKEWNESS is vertical** — sharp high crests over broad shallow troughs — and this sea has had it since the Stokes term shipped. **ASYMMETRY is horizontal** — the front face steeper than the back, the crest pitched forward into the direction of travel — and it is the one that reads as a wave about to break. Coastal engineering keeps the two words apart for exactly this reason, and **measured before this went in the sea's asymmetry was −0.003: perfectly symmetric front-to-back**, which is a sine with a sharpened crest. ⚠ **THE TWO TERMS ARE THE SAME HARMONIC IN QUADRATURE**, which is why the lean costs one more sine per train: skewness rides `cos 2θ` and asymmetry rides `sin 2θ`. ⚠ **AND BOTH SCALE WITH `kA`, WHICH IS NOT A STYLE CHOICE** — the second-order correction to a Stokes wave IS proportional to steepness, so a glassy sea is symmetric and sinusoidal BY ARITHMETIC rather than by a guard, and a gale leans because it is steep; written as a flat offset a dead calm would sit there permanently pitched forward. ⚠ **`SEA_SKEW` IS A GAIN ON THE NONLINEARITY AND NEVER ON THE HEIGHT**: a wave approaching breaking redistributes energy INTO its harmonics at the same total energy — sharper, not taller — so JONSWAP goes on owning Hs, and the measured pair says so, crest 2.74 → 2.86 m and Hs 3.99 → 4.06 m while the shape changes completely.

⚠ **AND BREAKING OVER IS NOT REACHABLE AT ALL, WHICH IS A WALL AND NOT A BUDGET.** A breaking wave is **multi-valued** — the lip is over the trough, so one (x,y) carries three surface heights — and this entire sea is `seaHeight(u, v, t)`, single-valued. That is not an implementation detail: **four readers ask exactly "what is the height at this world point"** — the floor shader (which recovers a world point from the screen), the mesh's vertex shader, the hull's ride, and the gate — and a true Gerstner wave is parametric, so x and z are both functions of a phase and answering that question needs an inversion. Gerstner also **FOLDS past ka ≈ 0.5**, which is the cusped-shoreline bug `coastWarp` already shipped once. So the lean is written as a vertical term: it can pitch a wave forward and can never overturn it, and the honest statement of the limit is that a height field cannot break.

⚠ **THE GAINS WERE SWEPT AGAINST THE FACE ANGLE, WHICH WAS ALREADY NEARLY AT ITS CEILING.** The Stokes limiting wave has a 120° crest, which is a **30° face** — and the sea as it shipped already peaked at **30.8°** over a long sweep, because a superposition reaches slopes no single train in it does. At skew 1.4 / asym 2.0: vertical skewness **0.178** (observed seas +0.1 to +0.3), horizontal asymmetry **0.483** (observed 0.5 to 1.5), steepest face **34.8°**. ⚠ **Asymmetry SATURATES near 0.6** — 2.0 → 3.0 buys 0.51 → 0.60 and costs three degrees of face — so there is nothing above this worth taking. ⚠ **AND THE DERIVATIVE WAS WRITTEN OUT AT SEVEN CALL SITES** (four in the JS, three in the GLSL), all the same shape with different names, which is seven chances for the lighting to describe a surface the geometry is not drawing; it is one `stokesD`/`seaStokesD` now. The existing claim that the analytic slope IS the slope catches a mismatch for free and did — mutation-tested, dropping the lean from the derivative alone fails it at 8.02e-4.

⚠ **AND FOAM SPILLS DOWN THE FRONT OF A CREST (`SEA_FOAM_LEAD`).** Whitecaps threshold on the CHOP — three plain sine trains carrying no Stokes term, so they never leaned — which put every whitecap symmetric about a crest that no longer is. ⚠ **THE AMOUNT MAY NOT CHANGE, ONLY WHERE IT LANDS**: that threshold is quantile-inverted to hit Monahan & O'Muircheartaigh's observed coverage, so biasing the TEST makes the sea foam at the wrong fraction for its wind — a physical claim this file makes and the suite checks. Shifting the sampling POSITION moves the pattern and leaves the distribution exactly alone. ⚠ **A SECOND SAMPLE, NOT A SHIFTED `wv`** — that variable also drives the surface shading, so moving it wholesale slides the lighting off the geometry it is describing. ⚠ **And it is read off the CHOP and never off the whole surface**, or the mask inherits the swell's 8.6-tile period, which is a band of whitecaps marching across the bay rather than a scatter of them. Section 24 of `sea.mjs` gates all three, mutation-tested 5 of 5.

⚠ **AND `gl:glsl` CAUGHT THE FIRST ATTEMPT AT THAT CONSTANT, WHICH IS WORTH KNOWING BEFORE THE NEXT ONE.** A `const float` declared inside `SEA_GLSL` is **invisible to any shader that includes it by `${…}`**, because that gate cuts interpolations before it scans — correctly, since the name inside one is JavaScript rather than a GLSL identifier. The file's own answer was already written down for the sidebands: **interpolate the JS value**, so there is one number and no second copy. ⚠ And two heredoc-mangled regexes went in with it: **a regex with its escapes stripped is still a VALID regex that matches nothing**, so section 23's `SEA_FULL_KT = (d+)` silently took its own fallback of 45 and passed green against a number it had never read. A gate reading its own default is testing itself.

⚠ **AND THE MODELSHOP SERVER CACHES `glbench.js` IN MEMORY.** It served **206 KB against a 305 KB file on disk** — a third of the benches missing, `window.__glSeaShot` simply absent, no error anywhere — so a bench edited mid-session can be measured in its old form with nothing to say so. Restart it (`npm run modelshop`) after editing a bench, and if a bench that exists on disk is `undefined` in the page, check the served byte count before looking for anything else.

⚠ **AND A BREAKING CREST THROWS WATER NOW (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaSpray`, 0 is the sea as it shipped).** ⚠ **SPINDRIFT ALREADY EXISTED AND IS A DIFFERENT THING** — foam ALREADY TORN OFF and dragged downwind ALONG the surface, in the water fragment shader above about Force 7. This is the water in the AIR, the burst a crest throws as it collapses, and it is the half that reads as violence rather than as texture. Measured: **0 droplets below sea state 0.5, then 14 / 28 / 70 / 154** up the scale, and 0 with the flag off. ⚠ **IT IS ARITHMETIC, NOT A PARTICLE SYSTEM** — the flocks' own rule: a puff is a pure function of its site and the wall clock, so nothing is stored, nothing is stepped, and two clients watching one sea see the same spray. A real emitter would be the one stateful thing in a surface whose whole architecture exists to avoid state, and it would buy nothing, because a droplet lives under a second and nobody can tell a derived burst from an integrated one. ⚠ **AND IT BREAKS WHERE THE WHITECAPS BREAK, OFF THE SHADER'S OWN THRESHOLD** — `seaFoamThreshold` is the quantile of the chop's distribution at Monahan's observed coverage, so spray lands exactly where the sea is already white; a second criterion, however reasonable, puts droplets in the air over unbroken water.

⚠ **AND IT MAY NOT GO THROUGH `pushLight`, WHICH IS THE `shippingOn()` TRAP A THIRD TIME.** That funnel opens `if (POWER_DUTY <= 0) return`, and `POWER_DUTY` is a property of the **last building the model pass drew** — set per tile and left wherever it landed. Sea spray asking it is spray that vanishes over open water whenever the previous tile happened to be a blacked-out tower, and silent about why; a droplet is not a building light, so it writes to the sink itself. ⚠ **And it is emitted beside the ground pass's own dressing**, with the sinks armed — a crest throwing water from anywhere earlier reaches a null sink and paints on the canvas OVER the composited city, which is `emitGroundLate`'s bug one pass along.

⚠ **THE SITE COUNT IS NOT THE DIAL AND THE FIRST TUNE GOT THAT BACKWARDS.** At 22 sites in a full gale exactly **2** came back breaking — which is 9%, and is Monahan's own Force 9 figure to within noise. The sea was not under-breaking, it was under-SAMPLED, and the honest fix is more drops per crest (a collapsing crest throws a great deal of water, not five beads) with more sites to find them. ⚠ **And a droplet is a PUFF, not a point**: at `2.2/f` a drop three tiles out is **0.73 px** and clamps to the floor, so the whole burst rendered as a few single-pixel specks — which is exactly what the first run looked like. Section 25 of `sea.mjs` gates the four silent failures, mutation-tested 4 of 4; the picture is `sprayTally()` on a painted frame, because no headless harness here reaches a sprite.

⚠ **AND 32 WAVE COMPONENTS WERE BUILT, MEASURED AND REVERTED — THE COUNT IS NOT THE LEVER.** The obvious answer to "our sea has 9 components and an FFT ocean has 65,000" is to add components, and it is wrong here. Built as a generated 13+14 sideband fan (the gate's directional-spread claim refused the first one, a golden-angle fan that put several sidebands on the peak's own heading): per-fragment transcendentals went **26 → 95** and per-vertex **22 → 91**, a 3.65× cost that this machine cannot resolve — `__glSeaCost` at 2560×1440 read 0.63 / 0.10 / 2.67 ms against a **5.59 ms spread**. And the picture barely moved, for a reason that is arithmetic rather than judgement: **the band carries only 7.4% of the swell's energy and 6.0% of the wind sea's**, so the peak keeps 96–97% and spreading that sliver over thirteen components makes each one worth **1–13 cm** against a 1.05 m peak swell — **most of them smaller than the chop** (14 cm), which is itself only a normal map past six tiles. ⚠ **The lever is the band's ENERGY SHARE, not its cardinality**, and raising it has a cost already recorded one section up: the Stokes correction goes as amplitude SQUARED, so splitting the energy flattens the crests — measured at 0.154 → 0.087 the last time the peak's share dropped, and the compensation built for it then turned out to be a fudge over a different bug. So a real spectral widening is a rebalance plus a retune of the nonlinearity, judged on screen. Adding components on their own is measurable cost for no picture.

⚠ **AND THE HYDRO IS A SEAT YOU SIT IN (`plugins/powerboat`, `client/game/js/panels/boat-view.js`).** The race boat had a hull, a flight model and a sea to ride and no way to drive it from the inside. It is the cab's shape rather than the Echelon's — a windscreen, a wheel, a lever and a text rung — and the four rules worth knowing are each a thing that was silently wrong first.

**THE INSIDE AND THE OUTSIDE ARE ONE GEOMETRY** (`client/shared/boat-house.js`). The pilothouse you look out of and the pilothouse somebody else looks at were two hand-written sets of stations, so the windows were in different places depending on which side of the glass you were on. One module owns the hull stations and the house curves now, `aircraft3d.js`'s `buildBoat` and `interior-shell.js`'s `boatProfile` both read it, and the exterior mesh is **byte-identical** to the one that shipped before the move (sha256 pinned) — which is the check to re-run if it is refactored again. ⚠ **The scale bridge is `mPerUnit = eyeM / eyeZ`** and it is stated once: tiles-per-unit cancels from both sides, so an eye height in METRES and a hull in TILE UNITS meet at exactly one expression rather than at a constant somebody fitted. ⚠ And `SHELL_PROFILES` evaluates its profiles at MODULE INIT, so a `const` used by one is in its temporal dead zone — the boat profile is a memoising getter.

**TRIM IS `1 + k·trim`, WHICH IS THE MIGRATION INVARIANT AND NOT A STYLE** — neutral trim reproduces the shipping handling EXACTLY (`Object.is`), so the tabs are provably free on every boat nobody touches them on, the same way `calibration: 100` is for chrome.

**THE TEXT RUNG IS `conn`, AND IT IS A SURFACE YOU ACT THROUGH** — `prefersTextMinigames`, never `prefersLoggedPanels`, because deleting the wheel leaves you stuck in a moving boat. Bells are words (`stop`/`slow`/`half`/`full`/`flank`) and the bearing controller's gain is `err / max(6, turnLock · dt)`: ⚠ a fixed gain HUNTED — 19 overshoots on `slow` — because the rudder's authority is a function of the bell it is steering under. `steerFor` is exported so the gate measures the controller rather than a description of it.

⚠ **AND SIX CONTROLS WERE WIRED TO NOTHING, ALL FOUND BY ASKING "is anything left?" RATHER THAN BY A TEST.** Each looked correct at the call site. `onHold`/`setAngle` are **not in the wheel's API** (`setAccent setHeld setDragging wind setEnabled getAngle getLock destroy`), so the keyboard steering silently did nothing; `dispatchAction('BOAT_BREAKUP', {…})` takes ONE OBJECT and **returns** `Unknown action: undefined` rather than throwing, so a wreck was a no-op with no log line; the weather hand-off sent `ground` where the renderer reads `wxGround` (the eight-hand-off trap `gl:snow` exists for, one seat further out); `rampV8` reads `{rpm, pedal}` and was being handed `{load, nitro, airborne}` with the PEDAL passed as the rpm; the S key was bound to an astern term `stepBoat` does not have; and `boat_sim_close` **had no sender at all**, so ESC cleared `aboard` and left the seat open for ever. ⚠ **The verb-signature one is the lesson**: handlers are `(args, raw, player, broadcast)` and I wrote `(player, args)` — and it **passed a green 10,051-check suite**, because the case only asserted the refusal and a handler reading a player object as an argument string does refuse. A check that asserts a REFUSAL cannot tell a working gate from a broken verb; it has to seat the helm and assert the bearing moved.

⚠ **AND THE HUD WHEEL WAS A SHIP'S WHEEL ON A RACE BOAT.** `wheelFaces` in interior-shell.js has drawn a butterfly with shift lights in 3-D since the pilothouse shipped, while the 2-D widget beside it was the Echelon's rim — two wheels in one boat. `art: 'f1'` is the HUD's view of the same object. ⚠ **THE SHAPE IS A STATEMENT ABOUT THE STEERING**: a ship's wheel is round because it is geared several turns lock to lock and your hands travel round it, a race boat is under a turn (`lock: 0.5`, ±90°) and your hands never leave the grips, so the top and bottom of the circle are dead weight. ⚠ **THE WAIST IS THE WHOLE SHAPE AND A FIRST CUT HAD NONE** — flat top, flat bottom, straight sides is a rounded rectangle: correct in every dimension and reading as a games controller. ⚠ **AND THE GRIPS HAVE TO BE LIGHTER THAN THE FACE**, which is the `feedback_detail_parts_need_own_palette` trap again: drawn in the same near-black carbon they measured correct and the silhouette had no handles at all. ⚠ **The shift strip is driven off `st.sim.rpm` (the crank) and never off `pedal`** — what the engine is doing, not what you asked for — and a caller with no `getRevs` gets no strip, so the truck and the yacht are provably the wheels they were. ⚠ **No strobe at the limit** (the drug-FX rule), and the first cut's full-width flash BURIED the lamps it is made of at 0.55 alpha; it breathes at 0.16 behind them. ⚠ And the centre readout **counter-rotates**, the chess set's own trick, or the one number you read at full lock is upside down.

⚠ **NONE OF THAT IS VISIBLE TO A HEADLESS GATE.** `scripts/client/boatseat-smoke.mjs` mounts the seat, paints it, checks the packet's arity against the SERVER'S OWN unpack and builds all four marina tabs — and a wheel that draws a plausible wrong shape passes every one of those. The instrument is a scratch page served from `client/` as the web root with the widget mounted three ways (centred, at lock, and the truck art as the control), which is the `reference_fsim_layout_harness` idiom pointed at one widget; both defects above were found by looking at it and neither was findable any other way.

⚠ **AND FOAM IS TORN NOW, IN TWO POPULATIONS (`RENDER_TUNE.glSeaTear`, 0 is the smooth threshold exactly as it shipped).** Reported from the air as whitecaps reading like soft glowing blobs in rows, and measured before touching anything: the foam patches had a perimeter **1.25× that of a perfectly round disc** of the same area. ⚠ **NO AMOUNT OF RETUNING THE THRESHOLD COULD HAVE FIXED IT** — `cap = chop > threshold` is a smooth threshold on a smooth field, which can only ever produce smooth-edged islands, so the shape is a property of the field being cut and not of where the cut is made. Measured after: raggedness **19.6 → 24.3** with the coverage claim intact, and a control (the same setting rendered twice) at 19.60 against 19.61.

⚠ **THE TEAR GOES INTO THE FIELD AND NOT INTO THE FINISHED MASK, WHICH IS THE WHOLE DESIGN.** The obvious fix is to erode the mask with noise, and it silently breaks the one physical claim this sea makes about foam: `seaFoamThreshold` is the **quantile of the field the shader tests**, inverted against Monahan & O'Muircheartaigh's observed whitecap fraction. Erode afterwards and the sea foams over a smaller fraction than its wind says while every gate stays green, because the gate checks the threshold rather than the pixels. Perturb the field and re-quantile THAT and the coverage is preserved by construction — measured across 10/22/34/45 kt it tracks Monahan to ~1%. ⚠ **The quantile is therefore PER TEAR GAIN** (capped at four entries, quantised to 0.02 — each is 65,536 doubles and the gain is a slider), because with the flag at 0 the shader tests the smooth chop and must get the smooth chop's quantile, or an off switch would quietly change how much of the sea is breaking.

⚠ **AND THE CORE STAYS SOLID FOR FREE**: `cap` ramps from the threshold, so deep inside a breaking crest the mask is far past 1 and a perturbation changes nothing visible, while at the rim it is near 0 and the perturbation decides. Solid core, torn edge, from one term — where noise multiplied into the finished alpha punches holes in the middle of every whitecap and reads as dither.

⚠ **THE AMPLITUDE IS SET AGAINST THE FIELD'S OWN GRADIENT, AND BOTH WAYS OF GETTING IT WRONG LOOK IDENTICAL.** A perturbation moves the foam BOUNDARY by `amplitude / |grad chop|`, and that gradient is **2.43 per tile** here — so the first cut's 0.16 shifted the edge by 0.066 tiles against a crinkle period of 0.29, a gentle wobble that measured as no change at all. ⚠ **And too FINE is as invisible as too coarse**: at 90 rad/tile the shift EXCEEDED a whole period, the edge folded through itself into sub-pixel noise, and the raggedness again did not move. It ships at amplitude **0.40** and **42 rad/tile** — a 0.15-tile crinkle displaced 0.066, a ratio of 0.44, which is the ragged-edge regime. From a screenshot all three settings look like a foam edge that is still smooth.

⚠ **AND THERE ARE TWO POPULATIONS OF FOAM, WHICH THE MASK HAD NEVER HAD.** Monahan & Lu (1990): **STAGE A** is the actively breaking crest — about a second of life, dense, bright, lying along the crest — and **STAGE B** is the patch it leaves, which lives **five to ten times as long**, spreads, dims, and at any instant covers **1.5 to 40 times the area** of the active crests. The mask had one population and one brightness, which is neither. ⚠ **AND THE RAMP WAS THE OTHER HALF OF THE BLOB**: at `×9` it crossed 0 to 1 within about a tenth of the chop's range, so nearly every foam pixel saturated and a whitecap was a flat white slab with a hard rim. It is `×3.4` now, clamped, with the upper part of the ramp as Stage A. ⚠ **They are NESTED rather than separate**, which is what keeps one coverage number meaningful — the active core sits inside the patch it is generating, so the thresholded region is still exactly Monahan's fraction and what changes is how it is shaded across its own depth. ⚠ **And Stage B is NOT white**: decaying foam is a thinning raft of bubbles with water showing through, so it is a desaturated blue-grey; painted the same white as the active crest it only makes the slab bigger.

⚠ **AND A WHITECAP IS A STREAK, WHICH THE TEAR ALONE COULD NEVER HAVE PRODUCED.** The chop is near-isotropic, so thresholding it gives round islands however torn their edges are; a wave breaks along the LENGTH of its crest. The wind sea's crests are long lines and the shader already biased foam onto them — ⚠ **and that bias was not zero-mean, so it ADDED coverage on top of the quantile**, while the note beside it claimed it was "the same number of white pixels either way", which is only true if it takes foam OFF somewhere as well as putting it ON. Centred on 0.5 it redistributes instead, and the elongation comes free. The tear's own frame is stretched along the crest by `SEA_FOAM_ALONG`; ⚠ **that stretch is invisible to the quantiler and may therefore live in the shader alone**, because rotating and scaling a stationary field changes its correlation lengths and leaves its MARGINAL DISTRIBUTION exactly where it was.

⚠ **AND THE QUANTILER HAD BEEN INVERTING A DISTRIBUTION IT HAD BADLY ESTIMATED SINCE THE DAY IT SHIPPED.** At N = 8192 there are **eight** samples above the 0.1% quantile, which is not an estimate of anything, and the lattice was one multiplier per axis off the same index — which walks a LINE through the domain rather than filling it, so the two coordinates are correlated and whole regions are never sampled. Measured against 400,000 fresh samples the sea foamed over **0.050% of itself where Monahan says 0.102%, and 0.92% where he says 1.51%** — a factor of two at the winds an ordinary day actually reaches. At N = 65,536 on an **R2 low-discrepancy lattice** it is 0.103% and 1.500%, and the suite's own figure went from **1.49 points to 0.12**. ⚠ **It hid because the claim was checked in POINTS**: a factor-of-two error at 22 kt is 0.58 of a point and sailed through a 2-point bar, so the gate now carries a RELATIVE bar as well, and mutation-testing says a 51% error is caught where the absolute bar saw nothing.

⚠ **FOUR TRAPS IN ONE FEATURE, EVERY ONE OF THEM SILENT.** ⚠ **THE AMPLITUDE WAS SPENT TWICE** — resolved in JS as `SEA_FOAM_TEAR × tune`, handed over as a uniform, and multiplied by `SEA_FOAM_TEAR` again inside the shader: 0.16 × 0.16 = **0.0256**, a foam edge that moved two centimetres, with every JS-side number still correct and the shader testing a field nothing had inverted a threshold for. The gate asks for the RETURN'S SHAPE (`return amt * seaFoamTear(q, t);`) rather than for the identifier, because the first mutation of it wrote a bare `0.16` and sailed past a check looking for `SEA_FOAM_TEAR`. ⚠ **THE FLAG'S 0 WAS NOT THE RENDERER AS IT SHIPPED** — the ramp and the two stages went in unconditionally, so turning the tear off restored the smooth threshold and left the foam dimmer and differently coloured than the thing it was meant to be reverting to; an off switch that does not switch off is worse than none, because it is what every later A/B is measured against. ⚠ **A FLAT CALM'S THRESHOLD WAS A HARDCODED 1.2** meaning "above the field's own maximum", and the tear WIDENS the field — so the ceiling stopped being a ceiling and a glass morning grew whitecaps; it is derived from the samples now. ⚠ **AND THE COVERAGE GATE WAS SAMPLING THE SMOOTH CHOP AGAINST THE TORN THRESHOLD**, a mismatched pair — the very error the design exists to avoid making in the shader — reporting 2.0 points at 55 kt while measuring its own mismatch. It checks BOTH settings against their own field now.

⚠ **AND TWO OF THE GATE'S OWN CHECKS COULD NOT PASS RATHER THAN COULD NOT FAIL**, which is harder to notice because the board is red either way: the tear-spent-once check sliced `seaFoamTorn` backwards (bounded by `seaChop`, which is declared ABOVE it) and came back empty, reading as "not found"; and its regex reached the file with its backslashes eaten, where `/return amt * seaFoamTear(q, t);/` is a **valid regex that matches nothing** — the third time this repo has been caught by a heredoc, and the reason the memory says to use the Write tool for anything carrying a regex. Both "caught" every mutant by always failing.

⚠ **AND `floor.js` STILL USES ABSOLUTE WHITECAP THRESHOLDS (`wv > 0.90`, `wv > 0.86`) AND NEVER GOT THE MONAHAN INVERSION AT ALL.** So the sea beyond the 14-tile mesh patch foams on a fitted constant while the sea inside it foams on the observed coverage law, and neither knows about the other. Recorded rather than fixed: measured, `glSwell: 0` leaves **0% bright pixels** in the frames this was diagnosed from, so every whitecap a player is actually looking at is the mesh's — but that is a property of these seats and not a guarantee, and the far water is drawing foam nobody has held to a number.

⚠ **AND HOW BIG A WHITECAP IS HAS NOTHING TO DO WITH HOW RAGGED ITS EDGE IS (`seaFoamFine`).** Reported as the foam still being blobby after the tear landed, and the two are separate axes: a patch is a LEVEL SET of the field being thresholded, so its size is inherited from that field's wavelengths and no amount of tearing the rim changes it. The foam was tested on `seaChop`, whose trains are 1.09, 1.20 and 0.60 tiles — so every whitecap was a four-to-eight-metre island however torn it was.

⚠ **AND MORE TEAR IS NOT THE ANSWER, WHICH A PATCH CENSUS SAID AND THE RAGGEDNESS METRIC COULD NOT.** Connected components of the foam mask, swept to six times the shipping tear: the patch COUNT rose **43 → 122** while the LARGEST only fell **2454 → 755 px** and the median collapsed to **6 px**. Noise shatters the small patches into speckle and leaves the big ones standing, because a big patch is a chop-scale feature and noise only nibbles its rim. ⚠ **A metric that cannot answer the question asked will answer a different one confidently** — perimeter/√area rises the whole way up that sweep, so on raggedness alone cranking the tear looks like the fix.

⚠ **THE FIELD THAT DECIDES WHERE THE SEA IS WHITE SHOULD BE SHORTER THAN THE FIELD THAT DECIDES WHAT IT LOOKS LIKE**, which is physically right rather than a trick: whitecaps are thrown by the SHORT breaking waves and not by the dominant swell. Two real trains at about **0.25 and 0.17 tiles** (1.8 and 1.2 m), added to the foam test only. Measured on an air frame at 45 kt: **43 patches of mean 224 px became 80 of mean 69**, largest 2454 → 1149, median 38 → 10 — smaller and more numerous without the speckle collapse, and the control is bit-identical. ⚠ Their frequencies come from the **dispersion relation the rest of the sea uses** (ω² = gk, with g read off the chop's own trains at 0.141 in these units) and are snapped to the 0.01 rad/s grid, or `seaClock` stops being phase-exact and every client gets a seam across its foam every ten and a half minutes.

⚠ **AND IT MUST NEVER REACH THE SURFACE.** The mesh carries 5.4 samples across the SHORTEST chop train as it is, so it cannot resolve a 0.17-tile wave — and a fragment normal describing a wave the geometry is not drawing is the one thing this sea's own rules forbid outright. `gl:sea` now walks `seaRoll`, `seaWind`, `seaSlope`, `seaChop` and `seaHeight` and fails if any of them so much as mentions it. ⚠ **And it rides the tear's switch at BOTH ends** — `step(uTear)` in the shader and the same gain in the inversion — because gating one and not the other hands the flag's 0 a threshold cut for a field it does not test, which is an off switch that quietly changes how much of the sea is breaking. Mutation-tested 3 of 3, and ⚠ one of those three claims could not PASS on the clean tree first (a 900-character window that the comment block inside `quantiles` overran), which is the second time in this feature a check has been red for its own reasons rather than the code's.

⚠ **AND THE WORST WEATHER NOW MAKES A BIG SEA, WHICH MEANT MAKING THE WAVES LONGER BEFORE MAKING THEM TALLER.** Asked whether the game gets truly large waves, and measured, it did not: at the top of the entire scale the sea was **Hs 4.06 m, crest 2.97 m, trough-to-crest 5.57 m** — WMO sea state 6, "very rough", and only just, since 6 begins at 4. A real storm sea is state 8 at 9-14 m. It is **Hs 6.52 m, crest 4.64 m, trough-to-crest 8.72 m** now: **state 7, "high"**.

⚠ **THE CEILING WAS THE FETCH AND NEVER THE WIND SCALE.** JONSWAP puts Hs on `sqrt(FETCH)`, so 50 km was worth 4 m at 45 kt and 130 km is worth 6.5. Raising `SEA_FULL_KT` instead would have needed **73 kt** for the same answer and would have moved a number **eight other systems read** — the wind chill, the audio bed, the windsocks, the flags, the rain slant, the spindrift, NPC banter and `apparentTemperature` — to fix the water. The fetch is the one number that has only ever meant "how much open sea is out there beyond the map".

⚠ **AND A WAVE CANNOT BE TALL WITHOUT BEING LONG, WHICH IS THE PART THAT DECIDED THE SHAPE OF THE CHANGE.** The limiting steepness of a gravity wave is H/L = 1/7, so the old 60 m train could carry **8.6 m** trough-to-crest and no more — and at 5.57 m it was already at **64% of that**. Raising the amplitude alone walks into a wave the arithmetic refuses to draw: the Stokes correction goes as `kA` and past `SEA_KA_LIMIT` (0.443) the second-order term overtakes the fundamental and the crest NOTCHES rather than sharpens. So the dominant train went **60 m → 101 m** and the height followed it: measured, steepness went **0.092 → 0.086**, which is **64% → 60% of the limiting wave**. Half again the sea, slightly gentler in slope.

⚠ **AND THE WIND SEA HAD TO LENGTHEN WITH IT OR IT BREAKS — the half that is easy to miss.** Amplitude is split 60/40 between the two trains and rises with Hs while the wavenumber does not, so at Hs 6.5 m on its old 22 m wavelength that train reaches **kA = 0.610 against a limit of 0.443**. 38 m now, which puts it at **0.357** — gentler than the 0.381 it used to run at, while carrying half again the height.

⚠ **THE FREQUENCY IS SNAPPED TO THE 0.01 rad/s GRID FIRST AND THE WAVENUMBER DERIVED FROM IT**, never the other way round: `seaClock` wraps at 2π/0.01 and is phase-exact only because every frequency divides it, so solving for the wavelength you want and letting ω fall where it likes puts a seam across every client's sea every ten and a half minutes. ω 0.31 → **0.24** and k 0.7272 → **0.4357** through ω² = gk, with g read off the train's own old pair.

⚠ **AND IT IS SIZED TO THE PATCH ON PURPOSE — LONGER IS NOT BETTER HERE.** `PATCH_R` is 14 tiles and the new train is **14.42**, so exactly one whole wave spans the displaced mesh. A real Hs 12 m sea has a 14 s peak period and a **306 m wavelength — 43.7 tiles, three times the patch** — and what that draws is a slab of water tilting with the flat floor beyond it, which is not a bigger wave, it is a worse picture. That is the ceiling on this renderer, and it is geometry rather than budget.

⚠ **THE HULL ROLLS LESS IN A BIGGER SEA, WHICH IS CORRECT AND WAS NOT PREDICTED.** Worst attitude in a gale went **11.1° roll / 11.0° pitch → 9.0° / 7.7°**, because a fitted plane follows the SLOPE and slope is H/L, which fell — a long swell lifts a ship where a short steep sea throws her about. She heaves **0.425 tiles (3.0 m) over six seconds** instead. No retune was needed and the effective-slope coefficient is untouched.

⚠ **AND THE HYDRO GETS AIR LESS OFTEN, WHICH IS ALSO CORRECT AND IS THE ONE THING THAT MIGHT READ AS A LOSS**: **13.5 → 8.5 launches a minute at the same 0.032-tile peak**. A 101 m swell is a gentle rise under a race boat; what throws one is short steep chop. ⚠ **`launchVs` IS DELIBERATELY NOT RETUNED** — this file already records that the threshold is not the knob, because the launch speed IS the kick, so a lower bar buys more launches at the same negligible height (swept to 0.03 it left the water 357 times a minute and never cleared 0.011 tiles). If the race is meant to fly it is the CHOP that has to grow, which is a different change.

⚠ **AND `seaSlope` CARRIES ITS OWN COPY OF THE PEAK TERMS, WHICH `gl:sea` CAUGHT IMMEDIATELY.** Moving `seaRoll` and `seaWind` left the slope generator describing the old 60 m wave — self-consistent inside each function, and a surface DISPLACED at one wavelength and LIT as though it had another. That is the exact class the per-generator claims exist for, and the "analytic slope is the slope" check went red until both moved. ⚠ Its `0.540 * cA` / `-0.405 * cA` constants are `SEA_PH`'s own wavenumber times the wind train's `pm` and must NOT move with the train — only the ku/kv in front of them.

⚠ **AND THE AUDIO FOLLOWED WITH NO SECOND EDIT, WHICH IS WHAT THE DERIVATION BOUGHT.** `sea-audio.js` takes its LFO rates from `SEA_ROLL.w`/`SEA_WIND.w` rather than from literals, so the bed now breathes at **26.2 s and 16.1 s** instead of 20.3 and 12.3 — a longer swell sounds slower on its own, and `scripts/audio/sea-smoke.mjs` re-derives the same numbers so it stayed green. Only the COMMENTS quoting the old periods had to move, and a comment quoting a derived number is the thing most likely to go stale unnoticed.

⚠ **The knock-on to live with: a low camera is under the surface 36.3% → 41.6% of the time at 45 kt, ducking up to 4.29 m.** The dunk shader and the wet glass already handle it; what changes is how often a chase seat or a free camera near the water is in it.

⚠ **AND THE HEADROOM ABOVE THAT IS MEASURED, SO NOBODY HAS TO RE-DERIVE IT.** Asked whether the sea can go HUGE, and at the 101 m wavelength that ships the answer has a hard number on it: the limiting wave at that length is **14.4 m trough-to-crest**, and the sea sits at 8.72. Swept by pretending the fetch is longer, holding everything else still:

| fetch | Hs | trough-to-crest | % of the limiting wave | steepest face | sea state |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **130 km (ships)** | **6.52 m** | 8.72 | **60%** | 35.8° | 7 high |
| 200 km | 8.17 m | 10.99 | 76% | 42.9° | 7 high |
| 300 km | 10.14 m | 13.77 | **95%** | 50.1° | 8 very high |
| 450 km | 12.66 m | 17.34 | **120% — impossible** | 57.7° | — |

⚠ **SO THE ARITHMETIC REACHES SEA STATE 8 AND THE PICTURE DOES NOT.** Hs 10 m is inside the limiting wave at 95%, and its steepest face is **50° against a Stokes limiting face of 30°** — which does not read as a bigger swell, it reads as a sawtooth. The honest ceiling on this wavelength is around **Hs 8 m** (76%, 43°), and that is one fetch number away with nothing else touched. ⚠ Past 100% the second-order term overtakes the fundamental and the crest NOTCHES, so the 450 km row is not a steep sea, it is a broken one.

⚠ **AND TRULY HUGE IS A PATCH PROBLEM, NOT A PHYSICS ONE.** Holding steepness where it is now (60% of the limit), the wavelength a sea state needs is `9.36 × Hs`:

| want | needs a wave of | in tiles | against `PATCH_R` 14 |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hs 6.5 m (high) | 101 m | 14.5 | fits, exactly one wave |
| Hs 9 m (very high) | 140 m | 20.0 | 1.4× |
| Hs 12 m (very high) | 187 m | 26.7 | 1.9× |
| Hs 16 m (phenomenal) | 249 m | 35.6 | 2.5× |

⚠ **AND THE GRID CANNOT BE TRADED DOWN TO PAY FOR IT**: the chop is 0.60 tiles and needs about five samples across it, so `PATCH_PER_TILE` cannot fall below 9 inside `glSeaChopR` — which makes a bigger patch cost AREA. 14 → 28 tiles is **64k → 254k vertices, exactly 4×**.

⚠ **WHETHER THAT 4× IS AFFORDABLE IS UNRESOLVED ON THIS MACHINE, AND THE ATTEMPT IS THE USEFUL PART.** Measured through `__glSeaShot` at 1280×720, PATCH_R 28 came back at a **77.3 ms median against 129.2 for PATCH_R 14** — the bigger patch measured FASTER, with the two spreads (67–172 and 77–172) lying on top of each other. That is this file's own tell for a reading that is scene-build noise rather than a finding. The purpose-built bench agrees from the other side: `__glSeaCost` puts the whole sea at **0.16–0.23 ms against a 2.06 ms spread**, so the layer being grown is already smaller than the instrument's own noise. ⚠ **What IS deterministic is the vertex count**, and a real answer needs a bench that times the patch build and draw alone with the dials pinned — not a whole-scene stopwatch. Until somebody writes that, "4× the vertices" is the honest statement and "4× is free" is not.

⚠ **AND A UNIFORM GRID IS THE WRONG SHAPE FOR A BIG PATCH ANYWAY**, which is the design note for whoever does it: the chop is only displaced within `glSeaChopR` (6 tiles), so at PATCH_R 28 about **95% of those 254k vertices** sit where the only thing being displaced is a 101 m wave that needs roughly one sample per tile. A graded or ringed grid — dense at the eye, coarse at the rim — buys the radius at close to today's vertex count, and it is the standard answer for exactly this reason.

⚠ **AND THAT GRADED GRID WAS THEN BUILT, WHICH TOOK THE SEA TO STATE 8 (`PATCH_R` 14 → **28**, `PATCH_FINE_R` 6, `PATCH_RIM_STEP` 1.4).** Hs **12.0 m**, crest 8.62 m, trough-to-crest **16.1 m** — WMO sea state 8, "very high". ⚠ **THE FOUR PARAGRAPHS ABOVE ARE SUPERSEDED IN THEIR NUMBERS AND RIGHT IN THEIR REASONING**: both tables say "against `PATCH_R` 14" and the ceiling note says the honest limit is around Hs 8 m *on the 101 m wavelength*, which it was. The order they set out held exactly — **the patch has to grow before the wave can lengthen, and the wave has to lengthen before it can get tall** — and every step was forced by the one before it.

⚠ **A THIRD OF THE TRIANGLES AT TWICE THE RADIUS, WHICH IS THE WHOLE REASON IT WAS AFFORDABLE.** A uniform grid at 28 tiles is 504 divisions a side — **254,000 cells, 4× what the 14-tile one cost** — and it spends nearly all of them where nothing fine is drawn. Graded, 28 tiles costs **19,348 cells against the 63,504 the old uniform 14-tile grid was already paying**, measured live through `window.__seaPatch`. ⚠ **The division counts are DERIVED from the constraints rather than picked**: the fine zone owes `PATCH_PER_TILE` because of the chop and the rim owes `PATCH_RIM_STEP` because of the wind sea, so a grid sized by hand goes quietly wrong the moment either moves. ⚠ **And the grading is SEPARABLE**, which makes the fine zone a square rather than a disc — it wastes a little on the diagonals (the fine square reaches 8.5 tiles where it owes 6) and buys the one thing a radial grid cannot give without stitching: **no T-junctions anywhere**. ⚠ **`PATCH_FINE_R` may never fall below `glSeaChopR`**, or a 0.6-tile wave is displaced in a cell that cannot resolve it — which does not read as rougher water, it reads as torn geometry, and silently, because the lighting goes on describing a smooth wave over the top of it.

⚠ **AND THE KNEE IS A 12× SPACING JUMP THAT DOES NOT SHOW** — 0.111 tiles inside against 1.38 at the rim — which was worth checking rather than assuming. It is invisible because the chop taper has already gone to zero at 6 tiles, so the only thing crossing the boundary is a 179 m wave that a 1.38-tile cell carries at 18 samples.

⚠ **THE TRAINS BOTH LENGTHENED AND THE INDIVIDUAL STEEPNESSES WENT DOWN.** Roll **101 → 179 m** (k 0.4357 → 0.2451, ω 0.24 → 0.18, period 26.2 → 34.9 s) and the wind sea **38 → 69 m** (k 1.1462 → 0.6362, ω 0.39 → 0.29), with the fetch 130 → **440 km** carrying the height. `kA` is **0.113 roll / 0.239 wind** against the 0.443 limit — the wind sea no steeper than it ran at 4 m — and trough-to-crest is **63% of the limiting wave** against 60% before, while Hs nearly doubled. ⚠ **The SUPERPOSITION's worst face went the other way, 35.8° → 41.6°**, and that is arithmetic rather than a train being over-driven: a sum of trains reaches slopes no single one in it does, and there is simply more sea. It is still under the 43° the ceiling note above called the honest limit.

⚠ **AND FROM A BOAT IT READS AS A LONG OCEAN SWELL RATHER THAN AS A WALL OF WATER, WHICH IS CORRECT AND IS WORTH KNOWING BEFORE SOMEBODY "FIXES" IT.** From the air the trains are unmistakable and the whitecap rows run for tiles; from the pilothouse a 179 m wave carrying 8.6 m of crest subtends very little, because what a low eye reads is SLOPE and slope is H/L. That is what a real state-8 sea looks like from a small boat in deep water — the drama in the reference photographs is shoaling water, which is a different phenomenon. **If the water is meant to look violent at eye level the lever is steepness, not height**, and the one term left with any room in it is the chop.

⚠ **SHE ROLLS LESS AT EVERY STEP, AND THE FIGURE IS NOW SMALL ENOUGH TO NOTICE.** Worst attitude in a gale has gone **11.1° → 9.0° → 6.3°** across the three seas, for the reason recorded above: a fitted plane follows the slope. She takes up **96%** of the water's movement now against 91% at the short sea, which is what following a long swell means. Nothing about the hull was retuned, and the effective-slope coefficient is untouched.

⚠ **AND THE INERTIA GATE WENT RED ON A FILTER THAT WAS WORKING, WHICH IS THE BEST FINDING IN THE BATCH.** It cross-correlated 240 samples at 50 ms — **a 12 second window against a 34.9 second swell**, a third of one cycle, over which the deck height is very nearly monotonic. Cross-correlating a ramp with a shifted copy of itself peaks at zero however real the lag is, so it reported "she is not trailing the water at all" about a filter trailing it exactly as it always had. Its window and its "that is not inertia" bar are both derived from `SEA_ROLL.w` now — three whole waves of signal, searched out to a quarter of one — and it reads **1.04 s**. ⚠ **A window shorter than the period of the thing it measures is not a loose test, it is a test of something else**, and it is the same family as the `__glLights` bench that answered a question nobody had asked.

⚠ **THE HYDRO GETS AIR LESS OFTEN AT EVERY STEP, AND THE REASON UNDERNEATH IT IS THE REAL FINDING: `SEA_AMP` IS A MODULE CONSTANT.** Launches went **13.5 → 8.5 → 5.0 a minute** at an unchanged 0.03-tile peak, and `launchVs` is deliberately still untouched because this file already records that the threshold is not the knob. But the sea now grows **entirely in its two long trains** while the 4–8 m chop stays the size it is on a flat calm — so a storm makes the water bigger and not rougher, which is both why the race boat flies less and why the view from the pilothouse is calm. **Scaling the chop with sea state is the next change**, and it is bigger than a tune: the whitecap threshold is quantiled against the chop's own distribution, so it would have to be re-inverted per sea state rather than once.

⚠ **AND A LOW CAMERA IS UNDER THE WATER A LOT OF THE TIME NOW** — the crest reaches **1.23 tiles** against a camera floored at 0.05 — so a chase seat or a free camera near the surface spends much of a gale submerged. The dunk shader, Snell's window and the wet glass all handle it and it looks right; what it means in practice is that the SEAT matters more than it did, and the pilothouse (eye 1.35 m) is the one that reads as a boat in a storm rather than as a submarine.

⚠ **AND THE DECAL TEXTURE CACHE WAS SMALLER THAN THE CITY, WHICH COST ABOUT AN EIGHTH OF THE FRAME EVERYWHERE THE CITY IS DENSE.** `gl/decals.js` held `MAX_TEX = 192`, sized when a decal meant a SIGN — a few dozen baked name boards. It has not meant that for a long time: `emitDecoFill` files every flat coloured quad in the city there, keyed on its own CSS string, and the derived kit put trim, plinths, crown courses, window bands, pilaster ranks and recessed bays on most of the registry. A cab frame in Halcyon Fields wants **426 distinct decal textures**. ⚠ **THE TELL IS THAT `batches` AND `textures` COME BACK EQUAL** — at every district over the cap — which says the cache held exactly this frame's set and nothing else, because `evict` had deleted everything the previous frame left in it: the worst state a cache can be in, deleting ~230 textures a frame and re-creating each with a `texImage2D` for artwork that had not changed. ⚠ **AND IT GROWS WITH HOW MUCH TRIM THE CITY GAINS**, so every pass that has made the buildings better since has made this worse, silently. At 1024 the steady-state mint rate goes **91 → 26 a frame at Halcyon and 69/46/3 → 0 at civic, residential and nightlife**, for well under a megabyte: these are `solidTex` at **8×8** and `rampTex` at **4×32**, 256 and 512 bytes, against the 156 MB a single GL scene already holds. Measured on the clock, four interleaved A/Bs saved **1.0 / 3.0 / 2.3 / 9.3 ms** off a ~29 ms frame — all four the same direction, and ⚠ **the variance is the better half of the reading**: the new path measured 27.6 / 27.4 / 25.7 / 25.4 against 28.6 / 30.4 / 28.0 / 34.7, which is what a frame doing a variable amount of texture churn looks like beside one doing none. ⚠ **`billboards.js` WAS CHECKED AND IS FINE** — `bbTex` is a flat 241 under its own 256 cap at every district — so this is one layer's cap and not a pattern to go and apply.

⚠ **AND THE DISTRICT BENCH THAT FOUND IT IS `__glWhere` / `__glWheres` ([tools/modelshop/districtcost.js](tools/modelshop/districtcost.js)), WHICH IS THE FIRST COST BENCH HERE POINTED AT THE REAL CITY.** Every other one builds a synthetic city, which is right for a number that has to mean the same thing across days and useless for *why is it choppy HERE*. It profiles the baked world at a district landmark from a cab or an aeroplane. ⚠ **ITS HEADLINE FINDING IS THAT THE GLASSHOUSE IS NOT SPECIAL**: the dense districts are **Halcyon 48 · residential 50 · civic 44 · Marrow 40 ms** at a cab seat against **the Glasshouse 27 · nightlife 27 · industrial 20 · docks 11**, so the chop is any dense block and not a quarter. ⚠ **AND FACE COUNT RUNS THE OTHER WAY** — nightlife draws the most faces in the city (45k) and is nearly the cheapest, Halcyon draws 34k and is the dearest — while **decal count tracks the clock almost exactly**, which is the whole reason the cache was looked at at all.

⚠ **THREE WAYS THAT BENCH LIED BEFORE IT TOLD THE TRUTH, AND EVERY ONE OF THEM PRODUCED A CONFIDENT TABLE.** **The sweep order decided the answer**: six runs of one unchanged config went 55.3 → 46.3 → 40.0 → 41.6 → 36.5 → 37.7 ms, monotonic, because the engine is still compiling the renderer and the shape, kit and atlas caches are still filling — so the first district in the list is charged for all of it, and the first table it ever printed named Halcyon at **96 ms against the docks at 11** purely because Halcyon is first in `PLACES`. A per-place `warm` does not cover it; what is cold is the PROCESS, so the sweep pre-passes the whole list and keeps the MINIMUM over reps. **A fresh canvas per call is a fresh WebGL2 context per call**, the page caps at 16 and force-loses the oldest past that, which sent the same config 40.2 → 38.4 → 45.1 → 56.1 → 59.7 — the exact mirror of the warm-up drift and just as convincing. **And a hidden Browser pane cannot be timed at all**: the same config reads 43 ms visible and degrades to 124 hidden. ⚠ **THE CLOCK IS DELIBERATELY NOT FROZEN HERE**, which is the opposite of every pixel bench in this repo — `perfBegin` keeps time with `performance.now()`, so a stubbed clock makes every phase read exactly **0.000 ms** while every count stays correct, which reads as a profiler that was never wired up. That cost the first run.

⚠ **AND AN ARM COUNT ANSWERED THE WRONG QUESTION, SO AN ARM'S TIME IS ATTRIBUTED TO ITS MODEL NOW** (`setWindshieldProfiler(true, { arms: true })`, off by default because it is two `performance.now()` calls per building per frame). A dense district and a cheap one run the same seventy-odd arms; the expensive district is the one whose PARTICULAR buildings are expensive. The worst model in the city is **`shell_tower` at 0.52 ms a draw** — Halcyon's half-built glass tower, 7.9 of them in view, **4.1 ms a frame on its own** — against `authored` at 0.07. ⚠ **It is only meaningful on the GL path**, where the mass is suppressed and what is left in an arm IS the adornment collection. ⚠ **And only about a third of `world:arms` is inside `drawTypeModel` at all**, the rest being the marks, the LOD and the per-item work in the loop, so the table is a starting point and not a budget.

⚠ **AND `webBays` SHEDS A TRUSS'S WEB WITH DISTANCE (`RENDER_TUNE.webLod`, 0 is every lattice at its authored bay count exactly as it shipped) — WHICH REDUCES REAL WORK AND DOES NOT MOVE THE CLOCK.** A truss reads as a truss because of its bracing and the bracing is the whole of what it costs: `latticeTower` is 3 legs and a top belt against **36 braced segments**, and the count was a literal, so a lattice a third of a mile away paid for all 36 exactly as one at arm's length did. ⚠ **The measure is APPARENT SIZE IN PIXELS and not distance in tiles** — a quay crane, a tower crane and a radio mast differ by an order of magnitude in height, so a tile threshold that keeps a gantry's web has long since deleted a mast's, and the question is whether the bracing lands on more than one pixel. ⚠ **A jib is measured along ITSELF**: it is very nearly horizontal, so its height would shed every bay on the tallest crane in the city. ⚠ **It only ever sheds the WEB** — the chords, legs and top belt are drawn outside any bay loop, so the silhouette at any range is what it always was — which is why it is not an `ADORN_TIER` rung: a tier is a statement about a whole building's budget and this is one about one part's resolution. Measured, strokes fall **1563 → 1316 at Halcyon, 953 → 837 at Marrow, 592 → 503 at nightlife** (12–16%) and the picture moves **0.20% against a 0.126% control**, and **the frame time does not move at all** — eight A/Bs came back −5.6 to +6.6 ms, mean −0.4. ⚠ **That is the finding, not a failure to measure**: ~1,500 strokes is ONE draw call, so strokes were never the bottleneck, and it is recorded here so nobody spends a second afternoon on the stroke layer. It ships because it is a genuine reduction with an off switch and no visible cost, not because it made anything faster.


⚠ **AND A MASSIF IS NO LONGER A TABLE (`RENDER_TUNE.cliffRough`, `cliffWarp`, and `plateau` in `BIOME_GROUND`).** Reported as the cliffs inside the basin not looking natural, and measured over all 2,214 cliff and plateau tiles it was three separate things, each of which reads as one. ⚠ **THE TWO QUESTIONS A HEIGHT ANSWERS WERE FIGHTING OVER ONE WEIGHTED MEAN.** `cliffHeightAt` answers WHICH tableland this is (low frequency, wanting a big swing) and HOW BROKEN its rim is (high frequency, wanting a real amplitude), and written as one mean over four octaves those trade against each other by arithmetic: reweighting toward the fine end doubled the tile-to-tile step and took the massif-scale swing from 49% of the median to 39%. Split — `cliffRelief` stretching the LEVEL, `cliffRough` added on top — both go up at once, to 52% and 2.83× the step. ⚠ **AND THE ROUGHNESS IS SPENT INTO THE HEADROOM, NEVER CLAMPED AGAINST THE RAIL**: added and clipped, a roughness worth looking at pinned 2.4% of the world's cliff tiles to an identical height, which is a flat rim again manufactured by the term that exists to break one — the same trap the relief curve beside it was already written around, walked into by the next thing added. ⚠ **THE PLAN OUTLINE'S LIMIT WAS THE BOUND AND NOT A MEASUREMENT**: the warp was held under half a tile because two corners a tile apart can close by W, and they are sampled from ONE noise a third of a cycle apart, so they are strongly correlated and mostly move together — the tightest quad in the lattice keeps 0.686 of its area at 0.50 and 0.502 at 0.85, so the outline is nearly twice as ragged with the fold still a long way off. `cliffLatticeSmoke` reports that margin now rather than only its verdict, because a pass said nothing to anybody raising the number. ⚠ **AND THE CAPROCK WAS DARKER THAN THE PLAIN IT STANDS ON**, which is the opposite of what its own palette comment claims twice over (*"the caprock top takes the sun"*, *"both sit deliberately in the redrock family"*): `plateau` sat at a luminance of 55 against redrock's 99, so a cliff tile's cap resolved to 73 and every massif in the world read as a dark stain on a bright plain — top, face and apron all at once — rather than as ground standing up out of it. A mesa top and the flat beside it are the same rock under the same sun; what is dark on a butte is its FACE. The cap is now 96 against the plain's 99 and the faces stay at 61 and 79, so the drop to the face is what carries the landform. ⚠ **AND `cliffrelief.mjs` WAS RUN BY NOTHING** — an npm name in neither list and in no chain, the `gl:neighbour` case again — so it is in `pretest:regress` AND `shapes:smoke` now (two lists, per the warning above) plus `npm run gl:cliff`. It makes four claims rather than three, because each knob is measured against its OWN control (relief 1 is a power of 1, rough 0 removes the term) and the fourth is that the roughness stays out of the level's business, which is the entire reason the two were split. Mutation-tested 5 of 5. ⚠ **"1 IS EXACTLY WHAT SHIPPED" WENT STALE IN THE SAME COMMIT THAT MADE THE SPLIT** and is corrected in place: relief 1 is still the identity and is no longer the massif that shipped, which is the kind of claim that goes on reading true long after it stops being.
